From cubic norm pairs to 𝐆 𝟐 \mathbf{G_{2}} - and 𝐅 𝟒 \mathbf{F_{4}} -graded groups and Lie algebras
Tom De Medts, Torben Wiedemann

Abstract
. We construct Lie algebras arising from cubic norm pairs over arbitrary commutative base rings. Such Lie algebras admit a grading by a root system of type G 2 G_{2} , and when the cubic norm pair is a cubic Jordan matrix algebra, the G 2 G_{2} -grading can be further refined to an F 4 F_{4} -grading. We then use these Lie algebras and their gradings to construct corresponding root graded groups. Along the way, we produce many results providing detailed information about the structure of these Lie algebras and groups.



Introduction

The study of Lie algebras graded by root systems goes back to the influential work of Stephen Berman and Robert V. Moody from 1992
[
6
]
.
More precisely, Berman and Moody classified all Lie algebras over a base field
k
of characteristic
0
graded by simply-laced finite root systems of rank
\geq 2
, up to central isogeny.
Over the following decade, this result was extended to the types
A_{1}
,
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
,
BC_{r}
,
F_{4}
and
G_{2}
for
n\geq 2
,
r\geq 1
and strengthened to a classification up to isomorphism by the combined work of Bruce Allison, Georgia Benkart, Yun Gao, Oleg Smirnov and Efim Zel’manov
[
4
,
1
,
2
,
3
]
. A different, uniform approach to the classification for
3
-graded root systems (an assumption which excludes exactly the types
E_{8}
,
F_{4}
and
G_{2}
) which avoids case-by-case considerations was given by Erhard Neher
[
22
]
.

A different approach for
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebras, now over arbitrary fields
k
(including fields of characteristic
2
and
3
) was taken in
[
10
]
, based on the theory of extremal elements in Lie algebras and intersecting two different
5
-gradings on the Lie algebra.

The goals of the current paper are the following.

(1)

Construct and investigate
G_{2}
- and
F_{4}
-graded Lie algebras over arbitrary commutative base rings
k
.

(2)

Develop the theory of
cubic norm pairs
over arbitrary base rings
k
, required for (1).

(3)

Construct and investigate
G_{2}
- and
F_{4}
-graded groups.

Our description of the Lie algebras is similar as in
[
10
]
in the sense that we coordinatize each “piece” of the grading separately and explicitly describe the Lie bracket in terms of this coordinatization.

For
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebras, we encounter the same sort of algebraic structures as the ones that play a key role in
[
10
]
, namely
cubic norm pairs
, a twin version of the more common cubic norm structures.
When
k
is a field, such a cubic norm pair either has trivial norm or it arises from a genuine cubic norm structure, but this dichotomy fails to hold over base rings. At the same time, the occurrence of such a twin structure is no surprise and is simply the correct analogue of a cubic norm structure, very much like Jordan pairs form a twin version of Jordan algebras.
Nevertheless, not much has been written in the existing literature about these cubic norm pairs, and our only traces back are John Faulkner’s paper
[
15
]
and Michiel Smet’s recent preprint
[
25
]
.
We use the opportunity to spell out the basic theory of cubic norm pairs. We follow a similar setup as in the recent excellent monograph
[
17
]
, which develops the theory of cubic norm
structures
over arbitrary commutative rings.
The results from
[
17
]
will play a crucial role throughout the whole paper.

A purely combinatorial construction, which relies on a certain Tits index and an associated surjection
F_{4}\to G_{2}\cup\{0\}
of root systems, makes it possible to regard any
F_{4}
-graded Lie algebra as a
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra. For
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebras defined by a specific kind of cubic norm pairs, namely
cubic Jordan matrix algebras
, we can reverse this procedure: that is, we refine the
G_{2}
-grading to obtain an
F_{4}
-graded Lie algebra.
Again, we will be able to give a precise description of each “piece” of the grading.
This turns out to be rather delicate for the “middle piece” of the original
G_{2}
-grading.

In the next step, we pass from root graded Lie algebras to root graded groups. These are groups equipped with a family of subgroups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
for a finite root system
\Phi
such that certain
commutator relations

[U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}]\subseteq U_{\mathord{]\alpha,\beta[}}
for non-proportional
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
are satisfied and such that so-called
Weyl elements
, whose conjugation action permutes the root groups, exist for all roots.
The terminology of groups graded by root systems appears for the first time in
[
24
]
, where Zhiyong Shi proves coordinatization results for root graded groups of simply-laced type with rank at least 3. These results are similar in spirit to the recognition theorems for root graded Lie algebras. Partial progress towards a comparable result for type
C_{n}
with
n\geq 3
was made by Zezhou Zhang in
[
33
]
. Complete coordinatization results for root graded groups of the remaining types
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
,
F_{4}
and
BC_{n}
for
n\geq 3
were proven in
[
31
]
, and they were extended to the types
H_{3}
and
H_{4}
in
[
5
]
(with a slightly different axiomatic setup to account for the non-crystallographicness of these root systems). We will follow the terminology introduced in
[
31
]
for root graded groups (of crystallographic types), and point to its preface for further historical details.

It should also be noted that Jacques Tits’ notion of RGD-systems, introduced in
[
30
]
, is a special case of root graded groups. For these objects, a geometrical interpretation in terms of thick spherical Moufang buildings is available. In particular, Tits’ classification of thick spherical Moufang buildings of rank at least 3
[
29
]
provides a classification of RGD-systems of rank at least 3. This classification was extended to the rank 2 case—the so-called Moufang polygons—by Jacques Tits and Richard Weiss
[
28
]
.

The main structure theorem for
F_{4}
-graded groups is
[
31
, 10.7.8]
: Every
F_{4}
-graded group is parametrized by what is called in
[
17
]
a
multiplicative conic alternative algebra

C
over a commutative ring
k
, which means that the structure of the group is largely determined by
C
and
k
(though not up to isomorphism). Multiplicative conic alternative algebras should be thought of as a suitable generalization of composition algebras—such as octonions or quaternions—over rings, and they are precisely the kind of algebras over which the aforementioned cubic Jordan matrix algebras are defined. Our main goal in the setting of root graded groups—which was, in fact, our original motivation to undertake this project—is to prove a converse existence result: For every multiplicative conic alternative algebra
C
over any commutative ring
k
, there actually exists an
F_{4}
-graded group which is coordinatized by
C
. Along we way, we will also show that there exists a
G_{2}
-graded group for every cubic norm
structure
(while the group defined by a cubic norm
pair
may lack Weyl elements for the short roots). We remark that a similar existence question is answered positively in
[
34
]
, where Zezhou Zhang constructs a
C_{3}
-graded group that is coordinatized by an arbitrary alternative ring with a nuclear involution.

We construct the desired
G_{2}
-graded group as a subgroup of the automorphism group of our
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra. In principle, this amounts to “exponentiating” the appropriate adjoint maps in the Lie algebra, but since we are dealing with arbitrary commutative base rings (where
2
and
3
are not necessarily invertible), this is not straightforward at all, and simply defining the exponential maps along with the required verifications will occupy us for some time.
This can be compared with the definition of exponential maps over arbitrary base
fields
introduced in
[
10
, Theorem A]
, but we cannot simply extend the ideas of
loc. cit.
to commutative base rings.
One new technical tool that we use is the explicit formulation of what we call “higher Leibniz rules”.

The exponential maps in the
F_{4}
-graded group may be defined by specializing the exponential maps in the
G_{2}
-grading, but only for those roots that do not
“map to zero under the surjection
F_{4}\to G_{2}\cup\{0\}
”
. The exponential maps defined in this way allow us to define automorphisms of the Lie algebra that will later be proven to be Weyl elements, and the exponential maps for the
“problematic”
roots in
F_{4}
may be defined as conjugates of other exponential maps by a suitable choice of these automorphisms. The Weyl elements in the
G_{2}
-grading can be defined in a more straightforward way as certain
reflections in the Lie algebra
, which are given by explicit formulas inspired by
[
10
]
.

Once we have the exponential maps at our disposal, we can define root groups as
U_{\alpha}\coloneq\exp_{\alpha}(L_{\alpha})
. It then remains to show that these satisfy the desired commutator relations and that our candidates for the Weyl elements permute the root groups, which is a straightforward but long computation. For the
F_{4}
-grading in particular, we have to show that each Weyl element permutes the
48
root groups in the desired way, for which we use computer assistance.

Outline of the paper.

We start by recalling the required background on root graded Lie algebras and root graded groups in
Section

1
.
In
Section

2
, we develop the theory of cubic norm pairs, following a similar approach as in
[
17
]
. In the process, we collect many identities that we will need later.

We start the construction of the
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra from a cubic norm pair in
Section

3
by first producing the “classical” Tits–Kantor–Koecher Lie algebra of the cubic norm pair. In
Section

4
, we make this TKK Lie algebra into a larger,
G_{2}
-graded, Lie algebra. This involves an important subtlety where we first have to modify the
0
-graded part of the TKK construction.
It requires some computational effort to explicitly write down the Lie bracket and verify that it satisfies the Jacobi identity, but this pays off, since we will later have to refine the construction to produce an
F_{4}
-graded Lie algebra.

We take an intermezzo in
Sections

5
and
6
.
First, in
Section

5
, we show that surjective homotopies between cubic norm pairs give rise to surjective homomorphism between the corresponding Lie algebras, and we introduce reflections
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
and
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
of the Lie algebra.
Then, in
Section

6
, we investigate the ideal structure of the Lie algebra. The main results up to this point can be summarized as follows:

Theorem A

(
4.30
,
5.15
,
6.25
)
.

Let
(J,J^{\prime})
be a cubic norm pair over a commutative ring
k
. Then there exists a
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra
L=L(J,J^{\prime})
whose root spaces
L_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in G_{2}
are isomorphic to
k
,
J
or
J^{\prime}
. Further, the construction of
L
is functorial in
(J,J^{\prime})
(with respect to surjective homotopies), and
L
is simple if and only if
k
is a field and the trace of
(J,J^{\prime})
is non-degenerate.

We are then ready to pass from Lie algebras to groups.
In
Section

7
, we introduce the notion of
\alpha
-parametrizations and
\alpha
-exponential automorphisms (where
\alpha
is a root).
We then proceed to explicitly define such maps for the
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebras arising from any cubic norm pair.
The precise verification that these maps define automorphisms of the Lie algebra is rather computational, and we have decided to extract it from this section into a separate Appendix
A
.
In addition, some verifications involving Weyl elements have been moved into a second Appendix
B
.
As a final result of
Section

7
, we obtain:

Theorem B

(
7.24
)
.

Let
J
be a cubic norm structure over a commutative ring
k
and let
L\coloneq L(J,J)
be as in
A
. Then
\Aut(L)
contains a
G_{2}
-graded group
G(J)
with root groups
U_{\alpha}\cong L_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
.

We then move on to
F_{4}
-gradings. We begin by laying out the combinatorics of the Tits index that relates
F_{4}
-gradings to
G_{2}
-gradings in
Section

8
.
In a short
Section

9
, we collect the required background on cubic Jordan matrix algebras, following
[
17
]
, expressed in our setting of cubic norm pairs (rather than cubic norm structures).
Next, in
Section

10
, we show that for this specific class of cubic norm pairs, the
G_{2}
-grading can be refined to an
F_{4}
-grading, which we obtain by introducing three new
5
-gradings that we then intersect.
Finally, in
Section

11
, we show that also the
G_{2}
-grading in the automorphism group of the Lie algebra can be refined to an
F_{4}
-grading, leading to our final result.

Theorem C

(
10.23
,
11.15
)
.

Let
C
be a multiplicative conic alternative algebra (e.g., a composition algebra) over a commutative ring
k
, let
\Gamma=\operatorname{diag}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\in\Mat_{3}(k)
be invertible and let
J=\Her(C,\Gamma)
be the associated cubic Jordan matrix algebra.
Let
L\coloneq L(J,J)
be as in
A
and
G\coloneq G(J)
be as in
B
.

Then
L
is
F_{4}
-graded with root spaces
L_{\alpha}\cong k
for all long
\alpha\in F_{4}
and
L_{\alpha}\cong C
for all short
\alpha\in F_{4}
. Further,
G
is an
F_{4}
-graded group with root groups
U_{\alpha}\cong L_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in F_{4}
.

Acknowledgments.

We are grateful to Holger Petersson for pointing out the reference
[
15
]
to us and to Bernhard Mühlherr for several insightful discussions about Tits indices. The work of the second author was partially supported by a DAAD postdoctoral research scholarship and by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation), Project-ID 286237555, TRR 195.

1.
Root graded Lie algebras and groups

Convention 1.1
.

Throughout this paper, we will use the terminology of
[
18
]
for root systems. In other words, all root systems are finite, reduced and crystallographic. Further, for any root system
\Phi
, we put
\Phi^{0}\coloneq\Phi\cup\{0\}
.

1.1.
Root gradings

We first introduce the central objects that we intend to construct in this paper: Root gradings of Lie algebras and groups.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring and let
L
be a Lie algebra over
k
. For an abelian group
(A,+)
, an
A
-grading of
L
is a family
(L_{a})_{a\in A}
of
k
-submodules of
L
such that

L=\bigoplus_{a\in A}L_{a}

and
[L_{a},L_{b}]\leq L_{a+b}
for all
a,b\in A
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a
(2n+1)
-grading of
L
is a family
(L_{i})_{-n\leq i\leq n}
such that
(L_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
L
where
L_{i}\coloneq 0
for
\lvert i\rvert>n
. For a root system
\Phi
, a
\Phi
-grading of
L
is a family
(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi^{0}}
such that
(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\langle\Phi\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}}
is a
\langle\Phi\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading where
L_{\alpha}\coloneq 0
for all
\alpha\in\langle\Phi\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\Phi^{0}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Our notion of root graded Lie algebras is more general than the one in
[
6
]
. A
\Phi
-graded Lie algebra
L=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{0}}L_{\alpha}
in our sense is
\Phi
-graded in the sense of
[
6
, 1.1]
if it contains a copy
\bar{L}
of the split Lie algebra of type
\Phi
such that the root spaces
(\bar{L})_{\alpha\in\Phi^{0}}
of
\bar{L}
with respect to some split Cartan subalgebra satisfy
\bar{L}_{\alpha}\subseteq L_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{0}
. (Actually, root graded Lie algebras in the sense of
[
6
]
are usually only considered over base fields and not rings, but since the split Chevalley Lie algebras are defined over
\mathbb{Z}
(see
[
18
, Section 25]
), the notion makes sense over rings as well.) The Lie algebras that we construct in this paper are often root graded in this stronger sense, and they are
“close to being so”
in some other cases. We will discuss this in more detail in
Remark

10.34
.

Convention 1.4
.

For
x,y
in an arbitrary group
G
, we define their commutator to be
[x,y]\coloneq x^{-1}y^{-1}xy
.

Definition 1.5

(
[
31
, 2.5.2]
)
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system and let
G
be a group. A
\Phi
-grading of
G
is a family
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
of subgroups of
G
with the following properties, where we write
U_{A}\coloneq\langle U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in A\rangle
for any subset
A
of
\Phi
.

(1)

G
is generated by
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
and
U_{\alpha}\neq 1
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
.

(2)

For all non-proportional
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
, we have
[U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}]\subseteq U_{\mathord{]\alpha,\beta[}}
where
\mathord{]\alpha,\beta[}\coloneq\Phi\cap\{i\alpha+j\beta\mid i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\}
.

(3)

For all
\alpha\in\Phi
, the set of so-called
\alpha
-Weyl elements

M_{\alpha}\coloneq\{w\in U_{-\alpha}U_{\alpha}U_{-\alpha}\mid U_{\beta}^{w}=U_{\beta^{\sigma(\alpha)}}\text{ for all }\beta\in\Phi\}

is not empty. Here
\sigma_{\alpha}\colon\Phi\to\Phi
denotes the reflection along
\alpha^{\perp}
, which we let act on
\Phi
from the right-hand side.

(4)

If
\Pi
is a positive system in
\Phi
, then
U_{-\alpha}\cap U_{\Pi}=1
for all
\alpha\in\Pi
.

Remark 1.6
.

What we call a
\Phi
-grading here is actually called a
crystallographic
\Phi
-grading
in
[
31
]
. However, in the context of
\Phi
-graded groups arising from
\Phi
-graded Lie algebras, only crystallographic
\Phi
-graded groups are of interest, and hence we will always drop the word
“crystallographic”
in this paper.

Remark 1.7
.

It is a non-trivial but elementary observation that root groups in
A_{2}
-graded groups are necessarily abelian (see
[
31
, 5.4.9]
). Since
G_{2}
and
F_{4}
have the property that every root is contained in a subsystem of type
A_{2}
, it follows that all root groups in root graded groups of these types are abelian as well.

Remark 1.8

(
[
31
, 2.2.6]
)
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system, let
G
be a group with a family of subgroups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
and let
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
. Then
M_{\alpha}=M_{\alpha}^{-1}=M_{-\alpha}
and
M_{\alpha}^{w_{\beta}}=M_{\alpha^{\sigma(\beta)}}
for all
w_{\beta}\in M_{\beta}
. It follows from the second property that if
M_{\delta}\neq\emptyset
for all
\delta
in a system of simple roots of
\Phi
, then
M_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
for all
\alpha\in\Phi
.

1.2.
F_{4}
-graded groups

We now turn to the special case of
F_{4}
-graded groups. One of the main results of
[
31
]
is that every such group is
“coordinatized”
by what is called a multiplicative conic alternative algebra (
Theorem

1.15
). One of our main results in this paper is that for every multiplicative conic alternative algebra, there exists an
F_{4}
-graded groups coordinatized by this algebra (
Theorem

11.15
). In the following, we develop the necessary terminology to make these two statements precise. For this we will freely use the terminology of multiplicative conic alternative algebras that we will only later introduce in
Section

9
. For the moment, it suffices to know that a multiplicative conic alternative algebra is an algebra
C
over some commutative ring
k
which is equipped with a conjugation
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon C\to C
, a norm
n\colon C\to k
(which has a linearization
n\colon C\times C\to k
) and a trace
t\colon C\to k
. However, the reader who wishes to do so may safely skip ahead to
Section

9
to read the precise definitions.

Definition 1.9
.

A
standard system of simple roots in
F_{4}
is a tuple
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\delta_{4})
such that
\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\delta_{4}\}
is a simple system of roots,
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
are longer that
\delta_{3},\delta_{4}
and the subsystems spanned by
\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}
,
\{\delta_{2},\delta_{3}\}
,
\{\delta_{3},\delta_{4}\}
are of type
A_{2}
,
B_{2}
,
A_{2}
, respectively.

Definition 1.10

(
[
31
, 4.3.2, 10.1.17]
)
.

Let
C
be a multiplicative conic alternative algebra over a commutative ring
k
. The
twisting group (of
(k,C)
)
is the group
T\coloneq\{\pm 1\}\times\{\pm 1\}
together with the actions on (the sets)
k
and
C
defined by

\displaystyle(-1,1).t

\displaystyle\coloneq-t,\quad(1,-1).t\coloneq t,\quad(-1,1).c\coloneq-c,\quad(1,-1).c\coloneq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

for all
t\in k
,
c\in C
.

Definition 1.11

(
[
31
, 4.2.4]
)
.

Let
\Delta
be a system of simple roots in
F_{4}
and let
T
be any abelian group. A
\Delta
-parity map with values in
T
is any map
\eta\colon F_{4}\times\Delta\to T\colon(\alpha,\delta)\mapsto\eta_{\alpha,\delta}
. For any sequence
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{\ell}\in\Delta
with
\ell\geq 2
, we put

\eta_{\alpha,\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{\ell}}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\eta_{\alpha^{\sigma(\delta_{1})\cdots\sigma(\delta_{i-1})},\delta_{i}}.

Definition 1.12

(
[
31
, 4.3.4]
)
.

Let
C
be a multiplicative conic alternative algebra over a commutative ring
k
, let
T
be the twisting group of
(k,C)
, let
\Delta
be a system of simple roots in
F_{4}
and let
\eta
be a
\Delta
-parity map with values in
T
. Further, let
G
be a group with an
F_{4}
-grading
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
and let
w_{\delta}
be a
\delta
-Weyl element for all
\delta\in\Delta
. Put
P_{\alpha}\coloneq k
for all long
\alpha\in F_{4}
and
P_{\alpha}\coloneq C
for all short
\alpha\in F_{4}
. Then a
parametrization of
(G,(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}})
by
(k,C)
with respect to
(w_{\delta})_{\delta\in\Delta}
and
\eta
is a family of isomorphisms
(\theta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
such that

\theta_{\alpha}(p)^{w_{\delta}}=\theta_{\alpha^{\sigma(\delta)}}(\eta_{\alpha,\delta}.p)\quad\text{for all }p\in P_{\alpha}.

Remark 1.13
.

A parametrization automatically satisfies

\theta_{\alpha}(p)^{w_{\delta_{1}}\cdots w_{\delta_{\ell}}}=\theta_{\alpha^{\sigma(\delta_{1})\cdots\sigma(\delta_{\ell})}}(\eta_{\alpha,\delta}.p)\quad\text{for all }p\in P_{\alpha}.

Remark 1.14
.

Let
\Delta
be a system of simple roots in
F_{4}
. Not every parity map (with values in the twisting group of some pair
(k,C)
) is reasonable in the sense that it may arise in a parametrization of an
F_{4}
-graded group: For example, it is known that for many possible choices of
\alpha\in F_{4}
,
\delta\in\Delta
, the action of the square of any
\delta
-Weyl element on
U_{\alpha}
must be the inversion map (see
[
31
, 10.5.5]
), and hence any reasonable parity map
\eta
should satisfy
\eta_{\alpha,\delta\delta}=(-1,1)
. An example of a reasonable parity map
\eta^{\text{c}}\colon F_{4}\times\Delta\to\{\pm 1\}^{2}
is given in
[
31
, Figure 10.4]
. Here the letter
“c”
stands for the fact that this is the parity map that is used in the Coordinatization Theorem
1.15
for
F_{4}
-graded groups.

Theorem 1.15

(
[
31
, 10.7.8]
)
.

Let
G
be a group with an
F_{4}
-grading
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
. Choose a standard system
\Delta=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\delta_{4})
of simple roots in
F_{4}
and a
\delta
-Weyl element
w_{\delta}
for all
\delta\in\Delta
. Then there exist a commutative ring
k
, a multiplicative conic alternative algebra
C
over
k
and a parametrization
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
of
(G,(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}})
by
(k,C)
with respect to
(w_{\delta})_{\delta\in\Delta}
and the standard parity map
\eta^{\text{c}}
from
Remark

1.14
(whose codomain we identify with the twisting group
T
of
(k,C)
) such that the following commutator relations hold for all
s,t\in k
and all
c,d\in C
:

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{1}}(s),\theta_{\delta_{2}}(t)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}(-st),

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{2}}(s),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(c)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(-sc)\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(-sn(c)\big),

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(d)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(n(c,d)\big)=\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(t(c\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\big),

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{4}}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(d)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}(cd).

Remark 1.16
.

Keep the notation of
Theorem

1.15
and put

X\coloneq\{(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}),(\delta_{2},\delta_{3}),(\delta_{2}+\delta_{3},\delta_{3}),(\delta_{2},\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}),(\delta_{3},\delta_{4})\}.

For all
(\alpha,\beta)\in X
,
Theorem

1.15
provides an explicit commutator formula for
[U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}]
. Now let
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
be arbitrary non-proportional roots. Then by the transitivity properties of the Weyl group, there exists
u
in the Weyl group of
F_{4}
such that
(\alpha^{u},\beta^{u})\in X
. Thus we have a commutator formula for
[U_{\alpha^{u}},U_{\beta^{u}}]
. Conjugating this formula by a sequence
w_{\delta_{i_{\ell}}},\ldots,w_{\delta_{i_{1}}}
of Weyl elements where
u=\sigma_{\delta_{i_{1}}}\cdots\sigma_{\delta_{i_{\ell}}}
, we obtain a commutator formula for
[U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}]
. For example, it follows from the formula
[\theta_{\delta_{3}}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(d)]=\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}(cd)
that

[\theta_{\delta_{4}^{\sigma(\delta)}}(\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{4},\delta}.c),\theta_{\delta_{3}^{\sigma(\delta)}}(\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{3},\delta}.d)]=\theta_{\delta_{3}^{\sigma(\delta)}+\delta_{4}^{\sigma(\delta)}}(\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4},\delta}.cd)

for all
\delta\in\Delta
and all
c,d\in C
. Thus explicit knowledge of
\eta^{\text{c}}
grants explicit knowledge of all commutator relations.

Remark 1.17
.

We refer to the family
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
in
Theorem

1.15
as a
coordinatization
, meaning that it is a parametrization for which explicit commutator formulas (involving the structural maps of
(k,C)
) are known. In
[
31
, 10.4.14, 10.4.15]
, the parity map
\eta^{\text{c}}
and
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
are called the
“standard parity map”
and a
“coordinatization with standard signs”
, respectively. However, we avoid the terminology of
“standard signs”
in this paper because there is nothing canonical about the parity map
\eta^{\text{c}}
. For example, the
F_{4}
-graded group in our Existence Theorem
11.15
will, a priori, be coordinatized with respect to a different parity map
\eta^{\text{e}}
, and the commutator relations in this group (see
Proposition

11.13
) will be the same as in
Theorem

1.15
only
“up to twisting”
, that is,
“up to signs and conjugation”
. However, we will show in
Remark

11.16
how a coordinatization of one type can be modified to obtain a coordinatization of the other type, which illustrates that this difference in the choice of signs is not meaningful.

2.
Cubic norm pairs

2.1.
Definition and identities

Let
k
be an arbitrary commutative ring. To define cubic norm pairs, we first need to clarify the notion of a cubic form.

Definition 2.1

(
[
14
]
,
[
17
, 12.43]
)
.

Let
M,N
be
k
-modules. A
cubic map from
M
to
N
(over
k
)
is a pair
(f,g)
of maps

f\colon M\to N\quad\text{and}\quad g\colon M\times M\to N

with the following properties.

(1)

f(\lambda x)=\lambda^{3}f(x)
for all
x\in M
,
\lambda\in k
.

(2)

g
is quadratic in the first component and linear in the second component.

(3)

f(x+y)=f(x)+g(x,y)+g(y,x)+f(y)
for all
x,y\in M
.

(4)

Euler’s differential equation:

g(x,x)=3f(x)
for all
x\in M
.

We will also write
(f,g)\colon M\to N
. If
N=k
, we also call
(f,g)
a
cubic form on
M
.

The reason to include the
“
(2,1)
-linearization
g
of
f
”
in the definition of a cubic map is to ensure the existence and uniqueness of scalar extensions in the following sense.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
14
, Theorem 35]
,
[
17
, 12.43]
)
.

Let
M,N
be
k
-modules, let
R
be a
k
-algebra and let
(f,g)\colon M\to N
be a cubic map. Then there exists a unique cubic map
(f,g)_{R}=(f_{R},g_{R})\colon M_{R}=M\otimes_{k}R\to N_{R}=N\otimes R
, called the
R
-cubic extension of
(f,g)
, such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{R},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\mapsto m\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n\mapsto n\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{R}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{R}\times N_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,v)\mapsto(u\otimes 1,v\otimes 1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n\mapsto n\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{R}}

We are now ready to define cubic norm pairs.

Definition 2.3
.

A
cubic norm pair (over
k
)
is a pair
(J,J^{\prime})
of
k
-modules which are equipped with maps

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\colon J\times J\to J^{\prime},

\displaystyle\times^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon J^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}\to J,

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\colon J\times J^{\prime}\to k,

\displaystyle T^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon J^{\prime}\times J\to k,

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\colon J\to k,

\displaystyle N^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon J^{\prime}\to k,

\displaystyle\sharp

\displaystyle\colon J\to J^{\prime},

\displaystyle\sharp^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon J^{\prime}\to J

such that the following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

\times
and
T
are
k
-bilinear maps,
\times
is symmetric, and
T^{\prime}(a^{\prime},a)=T(a,a^{\prime})
for all
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

(2)

\sharp
is a quadratic map with linearization
\times
. That is,
(\lambda a)^{\sharp}=\lambda^{2}a^{\sharp}
and
(a+b)^{\sharp}=a^{\sharp}+b^{\sharp}+a\times b
for all
a,b\in J
and
\lambda\in k
.

(3)

Put
g\colon J\times J\to k\colon(a,b)\mapsto T^{\prime}(a^{\sharp},b)
. Then
(N,g)
is a cubic form on
M
. Explicitly,
N(\lambda a)=\lambda^{3}N(a)
,
T^{\prime}(a^{\sharp},a)=3N(a)
and the
gradient identity

N(a+b)=N(a)+T^{\prime}(a^{\sharp},b)+T^{\prime}(b^{\sharp},a)+N(b)

hold for all
a,b\in J
and
\lambda\in k
.

(4)

The
adjoint identity

(a^{\sharp})^{\sharp^{\prime}}=N(a)a
holds strictly. That is,
(a^{\sharp})^{\sharp^{\prime}}=\tilde{N}_{R}(a)a
for all
k
-algebras
R
and all
a\in J_{R}
.

(5)

N^{\prime}(a^{\sharp})=N(a)^{2}
holds strictly. That is,
N_{R}^{\prime}(a^{\sharp})=N_{R}(a)^{2}
for all
k
-algebras
R
and all
a\in J_{R}
where
N_{R}
,
N_{R}^{\prime}
are as in
Lemma

2.2
.

(6)

a\times(a^{\sharp}\times^{\prime}b^{\prime})=N(a)b^{\prime}+T(a,b^{\prime})a^{\sharp}
holds strictly. That is,
a\times(a^{\sharp}\times^{\prime}b^{\prime})=\tilde{N}_{R}(a)b^{\prime}+T(a,b^{\prime})a^{\sharp}
for all
k
-algebras
R
and all
a\in J_{R}
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}_{R}
.

(7)

All the previous conditions are still satisfied if we interchange the roles of
(J,\times,T,N,\sharp)
and
(J^{\prime},\times^{\prime},T^{\prime},N^{\prime},\sharp^{\prime})
.

We will usually simply write
\times
,
T
,
N
,
\sharp
for the maps
\times^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime}
,
\sharp^{\prime}
. We will refer to
T
as the
(bilinear) trace
and to
N
as the
norm
.

Definition 2.4

(
[
17
, 33.4]
)
.

A
cubic norm structure
is a
k
-module
J
which is equipped with maps

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\colon J\times J\to J,\quad T\colon J\times J\to k,\quad N\colon J\to k,\quad\sharp\colon J\to J

and a distinguished element
1_{J}\in J
, called the
base point
, such that the following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

(J,J)
is a cubic norm pair with structural maps
\times
,
T
,
N
,
\sharp
and
\times^{\prime}\coloneq\times
,
T^{\prime}\coloneq T
,
N^{\prime}\coloneq N
,
\sharp^{\prime}\coloneq\sharp
. It is called the
cubic norm pair associated to (or induced by)
J
.

(2)

The base point satisfies
1_{J}^{\sharp}=1_{J}
,
N(1_{J})=1_{k}
and
1_{J}\times a=T(1_{J},a)1_{J}-a
for all
a\in J
.

We will also sometimes refer to the cubic norm pair
(J,J)
induced by
J
as a cubic norm structure.

Remark 2.5
.

For the curious reader, we compare our definition of cubic norm pairs to some related ones that appear in the literature, and comment on the necessity of Axioms
Items

5
and
6
in
Definition

2.3
. These facts will not be used in the sequel.

(1)

If there exist
a,b\in J
such that
N(a)
is invertible and
kb
is free, then Axioms
Items

5
and
6
follow from the remaining ones. Namely, by similar arguments as in
[
17
, 33.7]
, the existence of
b
implies that
kc
is free for all
c\in J
for which
N(c)
is invertible, and with this observation,
Items

5
and
6
can be derived as in
[
17
, (33a.18), (33a.19)]
.

(2)

The condition in
1
on the existence of
a,b
is satisfied if there exists a base point
1_{J}
that is
unimodular
, meaning that
k1_{J}
is a free direct summand of
J
. The unimodularity of the base point is required in the definition of cubic norm structures in
[
17
, 33.1, 33.4]
, and hence Axioms
Items

5
and
6
are absent from
loc. cit.
. The condition in
1
is also satisfied if
k
is a field and
N\neq 0
, which is how
Item

5
is proven in
[
15
, (ADJ3’)]
.

(3)

Axiom
Item

6
does not appear in
[
15
]
, but follows from
Item

5
if
k
is a field. Further, it is shown in
[
15
, p. 4644]
that
Item

5
holds if
T
is non-degenerate in the sense that
T(a,J^{\prime})=0
implies
a=0_{J}
.

(4)

In
[
25
, 2.1.5]
, the identity in
Lemma

2.8
Item

21
is required as an axiom in place of our Axiom
Item

5
. In particular, it follows from
Lemma

2.8
Item

21
that a cubic norm pair in our sense is a cubic norm pair in the sense of
[
25
, 2.1.5]
.

Remark 2.6

(Linearizations)
.

By
[
17
, 12.10, 12.11]
, Axiom
2.3
Item

4
is equivalent to the requirement that the identity
(a^{\sharp})^{\sharp}=N(a)a
and its formal linearizations

(1)

\displaystyle a^{\sharp}\times(a\times b)

\displaystyle=N(a)b+T(a^{\sharp},b)a\quad\text{and}

(2)

\displaystyle\quad a^{\sharp}\times b^{\sharp}+(a\times b)^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=T(a^{\sharp},b)b+T(a,b^{\sharp})a

hold for all
a,b\in J
. (See also
[
17
, 33.14]
.) Further, by linearizing with respect to
b
, it follows from (
2
) that

a^{\sharp}\times(b\times c)+(a\times b)\times(a\times c)=T(a^{\sharp},b)c+T(a^{\sharp},c)b+T(a,b\times c)a

for all
a,b,c\in J
, which is also the linearization of (
1
) with respect to
a
.

Similarly, Axiom
2.3
Item

6
is equivalent to the requirement that
a\times(a^{\sharp}\times b^{\prime})=N(a)b^{\prime}+T(a,b^{\prime})a^{\sharp}
and the formal linearization

\displaystyle a\times\big((a\times c)\times b^{\prime}\big)+c\times(a^{\sharp}\times b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T(a^{\sharp},c)b+T(a,b^{\prime})a\times c+T(c,b^{\prime})a^{\sharp}

hold for all
a,c\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Finally, the formal linearizations of Axiom
2.3
Item

5
of respective multi-degrees
(5,1)
,
(4,2)
,
(3,3)
in
(a,b)\in J^{2}
are the identities

\displaystyle T\big((a^{\sharp})^{\sharp},a\times b\big)

\displaystyle=2T(a^{\sharp},b)N(a),

\displaystyle T\big((a^{\sharp})^{\sharp},b^{\sharp}\big)+T\big(a^{\sharp},(a\times b)^{\sharp}\big)

\displaystyle=2N(a)T(a,b^{\sharp})+T(a^{\sharp},b)^{2},

\displaystyle N(a)T(b,b^{\sharp})+T(a^{\sharp},b)T(a,b^{\sharp})+N(a\times b)

\displaystyle=2N(a)N(b)+2T(a^{\sharp},b)T(a,b^{\sharp}).

These formulas are obtained by computing
D^{i}(N\circ\sharp)
and
D^{i}(N\cdot N)
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
with the chain and product rules
[
17
, 12.17, 12.42]
, and equating the resulting expressions. The first two of these identities are consequences of
Lemma

2.8
Items

7
and
10
, whose proof is independent of Axiom
2.3
Item

5
, while the third one is equivalent to

(3)

N(a\times b)=T(a^{\sharp},b)T(a,b^{\sharp})-N(a)N(b)

because
T(b,b^{\sharp})=3N(b)
. Further, the formal linearization of (
3
) of multi-degree
(3,2,1)
in
(a,b,c)\in J^{3}
is

\displaystyle T\big((a\times b)^{\sharp},a\times c\big)

\displaystyle=T(a^{\sharp},b)T(a,b\times c)-N(a)T(b^{\sharp},c)+T(a^{\sharp},c)T(a,b^{\sharp}),

which is also a consequence of
Lemma

2.8
Item

10
. We conclude that we could replace Axiom
2.3
Item

5
by the requirement that
N(a^{\sharp})=N(a)^{2}
and (
3
) hold for all
a,b\in J
.

For the rest of this section, we assume that
(J,J^{\prime})
is a cubic norm pair.

Definition 2.7
.

The
U-operators

U_{a}\colon J^{\prime}\to J
are given by

U_{a}(a^{\prime})\coloneq T(a,a^{\prime})a-a^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime},

for all
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, with linearization

U_{a,b}(a^{\prime})=T(a,a^{\prime})b+T(b,a^{\prime})a-(a\times b)\times a^{\prime}

for all
a,b\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
We also write

D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b):=\{a,a^{\prime},b\}\coloneq U_{a,b}(a^{\prime}).

In particular, for each
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, this defines an endomorphism
D_{a,a^{\prime}}
of
J
.
Similarly, we have endomorphisms
D_{a^{\prime},a}
of
J^{\prime}
.
(There is no danger of confusion in notation, because
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.)
We will see in
Lemma

2.9
that the
U
-operators equip
(J,J^{\prime})
with the structure of a Jordan pair. We will sometimes write
D(a,a^{\prime})
for
D_{a,a^{\prime}}
.

The following lemma says that all identities which are proven in
[
17
, 33.8]
for cubic norm structures and which do not refer to the distinguished base point remain valid. We keep the same numbering as in
loc. cit.
.

Lemma 2.8
.

The following identities hold for all
a,b,c,d\in J
,
b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and also for all
a,b,c,d\in J^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in J
:

(7)

T(a\times b,c)=T(a,b\times c)
.

(8)

a^{\sharp}\times(a\times b)=N(a)b+T(a^{\sharp},b)a
.

(9)

(a\times b)\times(a\times c)+a^{\sharp}\times(b\times c)=T(a^{\sharp},b)c+T(a^{\sharp},c)b+T(a\times b,c)a
.

(10)

a^{\sharp}\times b^{\sharp}+(a\times b)^{\sharp}=T(a^{\sharp},b)b+T(a,b^{\sharp})a
.

(16)

U_{a}(a\times b)=T(a^{\sharp},b)a-N(a)b
.

(17)

\{a,a^{\sharp},b\}=2N(a)b
.

(20)

(U_{a}b^{\prime})^{\sharp}=U_{a^{\sharp}}((b^{\prime})^{\sharp})
.

(21)

N(U_{a}b^{\prime})=N(a)^{2}N(b^{\prime})
.

(25)

\begin{aligned} &(a\times b)\times(c\times d)+(b\times c)\times(a\times d)+(c\times a)\times(b\times d)\\
&\qquad=T(a\times b,c)d+T(b\times c,d)a+T(c\times d,a)b+T(d\times a,b)c.\end{aligned}

(26)

\begin{aligned} &a\times(b^{\prime}\times(a\times c))=T(a,b^{\prime})a\times c+T(a^{\sharp},c)b^{\prime}+T(b^{\prime},c)a^{\sharp}-(a^{\sharp}\times b^{\prime})\times c\\
&\qquad=(U_{a}b^{\prime})\times c+T(a^{\sharp},c)b^{\prime}+T(b^{\prime},c)a^{\sharp}\end{aligned}

(27)

\begin{aligned} &a\times(b^{\prime}\times(c\times d))+c\times(b^{\prime}\times(d\times a))+d\times(b^{\prime}\times(a\times c))\\
&\qquad=T(a,b^{\prime})c\times d+T(c,b^{\prime})d\times a+T(d,b^{\prime})a\times c+T(a\times c,d)b^{\prime}.\end{aligned}

(29)

N(a\times b)=T(a^{\sharp},b)T(a,b^{\sharp})-N(a)N(b)
.

(30)

T(U_{a}b^{\prime},c^{\prime})=T(b^{\prime},U_{a}c^{\prime})
.

(31)

T(\{a,b^{\prime},c\},d^{\prime})=T(c,\{b^{\prime},a,d^{\prime}\})
.

Proof.

The same arguments and computations as in
[
17
, 33.8]
for cubic norm structures work in our more general setting. Note that, unlike in
[
17
, 33.8]
, we cannot use the Zariski-density argument
[
17
, 12.24]
to restrict our attention to elements with invertible norm because it is not guaranteed that the set

\mathbf{D}(N)(k)\coloneq\{a\in J\mid N(a)\text{ invertible}\}

is non-empty. Note also that we have already seen
Items

8
,
9
,
10
,
26
and
29
in
Remark

2.6
.
∎

Lemma 2.9

(
[
15
, Proposition 1]
)
.

The
U
-operators from
Definition

2.7
equip
(J,J^{\prime})
with the structure of a Jordan pair. In other words, the identities

\displaystyle\{a,b^{\prime},U_{a}c^{\prime}\}=U_{a}(\{b^{\prime},a,c^{\prime}\}),

\displaystyle\{U_{a}b^{\prime},b^{\prime},c\}=\{a,U_{b^{\prime}}a,c\}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{U_{a}b^{\prime}}c^{\prime}=U_{a}U_{b^{\prime}}U_{a}c^{\prime}

hold for all
a,c\in J
,
b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This holds by the computations in
[
15
, Proposition 1]
. Alternatively, we may cite
[
25
, 1.2.9]
. The first and third identity also follow from the computations in
[
17
, 33.9]
. All identities required in the proof hold by
Lemma

2.8
.
∎

We briefly summarize the relationship between cubic norm pairs and cubic norm structures, which naturally leads to the notion of homotopies.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})
,
(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
be two cubic norm pairs, let
\varphi\colon J_{1}\to J_{2}
,
\varphi^{\prime}\colon J_{1}^{\prime}\to J_{2}^{\prime}
be two homomorphisms of
k
-modules and let
t\in k
be invertible. Then the pair
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is called a
t
-homotopy of cubic norm pairs
if

\displaystyle N_{2}\bigl(\varphi(a)\bigr)

\displaystyle=tN_{1}(a),

\displaystyle\varphi(a)^{\sharp_{2}}

\displaystyle=t\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\sharp_{1}}),

\displaystyle T_{2}\big(\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=T_{1}(a,a^{\prime}),

\displaystyle N_{2}\bigl(\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\bigr)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}N_{1}(a^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})^{\sharp_{2}}

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\varphi\bigl((a^{\prime})^{\sharp_{1}}\bigr)

for all
a\in J_{1}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{1}^{\prime}
. A
homotopy
is a
t
-homotopy for some invertible
t\in k
, and a
homomorphism
is a
1
-homotopy. We say that
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is surjective, injective or bijective if both
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
have the respective property. Bijective homotopies are also called isotopies and bijective homomorphisms are also called isomorphisms.

Remark 2.11
.

A
t
-homotopy
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of cubic norm pairs automatically satisfies

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(a\times_{1}b)

\displaystyle=\varphi^{\prime}\big((a+b)^{\sharp}-a^{\sharp}-b^{\sharp}\big)=t^{-1}\big((\varphi(a)+\varphi(b))^{\sharp}-\varphi(a)^{\sharp}-\varphi(b)^{\sharp}\big)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\varphi(a)\times_{2}\varphi(a)

and similarly
\varphi(a^{\prime}\times_{1}b^{\prime})=t\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\times_{2}\varphi^{\prime}(b^{\prime})

for all
a,b\in J_{1}
,
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J_{1}^{\prime}
. Further, if
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is a bijective
t
-homotopy, then
(\varphi^{-1},(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1})
is a bijective
t^{-1}
-homotopy, and the composition of a
t_{1}
-homotopy with a
t_{2}
-homotopy is a
t_{1}t_{2}
-homotopy.

Example 2.12
.

Let
t,s\in k
be invertible. Then
(t\id_{J},t^{-1}\id_{J^{\prime}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J,J^{\prime})
is a
t^{3}
-isotopy. It follows that for any
s
-isotopy
(\phi,\phi^{\prime})
, the pair
(t\phi,t^{-1}\phi^{\prime})=(\phi,\phi^{\prime})\circ(t\id_{J},t^{-1}\id_{J^{\prime}})
is a
t^{3}s
-isotopy.

Remark 2.13
.

Any homomotopy
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of cubic norm pairs is also a homomorphism of Jordan pairs: If
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is a
t
-homotopy for
t\in k
invertible, we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}\big(U_{a}(a^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=T(a,a^{\prime})\varphi(a)-\varphi(a^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime})=T\big(\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\big)\varphi(a)-t\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\sharp})\times\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T\big(\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\big)\varphi(a)-\varphi(a)^{\sharp}\times\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})=U_{\varphi(a)}\big(\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\big)

for all
a\in J_{1}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{1}^{\prime}
, and similarly,
\varphi(U_{a^{\prime}}(a))=U_{\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}\varphi(a)
. It follows that
\varphi(\{a,a^{\prime},b\})=\{\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime}),\varphi(b)\}
and
\varphi^{\prime}(\{a^{\prime},a,b^{\prime}\})=\{\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime}),\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(b^{\prime})\}
for all
a,b\in J_{1}
,
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J_{1}^{\prime}
. We conclude that we have an implication chain
“homomorphism of cubic norm pairs”

\implies

“homotopy of cubic norm pairs”

\implies

“homomorphism of Jordan pairs”
.

Remark 2.14
.

Let
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(J_{1},J_{1})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
be a
t
-homotopy of cubic norm pairs (for some invertible
t\in k
) where
(J_{1},J_{1})
is the cubic norm pair induced by a cubic norm structure
J_{1}
. Then
(\varphi^{\prime},\varphi)\colon(J_{1},J_{1})\to(J_{2}^{\prime},J_{2})
is a
t^{-1}
-homotopy.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
t\in k
be invertible. A
t
-involution
of a cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
is a
t
-isotopy
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)
of cubic norm pairs such that
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}
. An
involution
is a
1
-involution.

Remark 2.16
.

Any cubic norm pair that is induced by a cubic norm structure has an involution. However, a cubic norm pair with an involution is not necessarily induced by a cubic norm structure because it need not have a base point in the sense of
Definition

2.4
.

Remark 2.17
.

Assume that there exists
p\in J
such that
N(p)
is invertible, and put
p^{\prime}\coloneq N(p)^{-1}p^{\sharp}
. Then by
[
25
, 1.1.10]
or by the same computations as in
[
17
, 33.10]
,
p
is invertible in the sense of Jordan pairs with inverse
p^{\prime}
(meaning that
U_{p}\colon J^{\prime}\to J
is invertible with inverse
U_{p^{\prime}}
). Moreover, we may define a cubic norm structure with base point
p
on the
k
-module
J^{(p)}\coloneq J
by the formulas in
[
25
, 1.1.10]
or
[
17
, 33.11]
. It is called the
p
-isotope of
(J,J^{\prime})
and its structural maps are given by

\displaystyle N^{(p)}\colon J\to k

\displaystyle\colon a\mapsto N(p^{\prime})N(a)=N(p^{\prime})^{-1}N(U_{p^{\prime}}a),

\displaystyle\sharp^{(p)}\colon J\to J

\displaystyle\colon a\mapsto N(p^{\prime})^{-1}U_{p^{\prime}}(a)^{\sharp}=N(p^{\prime})U_{p^{\prime}}^{-1}(a^{\sharp}),

\displaystyle T^{(p)}\colon J\times J\to k

\displaystyle\colon(a,b)\mapsto T\big(U_{p^{\prime}}a,b\big)=T\big(a,U_{p^{\prime}}b\big),

\displaystyle\times^{(p)}\colon J\times J\to k

\displaystyle\colon(a,b)\mapsto N(p^{\prime})U_{p^{\prime}}^{-1}(a\times b)=N(p^{\prime})^{-1}\big(U_{p^{\prime}}(a)\times U_{p^{\prime}}(b)\big).

In this setup, a straightforward computation shows that the pair

(\id_{J},U_{p}^{-1})=(\id_{J},U_{p^{\prime}})\colon(J^{(p)},J^{(p)})\to(J,J^{\prime})

is an
N(p)
-isotopy. By
Remark

2.14
, this implies that

(U_{p}^{-1},\id_{J})=(U_{p^{\prime}},\id_{J})\colon(J^{(p)},J^{(p)})\to(J^{\prime},J)

is an
N(p)^{-1}
-isotopy. In particular, the composition

(U_{p}^{-1},U_{p})=(U_{p}^{-1},\id_{J})\circ(\id_{J},U_{p}^{-1})^{-1}\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)

is an
N(p)^{-2}
-involution, and hence

(N(p)U_{p}^{-1},N(p)^{-1}U_{p})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)

is an
N(p)
-involution.

We conclude that a cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
is isotopic to the cubic norm pair induced by a cubic norm structure if and only if there exists
p\in J
such that
N(p)
is invertible. Further, for a second cubic norm pair
(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
and a pair
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})\to(J,J^{\prime})
of
k
-linear maps, another straightforward computation shows that
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is an
N(p)
-homomoty if and only if the composition
(\id_{J},U_{p})\circ(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is a homomorphism of cubic norm pairs (from
(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
to
(J^{(p)},J^{(p)})
). Thus the notion
“
t
-homotopy to
(J,J^{\prime})
for some invertible
t\in k\cap\im(N)
”
coincides with the notion
“homomorphism to an isotope of
(J,J^{\prime})
”
. The second notion is precisely the definition of homotopies in the theory of Jordan algebras (see
[
17
, 31.17]
).

We conclude this section with some identities that will be needed later.

Lemma 2.18
.

For all
a,b,c\in J
, the following identities hold.

(1)

D_{a,a^{\sharp}}=2N(a)\id_{J}
.

(2)

D_{a^{\sharp},a}=2N(a)\id_{J^{\prime}}
.

(3)

D_{a,b^{\sharp}}+D_{b,a\times b}=2T(a,b^{\sharp})\id_{J}
.

(4)

D_{a^{\sharp},b}+D_{a\times b,a}=2T(a^{\sharp},b)\id_{J^{\prime}}
.

(5)

D_{a,b\times c}+D_{b,c\times a}+D_{c,a\times b}=2T(a,b\times c)\id_{J}
.

(6)

D_{a\times b,c}+D_{b\times c,a}+D_{c\times a,b}=2T(a\times b,c)\id_{J^{\prime}}
.

The same identities hold for all
a,b,c\in J^{\prime}
if the roles of
\id_{J}
and
\id_{J^{\prime}}
are interchanged.

Proof.

Let
a,b,c,d\in J
.
By
Definition

2.3
Item

3
and
Lemma

2.8
Item

8
, we have

D_{a,a^{\sharp}}(c)=T(a,a^{\sharp})c+T(c,a^{\sharp})a-(a\times c)\times a^{\sharp}=2N(a)c,

proving
Item

1
.
Next, by
Lemma

2.8
Items

7
and
9
, we have

\displaystyle D_{a,b^{\sharp}}(c)+D_{b,a\times b}(c)

\displaystyle=T(a,b^{\sharp})c+T(c,b^{\sharp})a-(a\times c)\times b^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.44434pt+T(b,a\times b)c+T(c,a\times b)b-(b\times a)\times(b\times c)

\displaystyle=2T(a,b^{\sharp})c,

proving
Item

3
.
Now
Item

5
follows immediately from
Item

3
by linearizing in
b
.

The proofs of
Item

2
,
Item

4
and
Item

6
are completely similar.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

For all
a,b\in J
and
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, the following identities hold.

(1)

U_{a}(a^{\sharp})=N(a)a
.

(2)

T(U_{a}(c^{\prime}),a^{\sharp})=N(a)T(a,c^{\prime})
.

(3)

U_{a}(c^{\prime})\times a=T(a,c^{\prime})a^{\sharp}-N(a)c^{\prime}
.

(4)

T\bigl((a\times b)\times c^{\prime},a^{\sharp}\bigr)=N(a)T(b,c^{\prime})+T(b,a^{\sharp})T(a,c^{\prime})
.

(5)

T\bigl((a\times b)^{\sharp},a^{\sharp}\bigr)=N(a)T(a,b^{\sharp})+T(b,a^{\sharp})^{2}
.

(6)

(a\times b)^{\sharp}\times a=T(a,b^{\sharp})a^{\sharp}+T(b,a^{\sharp})a\times b-N(a)b^{\sharp}
.

Proof.

Let
a,b\in J
and
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

(1)

Using
Definition

2.3
Items

3
and
4
, we have

U_{a}(a^{\sharp})=T(a,a^{\sharp})a-a^{\sharp}\times a^{\sharp}=3N(a)a-2(a^{\sharp})^{\sharp}=N(a)a
.

(2)

Again using
Definition

2.3
Items

3
and
4
, we get

\displaystyle T(U_{a}(c^{\prime}),a^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=T(a,c^{\prime})T(a,a^{\sharp})-T(a^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},a^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=3N(a)T(a,c^{\prime})-T(a^{\sharp}\times a^{\sharp},c^{\prime})=N(a)T(a,c^{\prime}).

(3)

By
Definition

2.3
Item

6
, we have

U_{a}(c^{\prime})\times a=T(a,c^{\prime})a\times a-(a^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime})\times a=T(a,c^{\prime})a^{\sharp}-N(a)c^{\prime}
.

(4)

Using
Lemma

2.8
Item

8
, we get

T\bigl((a\times b)\times c^{\prime},a^{\sharp}\bigr)=T\bigl((a\times b)\times a^{\sharp},c^{\prime}\bigr)=N(a)T(b,c^{\prime})+T(b,a^{\sharp})T(a,c^{\prime})
.

(5)

Using
Definition

2.3
Items

3
and
4
and
Lemma

2.8
Item

10
, we get

\displaystyle T\bigl((a\times b)^{\sharp},a^{\sharp}\bigr)

\displaystyle=T(b,a^{\sharp})T(b,a^{\sharp})+T(a,b^{\sharp})T(a,a^{\sharp})-T(a^{\sharp}\times b^{\sharp},a^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=T(b,a^{\sharp})^{2}+3N(a)T(a,b^{\sharp})-T(a^{\sharp}\times a^{\sharp},b^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=N(a)T(a,b^{\sharp})+T(b,a^{\sharp})^{2}.

(6)

Using
Definition

2.3
Item

6
and
Lemma

2.8
Item

10
, we get

\displaystyle(a\times b)^{\sharp}\times a

\displaystyle=T(b,a^{\sharp})b\times a+T(a,b^{\sharp})a\times a-(a^{\sharp}\times b^{\sharp})\times a

\displaystyle=T(b,a^{\sharp})a\times b+2T(a,b^{\sharp})a^{\sharp}-N(a)b^{\sharp}-T(a,b^{\sharp})a^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=T(a,b^{\sharp})a^{\sharp}+T(b,a^{\sharp})a\times b-N(a)b^{\sharp}.\qed

2.2.
Linear cubic norm pairs

One of our main goals, which will be accomplished in
Section

4
, is to construct a
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra
L=L(J,J^{\prime})
from an arbitrary cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
. Since a Lie algebra is a
“linear object”
, it is not surprising that
L
depends only on the ring
k
, the modules
J
,
J^{\prime}
and the bilinear maps
\times
and
T
. In other words, the maps
N
and
\sharp
of higher degree will appear neither in the definition of the module
L
nor in its multiplication table (
Table

1
). This observation, which may seem like a mere curiosity at first glance, will be needed explicitly in
Section

6
(specifically, in
Remark

6.17
). The purpose of this section is to make the aforementioned observation more precise by introducing the notion of
linear cubic norm pairs
, which capture the linear structure of (and hence generalize) cubic norm pairs. It is only this linear structure that will be needed in
Sections

3
,
4
,
5
and
6
. In contrast, the definition of exponential maps on the Lie algebra
L
in
Section

7
involves the maps
\sharp
and
N
. Hence our construction of
G_{2}
-graded groups works only for cubic norm pairs but not for arbitrary linear cubic norm pairs.

The reader who is enthusiastic to get to our main results may want to skip this somewhat technical section until later.

Definition 2.20
.

A
linear cubic norm pair (over
k
)
is a pair
(J,J^{\prime})
of
k
-modules which are equipped with maps

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\colon J\times J\to J^{\prime},

\displaystyle\times^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon J^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}\to J,

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\colon J\times J^{\prime}\to k,

\displaystyle T^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon J^{\prime}\times J\to k

such that the following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

\times
and
T
are
k
-bilinear maps,
\times
is symmetric, and
T^{\prime}(a^{\prime},a)=T(a,a^{\prime})
for all
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

(2)

The identities in
Lemma

2.8
Items

7
,
25
,
27
and
31
hold:

\displaystyle T^{\prime}(a\times b,c)=T(a,b\times c),

\displaystyle a\times(b^{\prime}\times^{\prime}(a\times c))=T(a,b^{\prime})a\times c+T^{\prime}(a^{\sharp},c)b^{\prime}+T^{\prime}(b^{\prime},c)a^{\sharp}-(a^{\sharp}\times^{\prime}b^{\prime})\times c

\displaystyle\qquad=(U_{a}b^{\prime})\times c+T^{\prime}(a^{\sharp},c)b^{\prime}+T^{\prime}(b^{\prime},c)a^{\sharp},

\displaystyle a\times(b^{\prime}\times^{\prime}(c\times d))+c\times(b^{\prime}\times^{\prime}(d\times a))+d\times(b^{\prime}\times^{\prime}(a\times c))

\displaystyle\qquad=T(a,b^{\prime})c\times d+T(c,b^{\prime})d\times a+T(d,b^{\prime})a\times c+T^{\prime}(a\times c,d)b^{\prime},

\displaystyle T(\{a,b^{\prime},c\},d^{\prime})=T(c,\{b^{\prime},a,d^{\prime}\})

for all
a,b,c\in J
and
b^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

(3)

All the previous conditions are still satisfied if we interchange the roles of
(J,\times,T)
and
(J^{\prime},\times^{\prime},T^{\prime})
.

We will usually simply write
\times
,
T
,
N
,
\sharp
for the maps
\times^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime}
,
\sharp^{\prime}
. We will sometimes refer to cubic norm pairs as
proper
cubic norm pairs to emphasize the distinction from linear cubic norm pairs.

Example 2.21
.

Any proper cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
with maps
\times
,
\times^{\prime}
,
T
,
T^{\prime}
,
N
,
N^{\prime}
,
\sharp
,
\sharp^{\prime}
induces a linear cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
with maps
\times
,
\times^{\prime}
,
T
,
T^{\prime}
. Certain quotients of cubic norm pairs, which we will encounter in
Remark

6.17
, provide examples of linear cubic norm pairs that are not (proven to be) proper cubic norm pairs.

For the rest of this section,
(J,J^{\prime})
denotes a linear cubic norm pair over
k
.

Definition 2.22
.

For all
a,b\in J
, we define a
U
-operator

U_{a,b}\colon J^{\prime}\to J
by

U_{a,b}(a^{\prime})\coloneq T(a,a^{\prime})b+T(b,a^{\prime})a-(a\times b)\times a^{\prime}

for all
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
We also write

D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b):=\{a,a^{\prime},b\}\coloneq U_{a,b}(a^{\prime}).

Remark 2.23
.

The set of axioms in
Definition

2.20
is adequate for our purposes, but it might not be sufficient to build a satisfying theory of linear cubic norm pairs (which we have no interest in doing). For example, we do not claim that the
U
-operators equip
(J,J^{\prime})
with the structure of a linear Jordan pair, and this seems cumbersome to prove (if it is even true).

Definition 2.24
.

Let
(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})
,
(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
be two linear cubic norm pairs, let
\varphi\colon J_{1}\to J_{2}
,
\varphi^{\prime}\colon J_{1}^{\prime}\to J_{2}^{\prime}
be two homomorphisms of
k
-modules and let
t\in k
be invertible. Then the pair
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is called a
t
-homotopy of linear cubic norm pairs
if

\displaystyle T_{2}\big(\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\big)=T_{1}(a,a^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(a\times_{1}b)=t^{-1}\varphi(a)\times_{2}\varphi(a),\quad\varphi(a^{\prime}\times_{1}b^{\prime})=t\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime})\times_{2}\varphi^{\prime}(b^{\prime})

for all
a,b\in J_{1}
,
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J_{1}^{\prime}
. A
homotopy
is a
t
-homotopy for some invertible
t\in k
, and a
homomorphism
is a
1
-homotopy. We say that
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
is surjective, injective or bijective if both
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
have the respective property. Bijective homotopies are also called isotopies and bijective homomorphisms are also called isomorphisms.

Remark 2.25
.

Any homotopy of linear cubic norm pairs satisfies

\varphi(\{a,a^{\prime},b\})=\{\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime}),\varphi(b)\}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi^{\prime}(\{a^{\prime},a,b^{\prime}\})=\{\varphi^{\prime}(a^{\prime}),\varphi(a),\varphi^{\prime}(b^{\prime})\}

for all
a,b\in J_{1}
,
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J_{1}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.26
.

The identities

\displaystyle D_{a,b\times c}+D_{b,c\times a}+D_{c,a\times b}

\displaystyle=2T(a,b\times c)\id_{J},

\displaystyle D_{a\times b,c}+D_{b\times c,a}+D_{c\times a,b}

\displaystyle=2T(a\times b,c)\id_{J^{\prime}}

from
Lemma

2.18
remain valid for linear cubic norm pairs because they can be proven using only
Lemma

2.8
Items

7
,
25
and
27
.

3.
The Tits–Kantor–Koecher Lie algebra for cubic norm pairs

Throughout this section, we assume that
(J,J^{\prime})
is a cubic norm pair (or, more generally, a linear cubic norm pair) over the commutative ring
k
. We first introduce derivations and inner derivations.
(We have not seen this notion before in the literature.)

Definition 3.1
.

(1)

A
derivation
of
(J,J^{\prime})
is a pair of
k
-linear maps
\delta=(\delta_{+},\delta_{-})
where
\delta_{+}\colon J\to J
and
\delta_{-}\colon J^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
such that there is a constant
\lambda\in k
for which

(4)

\displaystyle\delta_{-}(a\times b)

\displaystyle=\delta_{+}(a)\times b

\displaystyle+a\times\delta_{+}(b)

\displaystyle-\lambda(a\times b),

(5)

\displaystyle\delta_{+}(a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\delta_{-}(a^{\prime})\times b^{\prime}

\displaystyle+a^{\prime}\times\delta_{-}(b^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\lambda(a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime}),

(6)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=T(\delta_{+}(a),a^{\prime})

\displaystyle+T(a,\delta_{-}(a^{\prime}))

,

for all
a,b\in J
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

(2)

The collection of all derivations of
(J,J^{\prime})
forms a Lie algebra that we denote by
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
.

(3)

Let
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Then the pair

\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}:=(D_{a,a^{\prime}},-D_{a^{\prime},a})

defines an
inner derivation
of the cubic norm pair.
We will show in
Lemma

3.3
below that
\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}
is indeed a derivation of
(J,J^{\prime})
, with
\lambda=2T(a,a^{\prime})
.

(4)

We define the
grading derivation

\gamma:=(\id_{J},-\id_{J^{\prime}}).

This is indeed a derivation of
(J,J^{\prime})
, with
\lambda=3
.

Remark 3.2
.

If
\delta=(\delta_{+},\delta_{-})
is a derivation, then

\delta_{+}\bigl((a\times b)\times a^{\prime}\bigr)=\bigl(\delta_{+}(a)\times b\bigr)\times a^{\prime}+\bigl(a\times\delta_{+}(b)\bigr)\times a^{\prime}+(a\times b)\times\delta_{-}(a^{\prime})

for all
a,b\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Together with
Eq.

6
, it follows that

\delta_{+}\{a,a^{\prime},b\}=\{\delta_{+}(a),a^{\prime},b\}+\{a,\delta_{-}(a^{\prime}),b\}+\{a,a^{\prime},\delta_{+}(b)\}

for all
a,b\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
In the language of
Jordan pairs
, this tells exactly that
\delta=(\delta_{+},\delta_{-})
is a derivation of the Jordan pair
(J,J^{\prime})
.
See, for instance,
[
19
, §6.8]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The pair
\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}:=(D_{a,a^{\prime}},-D_{a^{\prime},a})
is a derivation of
(J,J^{\prime})
. More precisely, it satisfies
Eqs.

4
,
5
and
6
with
\lambda=2T(a,a^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.8
Item

27
(or
Definition

2.20
),

\bigl((a\times b)\times a^{\prime}\bigr)\times c+\bigl((b\times c)\times a^{\prime}\bigr)\times a+\bigl((c\times a)\times a^{\prime}\bigr)\times b\\
=T(a,b\times c)a^{\prime}+T(a,b^{\prime})b\times c+T(b,b^{\prime})c\times a+T(c,b^{\prime})a\times b

for all
a,b,c\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Using this identity, it is easy to verify that

(7)

D_{a^{\prime},a}(b\times c)+D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)\times c+b\times D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)=2T(a,a^{\prime})b\times c,

proving
Eq.

4
with
\lambda=2T(a,a^{\prime})
.
The proof of
Eq.

5
is similar, and
Eq.

6
holds by
Lemma

2.8
Item

31
(or
Definition

2.20
).
∎

We can now define the Tits–Kantor–Koecher Lie algebra
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
of the cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
.
First, let

I\coloneq\TKK_{0}(J,J^{\prime})\coloneq\langle\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in J,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle+k\gamma\leq\Der(J,J^{\prime}).

We then define the
TKK Lie algebra

\TKK(J,J^{\prime}):=J^{\prime}\oplus I\oplus J,

equipped with the usual Lie bracket on
I
inherited from
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
extended by the rules

(8)

[J,J]=0,\quad[J^{\prime},J^{\prime}]=0,\quad[\delta,a]=\delta_{+}(a),\quad[\delta,a^{\prime}]=\delta_{-}(a^{\prime}),\quad[a^{\prime},a]=\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}

for all
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
\delta\in I
.

To show that this defines a
3
-graded Lie algebra, we could make a detour via Jordan pairs (see
[
21
]
), but we believe that it is interesting to provide a (short and easy) direct proof of this fact.

Proposition 3.4
.

The algebra
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})=J^{\prime}\oplus I\oplus J
defined above is a
3
-graded Lie algebra, with
J^{\prime}
of degree
-1
,
I
of degree
0
and
J
of degree
1
.

Proof.

We first observe that
I
is, in fact, an ideal of
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
.
Indeed, let
\delta=(\delta_{+},\delta_{-})\in\Der(J,J^{\prime})
and let
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Then using
Eqs.

6
and
3.2
, we compute that

(9)

[\delta,\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}]=\delta_{\delta_{+}(a),a^{\prime}}+\delta_{a,\delta_{-}(a^{\prime})}.

Moreover,
\gamma
is central in
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
, so indeed
I=\langle\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in J,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle+k\gamma
is an ideal of
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
. In particular,
I
is itself a Lie algebra.

Next, we claim that the identities
Eq.

8
make
J
(and similarly,
J^{\prime}
) into a module for the Lie algebra
I
.
In fact, if we allow
\delta\in\Der(J,J^{\prime})
in these identities (rather than
\delta\in I
), then
J
becomes a module for the full Lie algebra
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
. Indeed, let
\delta=(\delta_{+},\delta_{-})
and
\epsilon=(\epsilon_{+},\epsilon_{-})
be two elements of
\Der(J,J^{\prime})
and
a\in J
, then we have to verify that
[[\delta,\epsilon],a]=[\delta,[\epsilon,a]]-[\epsilon,[\delta,a]]
, but since
[\delta,\epsilon]=(\delta_{+}\epsilon_{+}-\epsilon_{+}\delta_{+},\delta_{-}\epsilon_{-}-\epsilon_{-}\delta_{-})
, this is obvious from the identities
Eq.

8
.

Finally, we have to check that the Jacobi identity holds.
There are only two remaining non-trivial cases to consider.
We first consider triples of the form
(a,b,a^{\prime})\in J\times J\times J^{\prime}
. Then
[a,b]=0
and

\displaystyle[[a^{\prime},a],b]=[\delta_{a,a^{\prime}},b]=D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)

\displaystyle[[a^{\prime},b],a]=[\delta_{b,a^{\prime}},a]=D_{b,a^{\prime}}(a),

and by definition, both expressions are equal to
U_{a,b}(a^{\prime})
.
The case of triples in
J^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}\times J
is, of course, completely similar.

Next, we consider triples of the form
(a,a^{\prime},\delta)\in J\times J^{\prime}\times\Der(J,J^{\prime})
.
By
Eq.

9
, we have

[\delta,[a^{\prime},a]]=[\delta,\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}]=\delta_{\delta_{+}(a),a^{\prime}}+\delta_{a,\delta_{-}(a^{\prime})}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle[[\delta,a^{\prime}],a]=[\delta_{-}(a^{\prime}),a]=\delta_{a,\delta_{-}(a^{\prime})}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle[[\delta,a],a^{\prime}]=[\delta_{+}(a),a^{\prime}]=-\delta_{\delta_{+}(a),a^{\prime}},

and we see that indeed the Jacobi identity holds also in this case.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

In fact, we have shown that also the larger algebra

\widetilde{\TKK}(J,J^{\prime}):=J^{\prime}\oplus\Der(J,J^{\prime})\oplus J

is a
3
-graded Lie algebra, but we will not need this.

4.
Constructing the Lie algebra

4.1.
Constructing the bracket

We continue to assume that
(J,J^{\prime})
is a cubic norm pair (or, more generally, a linear cubic norm pair) over the commutative ring
k
. Our aim is to extend
\widetilde{L}_{0}:=\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
to a larger
5
-graded Lie algebra

L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus\widetilde{L}_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}

with

L_{-2}\cong L_{2}\cong k\quad\text{and}\quad L_{-1}\cong L_{1}\cong k\oplus J\oplus J^{\prime}\oplus k,

with a finer
5\times 5
-grading as in
Fig.

1
. This Lie algebra will be denoted by
L
, or (in later sections) by
L(J,J^{\prime})
to emphasize its dependence on
(J,J^{\prime})
.

\langle x\rangle
\langle f_{-}\rangle
J^{\prime}_{-1}
J_{-1}
\langle e_{-}\rangle
J
Z
J^{\prime}
\langle f_{+}\rangle
J^{\prime}_{1}
J_{1}
\langle e_{+}\rangle
\langle y\rangle
L_{-2}
L_{-1}
L_{0}
L_{1}
L_{2}
L^{\prime}_{2}
L^{\prime}_{1}
L^{\prime}_{0}
L^{\prime}_{-1}
L^{\prime}_{-2}
L^{\prime\prime}_{2}
L^{\prime\prime}_{1}
L^{\prime\prime}_{0}
L^{\prime\prime}_{-1}
L^{\prime\prime}_{-2}

Figure 1.
Construction of a
5\times 5
-graded Lie algebra

This involves an important subtlety, as we now explain, and in particular, we will only be able to construct such a Lie algebra after modifying
\widetilde{L}_{0}

(which is also why the middle part in
Fig.

1
is called
Z
rather than
I
).

We will first need to ensure the presence of a
grading derivation

\xi
for this new grading, which we can do, in principle, by defining

\overline{L}_{0}:=\TKK(J,J^{\prime})\oplus k\xi
, a central extension of
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
by a free
k
-module spanned by a new element
\xi
of degree
0
.
However, in the resulting Lie algebra that we will construct, the element
\xi
is not necessarily linearly independent from
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
, so we will replace
\overline{L}_{0}
by the Lie algebra
L_{0}
consisting of the corresponding inner derivations acting on
\bigoplus_{i\neq 0}L_{i}
, following a typical idea that has been worked out in detail in
[
7
, Construction 4.1.2]
.
In addition, this will also solve any problems that we might have if we would try to directly define an action of
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
on the
L_{i}
.
(See also
Remark

4.18
below.)

We start by setting up the notation for the
k
-modules
L_{i}
for
i\neq 0
.

Definition 4.1
.

(1)

We define four
k
-modules

\displaystyle L_{-2}

\displaystyle:=kx,

\displaystyle L_{-1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}\mid\lambda,\mu\in k,b\in J,b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\mid\lambda,\mu\in k,b\in J,b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle:=ky.

(2)

We will further decompose

\displaystyle L_{-1}

\displaystyle=L_{-1,-2}

\displaystyle\oplus L_{-1,-1}

\displaystyle\oplus L_{-1,0}

\displaystyle\oplus L_{-1,1},

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=L_{1,-1}

\displaystyle\oplus L_{1,0}

\displaystyle\oplus L_{1,1}

\displaystyle\oplus L_{1,2},

where

\displaystyle L_{-1,-2}

\displaystyle:=\{(\lambda,0,0,0)_{-}\mid\lambda\in k\},

\displaystyle L_{1,-1}

\displaystyle:=\{(\lambda,0,0,0)_{+}\mid\lambda\in k\},

\displaystyle L_{-1,-1}

\displaystyle:=\{(0,b,0,0)_{-}\mid b\in J\},

\displaystyle L_{1,0}

\displaystyle:=\{(0,b,0,0)_{+}\mid b\in J\},

\displaystyle L_{-1,0}

\displaystyle:=\{(0,0,b^{\prime},0)_{-}\mid b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle L_{1,1}

\displaystyle:=\{(0,0,b^{\prime},0)_{+}\mid b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle L_{-1,1}

\displaystyle:=\{(0,0,0,\mu)_{-}\mid\mu\in k\},

\displaystyle L_{1,2}

\displaystyle:=\{(0,0,0,\mu)_{+}\mid\mu\in k\}.

We also write
L_{-2,-1}:=L_{-2}
and
L_{2,1}:=L_{2}
.
We call elements of
L_{i,j}
, whenever
L_{i,j}
is defined,
homogeneous of
\xi
-degree
i
and
\zeta
-degree
j
.

(3)

We define the Lie algebra
M:=\bigoplus_{i\neq 0}\mathfrak{gl}(L_{i})
.
In
Definition

4.9
, we will be able to define the Lie algebra
L_{0}
as a subalgebra of
M
, an idea that also appears in
[
7
, Construction 4.1.2]
.
Notice that trivially, each
L_{i}
is a module for the Lie algebra
M
.

(4)

We define two specific elements
\zeta
and
\xi
in
M
by setting

\xi(l)=il\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta(l)=jl\quad\text{whenever }l\in L_{i,j}.

Explicitly,

\displaystyle\zeta(x)

\displaystyle:=-x,

\displaystyle\zeta\bigl((\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}\bigr)

\displaystyle:=(-2\lambda,-b,0,\mu)_{-},

\displaystyle\zeta\bigl((\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\bigr)

\displaystyle:=(-\lambda,0,b^{\prime},2\mu)_{+},

\displaystyle\zeta(y)

\displaystyle:=y,

\displaystyle\xi(l_{i})

\displaystyle:=il_{i}\quad\text{for all }l_{i}\in L_{i},i\neq 0.

(5)

Let
\varphi=(\varphi_{i})_{i\neq 0}\in M
.
We say that
\varphi

has
\zeta
-degree
j
if each
\varphi_{i}
maps all elements of
\zeta
-degree
i
to elements of
\zeta
-degree
i+j
.
(Visually, this means that
\varphi
shifts the parts in
Fig.

1
down by
j
.)
In particular,
\zeta
and
\xi
have
\zeta
-degree
0
.

(6)

Since our goal is to construct a Lie algebra
L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
with
L_{0}\leq M
, we will already denote actions of elements of
M
on each
L_{i}
using a Lie bracket, i.e., we will write
1
1

1

In principle, each
\varphi\in M
is a
4
-tuple
\varphi=(\varphi_{i})_{i\neq 0}
, so
[\varphi,l_{i}]:=\varphi_{i}(l_{i})
, but we will omit the subscript
i
and simply write
\varphi(l_{i})
without danger of confusion.

[\varphi,l_{i}]:=\varphi(l_{i})
for all
\varphi\in M
and
l_{i}\in L_{i}
,
i\neq 0
.

Remark 4.2
.

Whenever we define a Lie bracket between certain elements, it is silently understood that it is skew-symmetric.
In other words, once we define an element
[l,m]
, we silently assume that we also define
[m,l]:=-[l,m]
.

Remark 4.3
.

A graphical depiction of the subspaces of
L
defined in
Definition

4.1
is given in
Fig.

1
(where
Z
is a certain subspace of
M
that will be defined in
Definition

4.9
). Our goal is to define a Lie bracket on
L
such that these subspaces constitute a
5\times 5
-grading on
L
, meaning that
[L_{ij},L_{pq}]\subseteq L_{i+p,j+q}
for all
i,j,p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
where
L_{st}\coloneq 0
for all
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
for which no such space
L_{st}
has been defined previously. In particular, the
“vertical lines”

(L_{i})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
, the
“horizontal lines”

(L_{i}^{\prime})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
and the
“diagonal lines”

(L_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
that can be read off from
Fig.

1
will each constitute a 5-grading of
L
. When
k
is a field, these 5-gradings correspond to the so-called
“hyperbolic pairs”

(x,y)
,
(e_{-},f_{+})
and
(-f_{-},e_{+})
in
L
, respectively; see
[
10
, §4, page 28]
. Note that
L_{i,j}=L_{i}\cap L_{j}^{\prime}
for all
i,j\in\{-2,\ldots,2\}
. Further, we have a
7
-grading
(L^{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}_{i})_{-3\leq i\leq 3}
of
L
defined by

L^{{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}}_{i}\coloneq\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{p,i-p}

for all
i\in\{-3,\ldots,3\}
. (This grading, however, does not correspond to a hyperbolic pair.)

Remark 4.4
.

Let
\{\gamma,\delta\}
be a choice of simple roots for the root system
G_{2}
, where
\gamma
is long and
\delta
is short.
Then there is a unique group homomorphism
\omega
from
\mathbb{Z}G_{2}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
mapping
\gamma
to
(-2,-1)
and
\delta
to
(1,0)
.
Now a straightforward computation shows that
\omega(G_{2})
is precisely the set of indices
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
for which we defined a module
L_{i,j}
in
Definition

4.1
.
Hence
\omega(G_{2}^{0})
is precisely the set of indices for which a non-trivial submodule of
L
is depicted in
Fig.

1
.
We conclude that any
5\times 5
-grading of the form in
Fig.

1
may equivalently be regarded as a
G_{2}
-grading.
For this reason, we will sometimes identify
G_{2}^{0}
with the set
\omega(G_{2}^{0})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, so that we have a decomposition

L=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}L_{\alpha}.

(This means that we implictly choose a system of simple roots
\{\gamma,\delta\}
in
G_{2}
in such situations.)

Remark 4.5
.

Let
W
be the subgroup of the Weyl group of
G_{2}
which is generated by the reflections corresponding to long roots. Then there are three orbits of
G_{2}
under
W
: One orbit consisting of all six long roots and two orbits consisting of three short roots in each. The first one corresponds to the root spaces of
L
parametrized by
k
while the other two correspond to the root spaces canonically parametrized by
J
or
J^{\prime}
, respectively. (For the last statement to be true, we have to consider
J
and
J^{\prime}
as distinct parametrizing spaces even if
J=J^{\prime}
, which we underline by saying
“canonically parametrized”
.)

Remark 4.6
.

The element
2\zeta-\xi
will appear at several points. It is the unique element
\varphi\in L_{0}
that satisfies

\varphi(L_{\pm 2})=0\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi\big((\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\big)=(-3\lambda,-b,b^{\prime},3\mu)_{\pm}

for all
(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\in L_{\pm 1}
.

Intuitively, our next goal is to make each of the four parts
L_{-2}
,
L_{-1}
,
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
into a module for the Lie algebra
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
.
This is not quite possible, so instead, we first make them into a module for the abelian Lie algebras
J^{\prime}
and
J
.

Definition 4.7
.

(1)

We define
L_{-2}
and
L_{2}
as trivial
J
- and
J^{\prime}
-modules, i.e.,
we set
[a,x]=[a,y]=0
and
[a^{\prime},x]=[a^{\prime},y]=0
for all
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

(2)

To define the action of
J
and
J^{\prime}
on
L_{-1}
and
L_{1}
, we set

\displaystyle\bigl[a,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\bigr]

\displaystyle:=\bigl(0,\ \lambda a,\ a\times b,\ T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{\pm}\ ,

\displaystyle\bigl[a^{\prime},(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\bigr]

\displaystyle:=-\bigl(T(b,a^{\prime}),\ a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},\ \mu a^{\prime},\ 0\bigr)_{\pm}\ ,

for all
a,b\in J
,
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
\lambda,\mu\in k
.

(3)

Together, this associates to each
a\in J
and to each
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
an element of
M
, which we will denote by
\ad_{a}
and
\ad_{a^{\prime}}
, respectively. Since the map
J\to M\colon a\mapsto\ad_{a}
is clearly injective, we can safely identify
a
and
\ad_{a}
(and similarly for
J^{\prime}
).
Notice that the elements
\ad_{a^{\prime}}
with
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
have
\zeta
-degree
-1
and that the elements
\ad_{a}
with
a\in J
have
\zeta
-degree
1
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Definition

4.7
makes each
L_{i}\ (i\neq 0)
into a module for the abelian Lie algebras
J
and
J^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is obvious for the trivial modules
L_{-2}
and
L_{2}
.
We will now verify that
L_{1}
is a
J
-module; the remaining three cases are completely similar.
So let
a,c\in J
and
z=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\in L_{1}
. We compute

\displaystyle[a,[c,z]]

\displaystyle=\bigl[a,\bigl(0,\ \lambda c,\ c\times b,\ T(c,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ 0,\ \lambda a\times c,\ T(a,c\times b)\bigr)_{+},

and since
T(a,c\times b)=T(c,a\times b)
by
Lemma

2.8
Item

7
, we get
[a,[c,z]]=[c,[a,z]]
as required.
∎

We can rephrase
Proposition

4.8
as the fact that the maps
J\to M\colon a\mapsto\ad_{a}
and
J^{\prime}\to M\colon a^{\prime}\mapsto\ad_{a^{\prime}}
are homomorphisms of Lie algebras.
(As in
Definition

4.7
Item

3
, this now implies that we may view
J
and
J^{\prime}
as Lie subalgebras of
M
.)
Interestingly, this does
not
extend to a Lie algebra homomorphism
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})\to M
. Instead, we do the following.

Definition 4.9
.

For each
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, we define

\mathbf{d}(a,a^{\prime})\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\coloneq[\ad_{a^{\prime}},\ad_{a}]\in M.

We also set

Z:=L_{0,0}:=\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in J,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle+k\zeta+k\xi\leq M

and finally also

L_{0}:=J^{\prime}\oplus Z\oplus J\leq M.

Further, we denote by
L_{0,-1}
,
L_{0,0}
and
L_{0,1}
the subspaces
J^{\prime}
,
Z
and
J
of
L_{0}
, respectively.

Remark 4.10
.

Notice that the sum in the definition of
L_{0}
is indeed a direct sum.
Indeed, as we observed in
Definition

4.7
Item

3
, the elements of
J^{\prime}
have
\zeta
-degree
-1
and the elements of
J
have
\zeta
-degree
1
.
On the other hand, the elements of
Z
all have
\zeta
-degree
0
.
However, the sum

\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in J,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle+k\zeta+k\xi

is never a direct sum (cf.
Lemma

4.19
).

Remark 4.11
.

Observe that
\xi
centralizes
M
and that
\zeta
centralizes
Z
. Further, since elements of
J
have
\zeta
-degree
1
, we have
[\zeta,\ad_{a}]=\ad_{a}
for all
a\in J
. Similarly,
[\zeta,\ad_{a^{\prime}}]=-\ad_{a^{\prime}}
for all
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

We first explicitly compute the action of
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
on each
L_{i}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Then
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}(L_{\pm 2})=0
and

\bigl[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\bigr]\\
=\bigl(-\lambda T(a,a^{\prime}),\ D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)-T(a,a^{\prime})b,\ -D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})+T(a,a^{\prime})b^{\prime},\ \mu T(a,a^{\prime})\bigr)_{\pm}

for all
\lambda,\mu\in k
,
b\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
z=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}
.
Since
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}=\ad_{a^{\prime}}\ad_{a}-\ad_{a}\ad_{a^{\prime}}
, we can use
Definition

4.7
to get

\displaystyle[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},z]

\displaystyle=[a^{\prime},[a,z]]-[a,[a^{\prime},z]]

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}-\bigl(\lambda T(a,a^{\prime}),\ a^{\prime}\times(a\times b),\ T(a,b^{\prime})a^{\prime},\ 0\bigr)_{\pm}\\
+\bigl(0,\ T(b,a^{\prime})a,\ a\times(a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime}),\ \mu T(a,a^{\prime})\bigr)_{\pm},\end{multlined}

and using the definition of
D_{a,a^{\prime}}
and
D_{a^{\prime},a}
, we get the required formula.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
a,b\in J
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Then

(10)

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}]=\mathbf{d}_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b),b^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{b,D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})}.

In particular,
Z
is a Lie subalgebra of
M
.

Proof.

First observe that by
Eq.

9
, we have

[\delta_{a,a^{\prime}},\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}]=\delta_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b),b^{\prime}}-\delta_{b,D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})},

so in particular,

[D_{a,a^{\prime}},D_{b,b^{\prime}}]=D_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b),b^{\prime}}-D_{b,D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})}.

By
Proposition

4.12
, on the other hand, we have

\bigl[[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}],(\lambda,c,c^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\bigr]=\bigl(0,\ [D_{a,a^{\prime}},D_{b,b^{\prime}}](c),\ -[D_{a^{\prime},a},D_{b^{\prime},b}](c^{\prime}),\ 0\bigr)_{\pm}.

Since

(11)

T(D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b),b^{\prime})-T(b,D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime}))=0

by
Eq.

6
, we conclude that both sides of
Eq.

10
have the same action on
L_{\pm 1}
.
Since they both act trivially on
L_{\pm 2}
, they have the same action on each
L_{i}
(
i\neq 0
), and hence they represent the same element of
M
.
This proves
Eq.

10
and shows, in particular, that
\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in J,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle
is a Lie subalgebra of
M
.
Together with
Remark

4.11
, it follows that
Z
is indeed a Lie subalgebra of
M
.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
a,c\in J
and
a^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Then

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\ad_{c}]=\ad_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)}\quad\text{and}\quad[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\ad_{c^{\prime}}]=-\ad_{D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime})}.

Proof.

We compute the action of
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\ad_{c}]
on an arbitrary element
(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\in L_{\pm 1}
:

\displaystyle\big[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},[c,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}]]

\displaystyle=\big[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\big(0,\lambda c,c\times b,T(c,b^{\prime})\big)_{\pm}\big]

\displaystyle=\big(0,\lambda\big(D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)-T(a,a^{\prime})c\big),-D_{a^{\prime},a}(c\times b)+T(a,a^{\prime})(c\times b),T(c,b^{\prime})T(a,a^{\prime})\big)_{\pm},

\displaystyle\big[c,[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}]]
