\displaystyle=\big[c,\big(-\lambda T(a,a^{\prime}),D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)-T(a,a^{\prime})b,-D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})+T(a,a^{\prime})b^{\prime},\mu T(a,a^{\prime})\big)_{\pm}\big]

\displaystyle=\big(0,-\lambda T(a,a^{\prime})c,c\times\big(D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)-T(a,a^{\prime})b\big),T\big(c,-D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})+T(a,a^{\prime})b^{\prime}\big)\big)_{\pm}.

Using
Eqs.

7
and
11
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\big[[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\ad_{c}],\ (\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}]

\displaystyle=\big(0,\ \lambda D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c),\ D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)\times b,\ T(D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c),b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\big[D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c),(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\big].

This shows that
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\ad_{c}]
and
\ad_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)}
have the same action on
L_{1}
and
L_{-1}
.
Since both elements also act trivially on
L_{2}
and
L_{-2}
, they represent the same element of
M
, i.e., they coincide. The proof of the second assertion is completely similar.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

L_{0}
is a Lie subalgebra of
M
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

4.13
, we already know that
Z
is a Lie subalgebra of
M
.
Further,
[J,k\zeta+k\xi]\subseteq L_{0}
and
[J^{\prime},k\zeta+k\xi]\subseteq L_{0}
by
Remark

4.11
.
Finally, by
Proposition

4.14
,
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},J]\subseteq L_{0}
and
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},J^{\prime}]\subseteq L_{0}
for all
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Notice that since each
L_{i}
is an
M
-module, it is automatically also an
L_{0}
-module, now that we know that
L_{0}
is a Lie subalgebra of
M
.

We will need the following lemma in the proof of
Proposition

4.27
below.
At the same time, it may serve as a good example to explain the subtle difference between the
\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}\in\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
and the
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\in M
that we have just defined.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
a,b,c\in J
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Then the following hold.

(1)

\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\sharp}}=N(a)\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)
.

(2)

\mathbf{d}_{(a^{\prime})^{\sharp},a}=N(a^{\prime})\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)
.

(3)

\mathbf{d}_{a,b^{\sharp}}+\mathbf{d}_{b,a\times b}=T(a,b^{\sharp})\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)
.

(4)

\mathbf{d}_{(a^{\prime})^{\sharp},b}+\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},a^{\prime}}=T((a^{\prime})^{\sharp},b)\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)

(5)

\mathbf{d}_{a,b\times c}+\mathbf{d}_{b,c\times a}+\mathbf{d}_{c,a\times b}=T(c,a\times b)\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)
.

(6)

\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime},a^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{c^{\prime}\times a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}=T(c^{\prime},a^{\prime}\times b^{\prime})\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)
.

Proof.

Notice that these are identities in
L_{0}
, and by construction, two such elements coincide if and only if they have the same action on
L_{i}
for all
i\neq 0
.
Since each
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
acts trivially on
L_{2}
and
L_{-2}
and the grading derivation
2\zeta-\xi\in M
kills both
L_{2}
and
L_{-2}
, it suffices to verify this for
i=\pm 1
.

Recall from
Lemma

2.18
Item

1
that

D_{a,a^{\sharp}}(d)=2N(a)d.

Together with
Proposition

4.12
, this yields, using
T(a,a^{\sharp})=3N(a)
, that

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\sharp}},\ (\lambda,d,d^{\prime},\mu)_{+}]=\bigl(-3\lambda N(a),\ -N(a)d,\ N(a)d^{\prime},\ 3\mu N(a)\bigr)_{+}.

Using
Remark

4.6
, we conclude that both sides of
Item

1
have the same action on
L_{1}
. The verification for
L_{-1}
is similar, so
Item

1
holds.

The proofs of the remaining identities follow in a completely similar way from the other identities in
Lemma

2.18
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

In contrast, notice that in
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
, we have

\delta_{a,a^{\sharp}}=2N(a)\cdot\gamma,

by
Lemma

2.18
Items

1
and
2
.
(See
Definition

3.1
Item

4
and
Lemma

3.3
.)
Comparing this with
Lemma

4.17
shows, in particular, that the action introduced in
Definition

4.7
does not extend to an action of
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
on
L_{i}
, as we mentioned just before
Definition

4.9
.
See, however,
Corollary

4.20
below.

The appearance of a multiple of
2\zeta-\xi
in
Lemma

4.17
is not a coincidence.
In the following lemma and its proof, all summations are understood to run over
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
f\in L_{0,0}
be arbitrary and write

(12)

f=\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta+\lambda_{\xi}\xi

for certain scalars
\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}},\lambda_{\zeta},\lambda_{\xi}\in k
. Then
f=0
if and only if the following assertions are satisfied.

(1)

\lambda_{\zeta}+2\lambda_{\xi}=0
.

(2)

\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})=3\lambda_{\xi}
.

(3)

\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}=2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J}
and
\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}=2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J^{\prime}}
.

In this situation, we have

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}=\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)\quad\text{and}\quad\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}=\lambda_{\xi}\cdot 2\gamma.

Proof.

Let
t:=\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})
. Then

\displaystyle[f,(1,0,0,0)_{+}]

\displaystyle=(-t+\lambda_{\xi}-\lambda_{\zeta},0,0,0)_{+},

\displaystyle[f,(1,0,0,0)_{-}]

\displaystyle=(-t-\lambda_{\xi}-2\lambda_{\zeta}),

\displaystyle[f,(0,b,0,0)_{+}]

\displaystyle=\left(0,\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)+(-t+\lambda_{\xi})b,0,0\right)_{+},

\displaystyle[f,(0,b,0,0)_{-}]

\displaystyle=\left(0,\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)+(-t-\lambda_{\xi}-\lambda_{\zeta})b\right)_{-},

\displaystyle[f,(0,0,b^{\prime},0)_{+}]

\displaystyle=\left(-\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})+(t+\lambda_{\xi}+\lambda_{\zeta})b^{\prime},0\right)_{+},

\displaystyle[f,(0,0,b^{\prime},0)_{-}]

\displaystyle=\left(-\textstyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})+(t-\lambda_{\xi})b^{\prime},0\right)_{-},

\displaystyle[f,(0,0,0,1)_{+}]

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,t+\lambda_{\xi}+2\lambda_{\zeta}),

\displaystyle[f,(0,0,0,1)_{-}]

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,t-\lambda_{\xi}+\lambda_{\zeta}),

\displaystyle[f,x]

\displaystyle=(-2\lambda_{\xi}-\lambda_{\zeta})x,

\displaystyle[f,y]

\displaystyle=(2\lambda_{\xi}+\lambda_{\zeta})y.

Since
f=0
if and only if all these expressions are zero, the equivalence in the assertion follows. The remaining equations are then a straightforward consequence, using that
\gamma=(\id_{J},-\id_{J^{\prime}})
and
\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}=(D_{a,a^{\prime}},-D_{a^{\prime},a})
.
∎

Corollary 4.20
.

The linear map

\pi\colon Z\to\TKK_{0}(J,J^{\prime})\colon\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta+\lambda_{\xi}\xi\mapsto\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\gamma

is well-defined. It is an epimorphism of Lie algebras, which extends to an epimorphism
L_{0}\to\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
.
In particular,
L_{0}
is a central extension of
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
.

Proof.

If
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta+\lambda_{\xi}\xi=0
, then also
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\gamma=0
by
Lemma

4.19
, so
\pi
is well-defined. Further, since
\xi,\zeta
are central in
Z
and
\gamma
is central in
\TKK_{0}(J,J^{\prime})
, it follows from the comparison of
Eqs.

9
and
4.13
that
\pi
is a homomorphism of Lie algebras. Clearly,
\pi
is surjective. Since
L_{0}=J\oplus Z\oplus J^{\prime}
and
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})=J\oplus\TKK_{0}(J,J^{\prime})\oplus J^{\prime}
, we can extend
\pi
to an epimorphism
L_{0}\to\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
of
k
-modules in a natural way. This defines an epimorphism of Lie algebras because for all
a,b\in J
,
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, we have
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},b]=D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)
and
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},b^{\prime}]=-D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})
in
L_{0}
by
Proposition

4.14
and
[\delta_{a,a^{\prime}},b]=(\delta_{a,a^{\prime}})_{+}(b)=D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)
and
[\delta_{a,a^{\prime}},b^{\prime}]=(\delta_{a,a^{\prime}})_{-}(b^{\prime})=-D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})
in
\TKK(J,J^{\prime})
by
Eq.

8
.

It remains to show that the kernel of
\pi
is central. Let

f=\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\xi}\xi+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta

denote an arbitrary element of the kernel and let
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle[f,\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\left[\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}\right]=\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\big(\mathbf{d}(D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c),c^{\prime})-\mathbf{d}(c,D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}\big(\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c),c^{\prime}\big)-\mathbf{d}\big(c,\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime})\big).

Since
f
lies in the kernel of
\pi
, we have
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\gamma=0
or, in other words,

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}=-\lambda_{\zeta}\id_{J}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}=-\lambda_{\zeta}\id_{J^{\prime}}.

Hence

\displaystyle[f,\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}]=\mathbf{d}(-\lambda_{\zeta}c,c^{\prime})-\mathbf{d}(c,-\lambda_{\zeta}c^{\prime})=0.

Further,
[f,\xi]=0=[f,\zeta]
. Thus the kernel of
\pi
is indeed central.
∎

We are now ready to make the
k
-module

(13)

L:=L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}

into a Lie algebra.
It remains to describe the Lie bracket between elements of
L_{i}
and
L_{j}
for
i,j\neq 0
, followed by a somewhat lengthy verification that this does indeed define a Lie algebra.
Before we proceed, we also fix the second grading on
L
that was long visible on
Fig.

1
.

Definition 4.21
.

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

L^{\prime}_{j}:=\bigoplus_{i}L_{i,j},

where the
L_{i,j}
are as in
Definitions

4.1
and
4.9
, with the convention that
L_{i,j}=0
if it has not been defined.
In particular,
L
now admits two decompositions

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=L^{\prime}_{-2}\oplus L^{\prime}_{-1}\oplus L^{\prime}_{0}\oplus L^{\prime}_{1}\oplus L^{\prime}_{2}.

(Observe that both
L_{i}=0
and
L^{\prime}_{i}=0
whenever
|i|>2
.)

Notice that
[\xi,l]=il
for all
l\in L_{i}
and
[\zeta,l]=jl
for all
l\in L^{\prime}_{j}
.
However, we cannot use these identities to
define

L_{i}
and
L^{\prime}_{j}
in general because our base ring
k
is arbitrary (so e.g. we might have
2=0
in
k
).

Definition 4.22
.

Let

\displaystyle[x,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}]

\displaystyle:=-(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}\,,

\displaystyle[y,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}]

\displaystyle:=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\,,

\displaystyle[x,y]

\displaystyle:=\xi,

\displaystyle\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},\ (\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\bigr]

\displaystyle:=\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)x,

\displaystyle\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+},\ (\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle:=\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)y,

for all
\lambda,\mu,\nu,\rho\in k
,
a,b\in J
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Of course, we define
[L_{i},L_{j}]:=0
whenever
|i+j|>2
.
It only remains to define
[L_{-1},L_{1}]
, which is the most complicated case:

(14)

\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},\ (\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]\\
\quad:=\bigl(-\lambda c^{\prime}+b\times c-\nu b^{\prime}\bigr)\\
+\bigl((\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot\zeta+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)+\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\bigr)\\
+\bigl(-\rho b+b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}-\mu c\bigr).

We first observe that all these definitions respect both gradings.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
l\in L_{i}\cap L^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
and
m\in L_{j}\cap L^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
.
Then
[l,m]\in L_{i+j}\cap L^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward verification, going through the different cases, using
Definitions

4.7
,
4.9
and
4.22
.
∎

We have summarized the complete definition of the Lie bracket on
L=L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
in
Table

1
.

\hskip 8.19447pt-D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime})+T(a,a^{\prime})c^{\prime},\\[0.90417pt]
\hskip 45.20828pt\rho T(a,a^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\bigl(-\nu T(a,a^{\prime}),\\[0.64583pt]
\hskip 7.3612ptD_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)-T(a,a^{\prime})c,\\[0.64583pt]
\hskip 7.3612pt-D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime})+T(a,a^{\prime})c^{\prime},\\[0.64583pt]
\hskip 32.29156pt\rho T(a,a^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\end{array}$}}$}}}\hfill\hbox{}&0\\
\xi&-2x&-(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}&0&0&0&0&0&(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}&2y\\
\zeta&-x&(-2\nu,-c,0,\rho)_{-}&-c^{\prime}&0&0&0&c&(-\nu,0,c^{\prime},2\rho)_{+}&y\\
b&0&\bigl(0,\ \nu b,\ b\times c,\ T(b,c^{\prime})\bigr)_{-}&-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}&-D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)&0&-b&0&\bigl(0,\ \nu b,\ b\times c,\ T(b,c^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}&0\\
\hline\cr(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}&(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}&\noindent\hbox{}{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\ \bigl(\lambda c^{\prime}-b\times c+\nu b^{\prime}\bigr)\\[3.44444pt]
\quad{}+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot\zeta\\
\quad{}+(\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot(\xi-\zeta)\\
\quad{}-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\\[2.58334pt]
\hskip 38.74988pt{}+\bigl(\rho b-b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}+\mu c\bigr)\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\ \bigl(\lambda c^{\prime}-b\times c+\nu b^{\prime}\bigr)\\[3.44444pt]
\quad{}+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot\zeta\\
\quad{}+(\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot(\xi-\zeta)\\
\quad{}-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\\[2.58334pt]
\hskip 38.74988pt{}+\bigl(\rho b-b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}+\mu c\bigr)\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\ \bigl(\lambda c^{\prime}-b\times c+\nu b^{\prime}\bigr)\\[2.41112pt]
\hskip 8.19447pt{}+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot\zeta\\
\hskip 8.19447pt{}+(\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot(\xi-\zeta)\\
\hskip 8.19447pt{}-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\\[1.80835pt]
\hskip 27.12497pt{}+\bigl(\rho b-b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}+\mu c\bigr)\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\ \bigl(\lambda c^{\prime}-b\times c+\nu b^{\prime}\bigr)\\[1.72221pt]
\hskip 7.3612pt{}+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot\zeta\\
\hskip 7.3612pt{}+(\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot(\xi-\zeta)\\
\hskip 7.3612pt{}-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\\[1.29167pt]
\hskip 19.37494pt{}+\bigl(\rho b-b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}+\mu c\bigr)\end{array}$}}$}}}\hfill\hbox{}&\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime}),\ c^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},\ \mu c^{\prime},\ 0\bigr)_{+}&\noindent\hbox{}{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\bigl(\lambda T(e,e^{\prime}),\\[1.29167pt]
\quad-D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)+T(e,e^{\prime})b,\\[1.29167pt]
\quad D_{e^{\prime},e}(b^{\prime})-T(e,e^{\prime})b^{\prime},\\[1.29167pt]
\hskip 51.6665pt-\mu T(e,e^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\bigl(\lambda T(e,e^{\prime}),\\[1.29167pt]
\quad-D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)+T(e,e^{\prime})b,\\[1.29167pt]
\quad D_{e^{\prime},e}(b^{\prime})-T(e,e^{\prime})b^{\prime},\\[1.29167pt]
\hskip 51.6665pt-\mu T(e,e^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\bigl(\lambda T(e,e^{\prime}),\\[0.90417pt]
\hskip 8.19447pt-D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)+T(e,e^{\prime})b,\\[0.90417pt]
\hskip 8.19447ptD_{e^{\prime},e}(b^{\prime})-T(e,e^{\prime})b^{\prime},\\[0.90417pt]
\hskip 36.16663pt-\mu T(e,e^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\end{array}$}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox{$\begin{array}[c]{@{}l@{}}\bigl(\lambda T(e,e^{\prime}),\\[0.64583pt]
\hskip 7.3612pt-D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)+T(e,e^{\prime})b,\\[0.64583pt]
\hskip 7.3612ptD_{e^{\prime},e}(b^{\prime})-T(e,e^{\prime})b^{\prime},\\[0.64583pt]
\hskip 25.83325pt-\mu T(e,e^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\end{array}$}}$}}}\hfill\hbox{}\par&-(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}&(\lambda,0,-b^{\prime},-2\mu)_{+}&-\bigl(0,\ \lambda c,\ c\times b,\ T(c,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}&\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)y&0\\
\hline\cr y&-\xi&(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}&0&0&-2y&-y&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1.
The Lie bracket on
L=L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}

4.2.
Verification of the Jacobi identity

To verify the Jacobi identity on
L
, we use a little trick to simplify some computations.
The following auxiliary definition is a useful shorthand.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
a,b,c\in L
, where
L
is a
k
-module equipped with a
k
-bilinear skew-symmetric operation
[\cdot,\cdot]
.
We write
\Jac(a,b,c)
if the Jacobi identity holds for the triple
(a,b,c)
, i.e.,

[[a,b],c]+[[b,c],a]+[[c,a],b]=0.

Notice that by the skew-symmetry,
\Jac(a,b,c)
implies
\Jac(x,y,z)
for any permutation
(x,y,z)
of
(a,b,c)
.
Similarly, if
A
,
B
and
C
are subsets (usually subspaces) of
L
, we write
\Jac(A,B,C)
if
\Jac(a,b,c)
for all
a\in A
,
b\in B
and
c\in C
. We also write, for example,
\Jac(a,B,C)
as a shorthand for
\Jac(\{a\},B,C)
when
a\in L
and
B
and
C
are subsets of
L
.

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
L
be a
k
-algebra with a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
L=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{i}
, denote the multiplication in
L
by
[\cdot,\cdot]

and assume that it is skew-symmetric.
Fix
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
l,m\in L_{0}
.
Assume that
\Jac(l,L_{i},L_{j})
,
\Jac(m,L_{i},L_{j})
and
\Jac(l,m,L_{i}\cup L_{j}\cup L_{i+j})
.

Then also
\Jac([l,m],L_{i},L_{j})
.

Proof.

This is a straighforward verification, using the fact that when
z\in L_{i}
, then also
[l,z],[m,z]\in L_{i}
, because of the grading.
∎

Remark 4.26
.

By
Definition

4.21
, we have

[\xi,\ell]=i\ell\quad\text{and}\quad[\zeta,\ell]=j\ell\quad\text{whenever }\ell\in L_{i}\cap L^{\prime}_{j}.

As a consequence, the Jacobi identity will be automatically satisfied for triples involving
\xi
or
\zeta
.
More precisely, if
\ell\in L_{i}\cap L^{\prime}_{j}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in L_{i^{\prime}}\cap L^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
, then by
Lemma

4.23
, we have
[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]\in L_{i+i^{\prime}}\cap L^{\prime}_{j+j^{\prime}}
, so we have

\displaystyle[[\zeta,\ell],\ell^{\prime}]+[[\ell,\ell^{\prime}],\zeta]+[[\ell^{\prime},\zeta],\ell]

\displaystyle=[j\ell,\ell^{\prime}]-[\zeta,[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]]+[\ell,j^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=j[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]-(j+j^{\prime})[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]+j^{\prime}[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]=0,

and similarly for
\xi
.

Proposition 4.27
.

The algebra
L=L_{-2}\oplus L_{-1}\oplus L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
equipped with the Lie brackets from
Definitions

4.7
,
4.9
and
4.22
is a (5-graded) Lie algebra.

Proof.

We have to check the Jacobi identity for all triples of elements in
L
.
By linearity, we may of course assume that each of these elements is homogeneous, i.e., belongs to some
L_{i}
.
It is clear from our definitions that the proof for triples in
L_{i}
,
L_{i^{\prime}}
,
L_{i^{\prime\prime}}
can be translated
mutatis mutandis
to a proof for triples in
L_{-i}
,
L_{-i^{\prime}}
,
L_{-i^{\prime\prime}}
.
In addition, the Jacobi identity is trivially satisfied if
|i+i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}|>2
, and by
Remark

4.16
, we already know that it is satisfied if at least two of the three indices are
0
.
Recall also that by
Remark

4.2
, the Lie bracket is skew-symmetric.
That leaves us with the following nine cases for
(i,i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime})
:

\displaystyle(0,1,1),

\displaystyle(0,1,-1),

\displaystyle(0,1,-2),

\displaystyle(0,2,-2),

\displaystyle(1,1,-1),

\displaystyle(1,1,-2),

\displaystyle(1,2,-1),

\displaystyle(1,2,-2),

\displaystyle(2,2,-2).

Unavoidably, we will deal with these different cases one by one.
In each case, we will assume that
\ell\in L_{i}
,
\ell^{\prime}\in L_{i^{\prime}}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in L_{i^{\prime\prime}}
and our goal is to show the Jacobi identity for the triple
(\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime})
.

Before we proceed with our case-by-case analysis, we point out that whenever we want to check the Jacobi identity for all triples
(\ell,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime})
with
\ell\in L_{0}
, it suffices to consider
\ell\in J\cup J^{\prime}
. Indeed, the case
\ell=\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
then follows from
Lemma

4.25
, and the cases
\ell=\zeta
and
\ell=\xi
are automatically satisfied by
Remark

4.26
.
In addition, since the cases
\ell\in J
and
\ell\in J^{\prime}
are completely similar in each case, we will always assume that
\ell=a\in J
whenever
i=0
.

Case
(0,1,1)
:

Write
\ell=a
,
\ell^{\prime}=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
.
Notice that
[\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}]\in L_{2}
,
so
[a,[\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}]]=0
.
We have to show that
[[a,\ell^{\prime}],\ell^{\prime\prime}]=[[a,\ell^{\prime\prime}],\ell^{\prime}]
.
We compute

\displaystyle[[a,\ell^{\prime}],\ell^{\prime\prime}]

\displaystyle=\bigl[[a,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}],(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl[\bigl(0,\ \lambda a,\ a\times b,\ T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{+},(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(\lambda T(a,c^{\prime})-T(c,a\times b)+\nu T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot y,

and since
T(c,a\times b)=T(b,a\times c)
, this is indeed symmetric in
\ell^{\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}
.

Case
(0,1,-1)
:

This is the most interesting case.
Write
\ell=a
,
\ell^{\prime}=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
.
We compute

\displaystyle[[a,\ell^{\prime\prime}],\ell^{\prime}]=\bigl[[a,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}],(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl[\bigl(0,\ \lambda a,\ a\times b,\ T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{-},(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=(\lambda a\times c-\nu a\times b)

\displaystyle\hskip 25.83325pt+\bigl(-\lambda T(a,c^{\prime})\cdot\zeta+(T(c,a\times b)-\nu T(a,b^{\prime}))\cdot(\xi-\zeta)+\lambda\mathbf{d}_{a,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{c,a\times b}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 25.83325pt+\bigl(-\lambda\rho a+(a\times b)\times c^{\prime}-T(a,b^{\prime})c\bigr);

\displaystyle[[\ell^{\prime},a],\ell^{\prime\prime}]=[\ell^{\prime\prime},[a,\ell^{\prime}]]=\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},[a,(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}]\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},\bigl(0,\ \nu a,\ a\times c,\ T(a,c^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=(-\lambda a\times c+\nu a\times b)

\displaystyle\hskip 25.83325pt+\bigl((\lambda T(a,c^{\prime})-T(b,a\times c))\cdot\zeta+\nu T(a,b^{\prime})\cdot(\xi-\zeta)+\mathbf{d}_{b,a\times c}+\nu\mathbf{d}_{a,b^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 25.83325pt+\bigl(-T(a,c^{\prime})b+(a\times c)\times b^{\prime}-\mu\nu a\bigr);

\displaystyle[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime}],a]=\Bigl[\bigl(-\lambda c^{\prime}+b\times c-\nu b^{\prime}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 68.88867pt+\bigl((\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot\zeta+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)+\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 68.88867pt+\bigl(-\rho b+b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}-\mu c\bigr),\ a\Bigr]

\displaystyle=(-\lambda\mathbf{d}_{a,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{a,b\times c}-\nu\mathbf{d}_{a,b^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\hskip 25.83325pt+\bigl((\lambda\rho+\mu\nu-T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime}))a+D_{b,c^{\prime}}(a)+D_{c,b^{\prime}}(a)\bigr)

\displaystyle=(-\lambda\mathbf{d}_{a,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{a,b\times c}-\nu\mathbf{d}_{a,b^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\hskip 25.83325pt+\bigl((\lambda\rho+\mu\nu)a+T(a,c^{\prime})b+T(a,b^{\prime})c-(a\times b)\times c^{\prime}-(a\times c)\times b^{\prime}\bigr).

The Jacobi identity
[[a,\ell^{\prime\prime}],\ell^{\prime}]+[[\ell^{\prime},a],\ell^{\prime\prime}]+[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime}],a]=0
now follows from
Lemma

4.17
.

Case
(0,1,-2)
:

Write
\ell=a
,
\ell^{\prime}=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}
. Without loss of generality,
\ell^{\prime\prime}=x
.
Notice that
[a,x]=0
, and we quickly verify that

\displaystyle[a,[x,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}]]-[x,[a,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}]]

\displaystyle=-[a,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}]-[x,\bigl(0,\ \lambda a,\ a\times b,\ T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{+}]

\displaystyle=-\bigl(0,\ \lambda a,\ a\times b,\ T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{-}+\bigl(0,\ \lambda a,\ a\times b,\ T(a,b^{\prime})\bigr)_{-}=0.

Case
(0,2,-2)
:

Write
\ell=a
. Without loss of generality,
\ell^{\prime}=y
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=x
.
We have
[a,x]=0
and
[a,y]=0
. Moreover,
[[x,y],a]=[\xi,a]=0
.

Case
(1,1,-1)
:

This is the lengthiest computation, but there are no surprises.
Write
\ell=(\nu_{1},c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime},\rho_{1})_{+}
,
\ell^{\prime}=(\nu_{2},c_{2},c_{2}^{\prime},\rho_{2})_{+}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
.
First, we have

\displaystyle[\ell^{\prime\prime},[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},\bigl(T(c_{1},c_{2}^{\prime})-T(c_{2},c_{1}^{\prime})+\rho_{1}\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\rho_{2}\bigr)\cdot y\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-T(c_{1},c_{2}^{\prime})+T(c_{2},c_{1}^{\prime})-\rho_{1}\nu_{2}+\nu_{1}\rho_{2}\bigr)\cdot(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}.

To compute
[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell],\ell^{\prime}]
, we use
Eqs.

14
and
4.7
, and after working out all the different terms, we get

\displaystyle[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell],\ell^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\begin{multlined}\lambda T(c_{2},c_{1}^{\prime})-\lambda T(c_{2},b\times c_{1})+\nu_{1}T(c_{2},b^{\prime})-\lambda\rho_{1}\nu_{2}+\nu_{2}T(c_{1},b^{\prime})-2\mu\nu_{1}\nu_{2},\\[4.30554pt]
\lambda c_{1}^{\prime}\times c_{2}^{\prime}-(b\times c_{1})\times c_{2}^{\prime}+\nu_{1}b^{\prime}\times c_{2}^{\prime}-\nu_{2}\rho_{1}b+\nu_{2}b^{\prime}\times c_{1}^{\prime}-\nu_{2}\mu c_{1}+T(c_{1},b^{\prime})c_{2}\\
-\mu\nu_{1}c_{2}-c_{1}^{\prime}\times(b\times c_{2})+T(c_{2},c_{1}^{\prime})b-(c_{1}\times c_{2})\times b^{\prime}+T(c_{2},b^{\prime})c_{1},\\[4.30554pt]
\lambda\rho_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}-\rho_{2}b\times c_{1}+\nu_{1}\rho_{2}b^{\prime}-\rho_{1}b\times c_{2}+(b^{\prime}\times c_{1}^{\prime})\times c_{2}-\mu c_{1}\times c_{2}+\lambda\rho_{1}c_{2}^{\prime}\\
-T(b,c_{1}^{\prime})c_{2}^{\prime}-T(b,c_{2}^{\prime})c_{1}^{\prime}+b\times(c_{1}^{\prime}\times c_{2}^{\prime})-T(c_{1},c_{2}^{\prime})b^{\prime}+c_{1}\times(b^{\prime}\times c_{2}^{\prime}),\\[0.0pt]
-\rho_{1}T(b,c_{2}^{\prime})+T(b^{\prime}\times c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime})-\mu T(c_{1},c_{2}^{\prime})+2\rho_{1}\rho_{2}-T(b,c_{1}^{\prime})\rho_{2}+\mu\nu_{1}\rho_{2}\Bigr)_{+}\,.\end{multlined}

The expression for
[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime}],\ell]
is, of course, exactly the same but with all subscripts
1
and
2
interchanged.
It is now pleasant to observe that when we compute the difference
[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell],\ell^{\prime}]-[[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell^{\prime}],\ell]
, many terms cancel out, and what is left is precisely the expression for
[\ell^{\prime\prime},[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]]
that we have computed above.

Case
(1,1,-2)
:

Write
\ell=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}
and
\ell^{\prime}=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
, and assume without loss of generality that
\ell^{\prime\prime}=x
.
We have

\displaystyle[[x,\ell],\ell^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\bigl[[x,(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}],(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=-\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr],

so we deduce from
Eq.

14
that

\displaystyle[[x,\ell],\ell^{\prime}]-[[x,\ell^{\prime}],\ell]

\displaystyle=\bigl[(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-},(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\bigr]-\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)\cdot\xi.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle[x,[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=\bigl[x,\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)y\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)\cdot[x,y],

and we conclude that indeed
[x,[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]]=[[x,\ell],\ell^{\prime}]-[[x,\ell^{\prime}],\ell]
.

Case
(1,2,-1)
:

Write
\ell=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
, and assume without loss of generality that
\ell^{\prime}=y
.
Notice that
[\ell,y]=0
.
Since
[y,J]=[y,J^{\prime}]=[y,[J^{\prime},J]]=0
, we have

\displaystyle[y,[\ell^{\prime\prime},\ell]]

\displaystyle=\bigl[y,(\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime}))\cdot\zeta+(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-\lambda\rho+T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu\bigr)\cdot y.

On the other hand, we also have

[[y,\ell^{\prime\prime}],\ell]=\bigl[(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+},(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\bigr]=\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\bigr)y.

Case
(1,2,-2)
:

Write
\ell=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}
and assume without loss of generality that
\ell^{\prime}=y
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=x
.
Notice that
[\ell,y]=0
and that
[x,y]=\xi
. The result now follows from the facts that
[\xi,\ell]=\ell
and
[y,[x,\ell]]=-\ell
.

Case
(2,2,-2)
:

Without loss of generality,
\ell=\ell^{\prime}=y
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}=x
, so the Jacobi identity is trivially satisfied.
∎

Proposition 4.28
.

The decomposition

L=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}L_{\alpha}

from
Remark

4.4
is a
G_{2}
-grading of the Lie algebra
L
.

Proof.

We can read off from
Table

1
that
[L_{\alpha},L_{\beta}]\leq L_{\alpha+\beta}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}^{0}
.
∎

Remark 4.29
.

In the case that
J=0=J^{\prime}
, there are only six non-trivial root spaces in
L
, and they constitute an
A_{2}
-grading of
L
.

Theorem 4.30
.

Let
(J,J^{\prime})
be a cubic norm pair over a commutative ring
k
. Then there exists a
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra
L
such that
L_{\alpha}\cong(k,+)
for all long roots
\alpha\in G_{2}
,
L_{\beta}\cong(J,+)
for three short roots
\beta\in G_{2}
which lie in the same of the three orbits of
G_{2}
in
Remark

4.5
and
L_{\gamma}\cong(J^{\prime},+)
for the remaining three short roots
\gamma
.

Proof.

As a module,
L
was constructed in
Definitions

4.1
,
4.9
and
13
, and its root spaces have the desired isomorphism type. The Lie bracket was constructed in
Definitions

4.1
,
4.7
and
4.22
and is summarized in
Table

1
. We showed in
Propositions

4.27
and
4.28
that
L
is a
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra.
∎

Remark 4.31
.

In
[
12
]
, John Faulkner constructs Lie algebras from “ternary algebras” over arbitrary commutative base rings.
Cubic norm structures provide examples of those, and the resulting Lie algebras are probably isomorphic to the Lie algebras that we have constructed (but we have not explicitly carried out this verification).

In any case, we need our more “refined” description to obtain the
G_{2}
-grading (and we want to refine it even further to an
F_{4}
-grading later).
In fact, our approach is more direct for cubic norm structures, because Faulkner uses the triple product—see
[
12
, (1.6) on p. 399]
—which in itself is already quite complicated. On the other hand, we recognize his bilinear form (1.5) directly in our formulas in
Definition

4.22
.

5.
Functoriality and reflections

In the previous section, we have constructed a Lie algebra
L=L(J,J^{\prime})
for every cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
(in fact, for every linear cubic norm pair). The current section is devoted to the construction of several interesting Lie algebra homomorphisms that are defined on
L(J,J^{\prime})
. We begin by establishing that
L(J,J^{\prime})
is functorial in
(J,J^{\prime})
: For any surjective homomorphism
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of cubic norm pairs, we construct a surjective homomorphism
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of Lie algebras that is bijective if and only if
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is (
Lemma

5.15
). Here the surjectivity of
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is a subtle requirement which ensures that
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is well-defined (see
Remark

5.3
), and which is also present in the construction of (the functorial homomorphisms defined on) the Tits-Kantor-Koecher algebra of a Jordan pair (see
[
19
, §7.1]
). We can even phrase our construction in a slightly more general way: Every surjective
t
-homotopy
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
between cubic norm pairs induces a surjective homomorphism
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
of Lie algebras. A corollary of this observation is that the isomorphism type of
L(J,J^{\prime})
depends only on the isotopy type of
(J,J^{\prime})
. Another application of the functoriality of
L(J,J^{\prime})
will be given in
Section

6
(in particular,
Remark

6.17
), where we investigate quotients of
L(J,J^{\prime})
that are induced by
“ideals of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
”
.

We also define automorphisms
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
of
L
that we call
“reflections”
because they permute the root spaces of
L(J,J^{\prime})
in a way that is prescribed by a reflection of the root system
G_{2}
. Each of these automorphisms corresponds to one of the
5
-gradings of
L(J,J^{\prime})
described in
Remark

4.3
in the sense that it reverses the grading: It maps the
i
-graded part to the
(-i)
-graded part. We can also define a fourth reflection
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
which reverses the
7
-grading of
L(J,J^{\prime})
, but only under the assumption that we are given an isomorphism (or, more generally, a
t
-isotopy)
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)
. This assumption is certainly necessary because a reflection that reverses the
7
-grading necessarily maps root spaces parametrized by
J
isomorphically to root spaces parametrized by
J^{\prime}
, and vice versa. In the general situation, we can only construct an isomorphism
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J^{\prime},J)
, and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
is simply the composition of
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
with
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
.

In
Section

7
, we will construct root groups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
in
\Aut(L)
. This would, if done naively, involve a large number of lengthy computations. The reflections described in the previous paragraph allow us to reduce the number of computations significantly by exploiting the symmetries of
L
. For details, see
Proposition

B.14
. Moreover, we will show in
Propositions

B.15
and
B.24
that
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
are Weyl elements with respect to
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
(under suitable assumptions in the case of
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
). This is one important step in proving that
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
is a
G_{2}
-grading of a subgroup of
\Aut(L)
.

Notation 5.1
.

For any linear cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
, we denote by
L(J,J^{\prime})
the corresponding Lie algebra constructed in
Proposition

4.27
. If no linear cubic norm pair is specified, we assume that
(J,J^{\prime})
is a fixed linear cubic norm pair and that
L\coloneq L(J,J^{\prime})
is the corresponding Lie algebra. Further, we will occasionally use the convention from
Remark

4.4
to identify
G_{2}^{0}
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Convention 5.2
.

We will always let automorphism of
L
act on
L
from the left.

Remark 5.3
.

We will define all homomorphisms on
L
by describing their action on all components. Since it is in general not true that the sum

\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in J,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle+k\zeta+k\xi

is direct, it is a priori not clear that these maps are well-defined on
L_{0,0}
. This will be verified in
Lemma

5.14
.

We begin with the functoriality of
L(J,J^{\prime})
with respect to surjective homotopies.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})
and
(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
be two linear cubic norm pairs (over the same ring
k
), let
t\in k
be invertible and let
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
be a surjective
t
-homotopy of linear cubic norm pairs. Then we define a map

L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)\colon L(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})\to L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime}),

denoted by
\psi
in the following set of equations, by setting

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq t^{-1}x,

\displaystyle\psi((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle\coloneq(t^{-1}\nu,t^{-1}c^{\iota},(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle\psi(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},

\displaystyle\psi(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c^{\iota},(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}},\quad\psi(\xi)\coloneq\xi,\quad\psi(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta,

\displaystyle\psi(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\iota},

\displaystyle\psi((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+})

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c^{\iota},t(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t\rho)_{+}

\displaystyle\psi(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq ty

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. If
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is a homomorphism (i.e., if
t=1
), we simply put
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\coloneq L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},1)
.

Remark 5.5
.

If
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)
is a
t
-involution for some invertible
t\in k
, then
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)^{-1}=L({\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},\iota,t^{-1})
.

Remark 5.6
.

The definition of
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
is not canonical: We made a choice
“where to put
t
and
t^{-1}
”
. Our choice corresponds to the
5
-grading
(L^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
from
Remark

4.3
in the sense that exactly the blocks of type
J
,
J^{\prime}
that lie in
L_{0}^{\prime\prime}
and the blocks of type
k
that lie in
L_{-1}^{\prime\prime}+L_{1}^{\prime\prime}
are multiplied by
t
or
t^{-1}
. In a similar way, we could for example define a map
\psi\coloneq L^{\prime}(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)\colon L(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})\to L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
that corresponds to the
5
-grading
(L_{i})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
by

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x,

\displaystyle\psi((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{\pm})

\displaystyle\coloneq(t\nu,c^{\iota},(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t^{-1}\rho)_{\pm},

\displaystyle\psi(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq t(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},

\displaystyle\psi(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c^{\iota},(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}},\quad\psi(\xi)\coloneq\xi,\quad\psi(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta,

\displaystyle\psi(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq t^{-1}c^{\iota},

\displaystyle\psi(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Note that
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)=L^{\prime}(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
if
t=1
, that is, if
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is a homomorphism. Further, the two maps agree on
L_{0,0}
for arbitrary
t
. The latter fact implies that the proof of well-definedness in
Lemma

5.14
is also valid for
L^{\prime}(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
.

Before we define the automorphism
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
, we formalize what we mean by
“reflections”
.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system, let
\alpha\in\Phi
and let

\bar{L}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\Phi^{0}}\bar{L}_{\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{L}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\Phi^{0}}\bar{L}_{\beta}^{\prime}

be two
\Phi
-graded Lie algebras. An
\alpha
-reflection from
\bar{L}
to
\bar{L}^{\prime}
is an isomorphism
\varphi\colon\bar{L}\to\bar{L}^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(\bar{L}_{\beta})=\bar{L}^{\prime}_{\beta^{\sigma(\alpha)}}
for all
\beta\in\Phi^{0}
where
\sigma_{\alpha}\colon\Phi^{0}\to\Phi^{0}
is the reflection along
\alpha^{\perp}
. The root
\alpha
will also sometimes be called the
type of
\varphi
. If
\bar{L}^{\prime}=\bar{L}
, we will simply call
\varphi
a
\alpha
-reflection on
\bar{L}
.

The defining formulas for the following reflections on
L
have been obtained by applying the formulas in
[
10
, Lemma 2.8]
to an
“extremal element”
with respect to the grading that should be reversed.

Definition 5.8
.

We define endomorphisms
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
of the
k
-module
L
by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq y,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}},\quad\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(\xi)\coloneq-\xi,\quad\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta-\xi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+})

\displaystyle\coloneq-(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq x,

and

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq(1,0,0,0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle\coloneq-\nu x+(0,c,0,0)_{-}+c^{\prime}-(\rho,0,0,0)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq-(0,0,c^{\prime},0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}+T(c,c^{\prime})(\xi-\zeta),\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\xi)\coloneq\zeta,\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\zeta)\coloneq\xi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq-(0,c,0,0)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+})

\displaystyle\coloneq-(0,0,0,\nu)_{-}+c+(0,0,c^{\prime},0)_{+}-\rho y,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,0,0,1)_{+}.

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
and
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Remark 5.9
.

In a similar way, we can define a third map
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,0,0,1)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle\coloneq-\rho x+(0,0,c^{\prime},0)_{-}+c+(0,0,0,\nu)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,0,c^{\prime},0)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}-T(c,c^{\prime})\zeta,\quad\varphi_{\updownarrow}(\xi)\coloneq\xi-\zeta,\quad\varphi_{\updownarrow}(\zeta)\coloneq-\zeta,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq-(0,c,0,0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+})

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,0,0,\rho)_{-}-c^{\prime}+(0,c,0,0)_{+}+\nu y,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\updownarrow}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq(-1,0,0,0)_{+}.

We introduce it here for completeness, but we will never use it in our arguments in a meaningful way. For this reason, we will not verify in detail that it is indeed compatible with the Lie bracket, as we do for
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
in
Lemma

5.15
.

We will also need the following reflection from
L(J,J^{\prime})
to
L(J^{\prime},J)
in
Definitions

5.18
and
B.13
.

Definition 5.10
.

We define a map
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\coloneq\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{(J,J^{\prime})}\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J^{\prime},J)
by setting

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,0,0,1)_{+},

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle\coloneq-\nu y+(0,0,-c,0)_{+}+c^{\prime}+(0,0,0,\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq\big(0,-c^{\prime},0,0\big)_{+},

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq-\mathbf{d}_{c^{\prime},c}+T(c,c^{\prime})(\zeta-\xi),\quad\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\xi)\coloneq-\zeta,\quad\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\zeta)\coloneq-\xi,

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,0,-c,0)_{-},

\displaystyle(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,0,0,0)_{+}+c+\big(0,-c^{\prime},0,0\big)_{-}-\rho x,

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq(1,0,0,0)_{-}

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Remark 5.11
.

It is immediate from
Definitions

5.10
,
5.8
and
5.9
that

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(L_{\alpha})=L_{\alpha^{\sigma((2,1))}},\quad\varphi_{\updownarrow}(L_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=L_{\alpha^{\sigma((1,2))}},\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(L_{\alpha})=L_{\alpha^{\sigma((-1,1))}},

\displaystyle\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{(J,J^{\prime})}\big(L(J,J^{\prime})_{\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=L(J^{\prime},J)_{\alpha^{\sigma((1,1))}}

for all
\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}
. Thus to show that these maps are reflections, it only remains to show that they are isomorphisms of Lie algebras.

Remark 5.12
.

It follows from
Remark

5.11
that

\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}(L_{i})=L_{-i},\qquad\varphi_{\updownarrow}(L_{i}^{\prime})=L_{-i}^{\prime},\qquad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(L_{i}^{\prime\prime})=L_{-i}^{\prime\prime}

for all
i\in\{-2,\ldots,2\}
. This means that the reflections
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
correspond to the
5
-gradings
(L_{i})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
,
(L_{i}^{\prime})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
and
(L_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}

defined in
Remark

4.3
, respectively. Similarly, for the
7
-gradings
(L(J,J^{\prime})^{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}_{i})_{-3\leq i\leq 3}
of
L(J,J^{\prime})
and
(L(J^{\prime},J)^{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}_{i})_{-3\leq i\leq 3}
of
L(J^{\prime},J)
from
Remark

4.3
, we have

\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{(J,J^{\prime})}\big(L(J,J^{\prime})^{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}_{i}\big)=L(J^{\prime},J)^{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}_{-i}

for all
i\in\{-3,\ldots,3\}
.

Remark 5.13
.

For all
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{\leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\updownarrow},\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}},\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\}
, the map
\varphi^{2}
is the parity automorphism of the grading to which
\varphi
corresponds. In particular,
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\updownarrow},\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
have order
4
if
2\neq 0
in
k
and order
2
if
2=0
in
k
.

We can now prove that all maps above are actually well-defined and homomorphisms of Lie algebras.

Lemma 5.14
.

(1)

For any linear cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
, the reflections
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
,
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
on
L(J,J^{\prime})
and the map
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J^{\prime},J)
are well-defined.

(2)

For any invertible
t\in k
and any surjective
t
-homotopy
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of linear cubic norm pairs, the map
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
is well-defined.

Proof.

In this proof, all summations are understood to run over
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Suppose that

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta+\lambda_{\xi}\xi=0

for certain, finitely many scalars
\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}},\lambda_{\zeta},\lambda_{\xi}\in k
. We have to show this sum evaluates to zero under the respective assignments in
Definitions

5.4
,
5.10
,
5.8
and
5.9
. We know from
Lemma

4.19
that

(15)

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\zeta-\xi),

(16)

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=3\lambda_{\xi},

(17)

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J},\qquad\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}=2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J^{\prime}}.

Both sides of (
15
) are fixed by the assignments defining
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
, so
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
is well-defined. Under the assignments defining
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
, they evaluate to

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})(\xi-\zeta)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\xi-\zeta)=\lambda_{\xi}(2\zeta-\xi)+3\lambda_{\xi}(\xi-\zeta).

By (
16
), we infer that
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
is well-defined. Similarly, the two sides of (
15
) are mapped to

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}-\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})\zeta=\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}-3\lambda_{\xi}\zeta\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(-2\zeta-\xi+\zeta)=\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)-3\lambda_{\xi}\zeta

under the assignments defining
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
, respectively. We infer that
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
is also well-defined.

For the proof that
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
is well-defined, we have to distinguish between the elements
\xi,\zeta,\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
of
L(J,J^{\prime})
and the elements
\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime},\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime},a}
of
L(J^{\prime},J)
. The two sides of (
15
) are mapped to

\displaystyle-\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime},a}+\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})(\zeta^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime})=-\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime},a}+3\lambda_{\xi}(\zeta^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(-2\xi^{\prime}+\zeta^{\prime})=-\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\zeta^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime})+3\lambda_{\xi}(\zeta^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime})

under the assignments defining
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
. By
Proposition

4.12
, (
16
) and (
17
), we have

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}[\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime},a},(\lambda,b^{\prime},b,\mu)_{\pm}]=\lambda_{\xi}(-3\lambda,-b^{\prime},b,3\mu)_{\pm}

for all
(\lambda,b^{\prime},b,\mu)_{\pm}\in L(J^{\prime},J)_{\pm 1}
and
\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime},a}(L(J^{\prime},J)_{\pm 2})=0
, so it follows from
Remark

4.6
that
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a^{\prime},a}=\lambda_{\xi}(2\zeta^{\prime}-\xi^{\prime})
. We infer that
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
is well-defined.

Now assume that
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
is a surjective
t
-homotopy of linear cubic norm pairs for some invertible
t\in k
. Similarly as in the previous paragraph, we have to distinguish between the elements
\xi,\zeta,\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
of
L(J,J^{\prime})
and the elements
\xi_{2},\zeta_{2},\mathbf{d}_{a^{\iota},(a^{\prime})^{{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}}}
of
L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
. Now the two sides of (
15
) are mapped to

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a^{\iota},(a^{\prime})^{{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{\xi}(2\zeta_{2}-\xi_{2})

under the assignments defining
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
. It follows from (
17
) and
Remark

2.13
that

\displaystyle\left(\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\iota},(a^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}}\right)(b^{\iota})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)\right)^{\iota}=2\lambda_{\xi}b^{\iota}

for all
b\in J
. Since
\iota
is surjective, we infer that
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\iota},(a^{\prime})^{{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}}}=2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J_{2}}
. By a similar computation,
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{(a^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},a^{\iota}}=2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J_{2}^{\prime}}
. Further, (
16
) implies that
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a^{\iota},(a^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})=3\lambda_{\xi}
. Thus by similar arguments as in the previous paragraph, it follows from
Propositions

4.12
and
4.6
that
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a^{\iota},(a^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}}=\lambda_{\xi}(2\zeta_{2}-\xi_{2})
. Hence
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
is well-defined.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

(1)

For any linear cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
, the maps
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
on
L(J,J^{\prime})
and the map
\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J^{\prime},J)
are isomorphisms of Lie algebras. In particular,
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
,
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
are reflections of type
(-2,-1)
,
(-1,-2)
and
(-1,1)
, respectively.

(2)

For any invertible
t\in k
and any surjective
t
-homotopy
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of linear cubic norm pairs, the map
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
is a surjective homomorphism of Lie algebras. It is bijective if and only if
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is bijective.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{\leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}},\varphi_{\updownarrow},\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\}
or
\varphi=L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
for a surjective
t
-homotopy
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
of linear cubic norm pairs and some invertible
t\in k
. The assertion about the surjectivity or bijectivity of
\varphi
are clear (see also
Remark

5.11
), so it remains to show that it is also compatible with the Lie bracket. For this it suffices to show that
\varphi([a,b])=[\varphi(a),\varphi(b)]
for all
a,b
in a generating set of the
k
-module
L
. By the anti-symmetry of the Lie bracket, we only have to check this for all
a<b
where
<
is an arbitrarily chosen total order on the generating set. The results of these computations for the
“standard”
generating set can be found in
Table

4
for
\varphi=\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
, in
Table

5
for
\varphi=\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
and in
Table

6
for
\varphi=\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
. The majority of computations are straightforward applications of the formulas in
Table

1
, but we also have to use
Lemma

3.3
to prove the relation
\varphi([\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}])=[\varphi(\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}),\varphi(\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}})]
for
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}},\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\}
. A similar computation can be done for
\varphi_{\updownarrow}
, but we omit the details (see
Remark

5.9
). For
\varphi=L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
, it is a straightforward verification that it is compatible with the Lie bracket.

The final assertion in
Item

1
follows from
Remark

5.12
.
∎

As announced, the functoriality of our construction has the following first application.

Proposition 5.16
.

The isomorphism type of
L(J,J^{\prime})
depends only on the isotopy type of
(J,J^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
be a
t
-isotopy of linear cubic norm pairs for some invertible
t\in k
. Then the map
L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism of Lie algebras by
Lemma

5.15
Item

2
.
∎

Remark 5.17
.

We state explicit formulas for the inverses of
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
for future reference: We have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(x)

\displaystyle=y,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle=-(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=c^{\prime},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}},\quad\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(\xi)=-\xi,\quad\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(\zeta)=\zeta-\xi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(c)

\displaystyle=c,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+})

\displaystyle=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle=x,

and

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(x)

\displaystyle=(-1,0,0,0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle=\nu x+(0,c,0,0)_{-}-c^{\prime}-(\rho,0,0,0)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(0,0,c^{\prime},0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}+T(c,c^{\prime})(\xi-\zeta),\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(\xi)=\zeta,\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(\zeta)=\xi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(c)

\displaystyle=(0,c,0,0)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+})

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,-\nu)_{-}-c+(0,0,c^{\prime},0)_{+}+\rho y,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,-1)_{+}.

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
and
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

We now define the final reflection
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
. Since it is a composition of maps defined earlier, we see immediately that it is a reflection.

Definition 5.18
.

Let
t\in k
be invertible and assume that
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)
is a
t
-isotopy of linear cubic norm pairs.
Then we define a fourth reflection
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)}\coloneq\psi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{(J^{\prime},J)}\circ L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)
, which is a
(-1,-1)
-reflection on
L(J,J^{\prime})
.

Remark 5.19
.

Let
t\in k
be invertible, let
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)
be a
t
-isotopy of linear cubic norm pairs and put
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\coloneq\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)}
. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(x)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,t^{-1})_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-})

\displaystyle=-\nu t^{-1}y+(0,0,-t^{-1}c^{\iota},0)_{+}+(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}+(0,0,0,\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\big(0,-(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},0,0\big)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{d}_{(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},c^{\iota}}+T(c,c^{\prime})(\zeta-\xi),\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\xi)=-\zeta,\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\zeta)=-\xi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(c)

\displaystyle=(0,0,-c^{\iota},0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}

\displaystyle=(\nu,0,0,0)_{+}+c^{\iota}+\big(0,-t(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},0,0\big)_{-}-t\rho x,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}(y)

\displaystyle=(t,0,0,0)_{-}

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Now assume in addition that
\iota^{-1}={\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}
. Then the inverse of
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(x)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,-t^{-1})_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\big)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\nu y+(0,0,-t^{-1}c^{\iota},0)_{+}-(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}+(0,0,0,\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\big(0,(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},0,0\big)_{+},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{d}_{(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},c^{\iota}}+T(c,c^{\prime})(\zeta-\xi),\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(\xi)=-\zeta,\quad\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(\zeta)=-\xi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(c)

\displaystyle=(0,0,c^{\iota},0)_{-},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle=(\nu,0,0,0)_{+}-c^{\iota}+\big(0,-t(c^{\prime})^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},0,0\big)_{-}+t\rho x,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle=(-t,0,0,0)_{-}

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Further,
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{2}
is the parity automorphism of the
7
-grading of
L
defined in
Remark

5.12
, which coincides with the parity automorphism
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{45.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{2}
of the
5
-grading
(L_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{-2\leq i\leq 2}
.

6.
The ideal structure of
L

In this section,
(J,J^{\prime})
denotes an arbitrary cubic norm pair (or, more generally, a linear cubic norm pair) over some commutative ring
k
and
L\coloneq L(J,J^{\prime})
denotes the corresponding Lie algebra. Our goal is to classify the ideals of
L
(
Propositions

6.24
and
6.23
). Namely, we will show that modulo
L_{0,0}
, ideals of
L
correspond bijectively to what we call ideals of the triple
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. In particular, we will see that
L
is simple if and only if
k
is a field and
T
is non-degenerate (
Theorem

6.25
). This section is independent of the following sections.

As a first step, we show that every ideal of
L
is graded (
Proposition

6.12
).

Notation 6.1
.

In this section, we denote by
\pi_{\alpha}\colon L\to L_{\alpha}
the natural projection map for all
\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}
. Further, we will write
\alpha\sim\beta
for
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
if they lie in the same of the three orbits in
G_{2}
described in
Remark

4.5
.

Definition 6.2
.

An ideal
I
of
L
is called
graded
if
I=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}(I\cap L_{\alpha})
, or equivalently, if
\pi_{\alpha}(I)\subseteq I
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}
.

Convention 6.3
.

In this section, we will always distinguish between
k
,
J
and
J^{\prime}
even though they could coincide in certain special cases. With this convention, we have
a\sim\beta
(for
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
) if and only if
L_{\alpha}
and
L_{\beta}
are (canonically) parametrized by the same of the three structures
k
,
J
,
J^{\prime}
.

The crucial ingredient in the proof of
Proposition

6.12
will be
Lemma

6.7
, which says that the Lie bracket induces
“natural”
isomorphisms between root spaces of
L
. We first need to make this notion precise.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\omega\colon G_{2}^{0}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be as in
Remark

4.4
. Then for all
(i,j)\in\omega(G_{2})
, we define
P_{(i,j)}=P_{ij}\in\{k,J,J^{\prime}\}
to be the additive group to which
L_{i,j}
(as defined in
Definitions

4.1
and
4.9
) is naturally isomorphic (as can be read off from
Fig.

1
). Further, the
root homomorphism with respect to
(i,j)
is the
“canonical”
isomorphism
\vartheta_{i,j}\colon P_{i,j}\to L_{i,j}
. For example,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{-2,-1}(t)

\displaystyle=tx,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{-1,-2}(t)

\displaystyle=(t,0,0,0)_{-},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1,2}(t)

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,t)_{+},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2,1}(t)

\displaystyle=ty,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0,-1}(b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=b^{\prime},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{-1,-1}(b)

\displaystyle=(0,b,0,0)_{-},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{1,1}(b^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(0,0,b^{\prime},0)_{+},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{0,1}(b)

\displaystyle=b

for all
t\in k
,
b\in J
and
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. We will in this section also refer to the isomorphisms
k\to k\rho\colon\lambda\to\lambda\rho
for
\rho\in\{\xi,\zeta\}
as
“canonical”
.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
such that
\alpha\sim\beta
. An isomorphism
\varphi\colon L_{\alpha}\to L_{\beta}
of
k
-modules is called
natural
if it is compatible with the root homomorphisms
\vartheta_{\alpha}
and
\vartheta_{\beta}
from
Definition

6.4
, meaning that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\alpha}=P_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{\beta}}

commutes. Similarly, an isomorphism between
L_{\gamma}
for
\gamma\in G_{2}
long and
k\rho
for
\rho\in\{\xi,\zeta\}
is called
natural
if it is compatible with
\vartheta_{\gamma}
and the
“canonical”
map
k\to k\rho\colon\lambda\mapsto\lambda\rho
, and an isomorphism between
k\xi
and
k\zeta
is called
natural
if it interchanges
\xi
and
\zeta
.

Remark 6.6
.

Note that there exists exactly one natural isomorphism from
L_{\alpha}
to
L_{\beta}
, namely
\vartheta_{\beta}\circ\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}
, and it is the identity map if
\alpha=\beta
. However, we will be interested in
k
-module endomorphisms of
L
that restrict to a natural isomorphism on certain root subspaces, and there may be multiple ways to extend a natural isomorphism between two root spaces to such an endomorphism.

In the following, we will use the convention from
Remark

4.4
to identify
G_{2}^{0}
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, so that we have root subspaces
(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}
and corresponding parametrising spaces
(P_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
such that
\alpha\sim\beta
. Then there exist
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\in G_{2}
and
a_{1}\in L_{\gamma_{1}},\ldots,a_{n}\in L_{\gamma_{n}}
such that
\ad(a_{1})\circ\cdots\circ\ad(a_{n})
induces a natural isomorphism from
L_{\alpha}
to
L_{\beta}
. Further, if
\alpha
is long and
\rho\in\{\xi,\zeta\}
, then there exist
b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}\in L
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{p}\in L
such that

\ad(b_{1})\circ\cdots\circ\ad(b_{m})\quad\text{and}\quad\ad(c_{1})\circ\cdots\circ\ad(c_{p})

induce natural isomorphisms from
L_{\alpha}
to
k\rho
and from
k\rho
to
L_{\alpha}
, respectively.

Proof.

In the following, an arrow labeled by an element
a\in L
stands for the
k
-linear homomorphism
\ad_{a}\colon L\to L\colon b\mapsto[a,b]
. On the root spaces (canonically) parametrized by
J
, we have a chain of isomorphisms
