{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,0,1)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1,0,0,0)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-x}

that are compatible with the root homomorphisms. On the root spaces (canonically) parametrized by
J^{\prime}
, we have a similar chain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0,-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0,0,0)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0,0,0)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-x}

On the long root spaces, the isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-2,-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1,-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{2,-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0,0,0)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,0,1)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,0,-1)_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1,0,0,0)_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-x}

are compatible with the root homomorphisms. Finally, the isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-2,-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1,-1},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-1,-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\zeta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1,0,0,0)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0,0,-1)_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-y}

are compatible with the root homomorphisms and with the
“canonical”
isomorphisms
\lambda\mapsto\lambda\xi
,
\lambda\mapsto\lambda\zeta
. By composing a suitable sequence of the previous isomorphisms, the assertion follows.
∎

Notation 6.8
.

For all
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
such that
\alpha\sim\beta
, we fix a
k
-module endomorphism
\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}\colon L\to L
that induces a natural isomorphism
L_{\alpha}\to L_{\beta}
and that is of the form
\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}=\ad(a_{1})\circ\cdots\circ\ad(a_{n})
for suitable chosen root space elements
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in L
. Note that
\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}
leaves all ideals of
L
invariant and satisfies
\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}(L_{\gamma})\subseteq L_{\gamma+\beta-\alpha}
for all
\gamma\in G_{2}^{0}
. We also choose maps
\varphi_{\alpha\to\rho}
,
\varphi_{\rho\to\alpha}
for
\alpha\in G_{2}
long and
\rho\in\{\xi,\zeta\}
and maps
\varphi_{\xi\to\zeta}
,
\varphi_{\zeta\to\xi}
in a similar way.

Remark 6.9
.

Let
\gamma\in G_{2}^{0}
and let
\psi_{\gamma}
be a
k
-module endomorphism of
L
such that
\psi_{\gamma}(L_{\alpha})\subseteq L_{\alpha+\gamma}
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}
. Then
\pi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\gamma}=\psi_{\gamma}\circ\pi_{\alpha-\gamma}
for all and
\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}
. In particular,
\pi_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\beta\to\alpha}=\varphi_{\beta\to\alpha}\circ\pi_{\beta}
and hence
\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}\circ\pi_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\beta\to\alpha}=\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}\circ\varphi_{\beta\to\alpha}\pi_{\beta}=\pi_{\beta}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}^{0}
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\alpha
be a long root in
G_{2}
. Then there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in L
such that
\ad(a_{1})\circ\cdots\circ\ad(a_{n})=\pi_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Since
\gamma-2\alpha\notin G_{2}^{0}
for all
\gamma\in G_{2}^{0}\setminus\{\alpha\}
(as can be seen in
Fig.

1
),
\varphi_{\alpha\to-\alpha}
maps all root spaces except for
L_{\alpha}
to zero. Hence
\varphi_{-\alpha\to\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\alpha\to-\alpha}
maps all root spaces except for
L_{\alpha}
to zero, and it is the identity map on
L_{\alpha}
(by
Remark

6.6
). Thus
\varphi_{-\alpha\to\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\alpha\to-\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}
.
∎

It is not possible to realize the projections to short root spaces as in
Proposition

6.10
: We only obtain the following result because we cannot annihilate the
(0,0)
-part of
L
in this way.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
I
be an ideal of
L
and let
b\in I
with
\pi_{(0,0)}(b)=0
. Then
\pi_{\alpha}(b)\in I
for all short roots
\alpha\in G_{2}
.

Proof.

For all long roots
\delta\in G_{2}
, we have
\pi_{\delta}(b)\in I
by
Proposition

6.10
, and hence we may assume that
\pi_{\delta}(b)=0
.
Let
\alpha\in G_{2}
be short and let
\beta,\gamma\in G_{2}
be the two short roots such that
\alpha\sim\beta\sim\gamma
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
are pairwise distinct.
Then

\varphi\coloneq\varphi_{\gamma\to\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\beta\to\gamma}\circ\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}

is the identity map on
L_{\alpha}
and, because each factor shifts the grading, sends all other root spaces to zero except (possibly) for
L_{0,0}
and some long root spaces. Hence
\pi_{\alpha}(b)
is the only non-zero component of
b
on which
\varphi
is non-zero, so

\displaystyle\varphi(b)

\displaystyle=\varphi\big(\pi_{\alpha}(b)\big)=\pi_{\alpha}(b).

Since
\varphi(b)\in I
by construction of
\varphi
, the assertion follows.
∎

Proposition 6.12
.

Every ideal of
L
is graded.

Proof.

Let
I
be an ideal of
L
and let
a\in I
. We have to show that
\pi_{\alpha}(a)\in I
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}
. For all long
\alpha\in G_{2}
, this holds by
Proposition

6.10
, so we may assume that
\pi_{\alpha}(a)=0
for such
\alpha
.
Put
a_{0}\coloneq\pi_{(0,0)}(a)\in L_{0,0}
and write
a_{0}=a_{0}^{\prime}+\lambda\xi+\mu\zeta
where
\lambda,\mu\in k
and
a_{0}^{\prime}\in\langle\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}\mid b\in J,b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\rangle
. (Note that this expression is, in general, not unique.) Since the root
(2,1)
is long and
\ad_{x}(a)\in I
, we have
\pi_{(2,1)}(\ad_{x}(a))\in I
. Here, by
Remark

6.9
,

\pi_{(2,1)}\big(\ad_{x}(a)\big)=\ad_{x}\big(\pi_{(0,0)}(a)\big)=\ad_{x}(a_{0}^{\prime}+\lambda\xi+\mu\zeta)=(2\lambda+\mu)x.

Thus
(2\lambda+\mu)x\in I
and hence, by
Lemma

6.7
,
(2\lambda+\mu)\zeta\in I
. By subtracting this element from
a
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mu=-2\lambda
, so that
a_{0}=a_{0}^{\prime}+\lambda(\xi-2\zeta)
. Thus
\ad_{y}(a_{0})=0
. This implies, by
Remark

6.9
, that

\displaystyle\pi_{(0,0)}\big(\ad_{-x}\circ\ad_{y}(a)\big)

\displaystyle=\ad_{-x}\circ\ad_{y}\big(\pi_{(0,0)}(a)\big)=\ad_{-x}\big(\ad_{y}(a_{0})\big)=0.

Hence
b\coloneq\ad_{-x}\circ\ad_{y}(a)
satisfies the requirements of
Lemma

6.11
. Thus for all short
\alpha\in G_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\ad_{-x}\circ\ad_{y}\big(\pi_{\alpha}(a)\big)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\alpha}\big(\ad_{-x}\circ\ad_{y}(a)\big)=\pi_{\alpha}(b)\in I.

Since
\ad_{-x}\circ\ad_{y}
is the identity map on
L_{1}
, we infer that
\pi_{\alpha}(a)\in I
for
\alpha\in\{(1,0),(1,1)\}
. In other words, we have proven that
\pi_{\alpha}(I)\subseteq I
for
\alpha\in\{(1,0),(1,1)\}
.

Now let
\beta\in G_{2}
be an arbitrary short root. Then
\beta\sim\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\{(1,0),(1,1)\}
and hence, by
Remark

6.9
,

\pi_{\beta}(I)=\varphi_{\alpha\to\beta}\circ\pi_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\beta\to\alpha}(I)\subseteq I.

Altogether, we have shown that
\pi_{\gamma}(I)\subseteq I
for all
\gamma\in G_{2}
. Since

\pi_{(0,0)}=\id_{L}-\sum_{\gamma\in G_{2}}\pi_{\gamma},

it follows that
\pi_{(0,0)}(I)\subseteq I
as well. Therefore,
I
is graded.
∎

With
Proposition

6.12
proven, we can now begin to associate ideals of
L
to substructures of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. The closure condition of ideals together with
Table

1
motivates the following definition.

Definition 6.13
.

An
ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
is a triple
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
with the following properties.

(1)

\ell
is an ideal of the ring
k
and
U
,
U^{\prime}
are
k
-submodules of
J
,
J^{\prime}
, respectively.

(2)

\ell J\subseteq U
and
\ell J^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
.

(3)

T(u,b^{\prime})\in\ell
for all
u\in U
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
T(b,u^{\prime})\in\ell
for all
u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
,
b\in J
.

(4)

u\times b\in U^{\prime}
for all
u\in U
,
b\in J
and
u^{\prime}\times b^{\prime}\in U
for all
u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Example 6.14
.

Let
\ell
be an ideal of
k
. Then
(\ell,\ell J,\ell J^{\prime})
is an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. In particular,
(0,0,0)
and
(k,J,J^{\prime})
are ideals of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
, called the
trivial ideals
.

Example 6.15
.

Let

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\coloneq\{u\in J\mid T(u,a^{\prime})=0\text{ for all }a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{u^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\mid T(a,u^{\prime})=0\text{ for all }a\in J\}

denote the
left radical of
T
and the
right radical of
T
, respectively.
Then for any ideal
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
, it follows from
Lemma

2.8
Item

7
that
(\ell,U+R,U^{\prime}+R^{\prime})
is an ideal as well. In particular,
(\{0\},R,R^{\prime})
is an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. Further, if
T=0
, then
(\ell,J,J^{\prime})
is an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
for all ideals
\ell
of
k
because then
R=J
and
R^{\prime}=J^{\prime}
.

Remark 6.16
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. Then by
Definition

2.7
,

D_{b,b^{\prime}}(u),D_{u,b^{\prime}}(b),D_{b,u^{\prime}}(c)\in U\quad\text{and}\quad D_{b^{\prime},b}(u^{\prime}),D_{u^{\prime},b}(b^{\prime}),D_{b^{\prime},u}(c^{\prime})\in U

for all
b,c\in J
,
b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
,
u\in U
and
u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
.

Remark 6.17
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. Then
(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})
is a linear cubic norm pair over the ring
k/\ell
with induced natural maps
\bar{T}
and
\bar{\times}
.
2
2

2

Note that, even if
(J,J^{\prime})
is a proper cubic norm pair, then
(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})
need not have the structure of a proper cubic norm pair because the definition of ideals of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
does not refer to
N
and
\sharp
at all. For this reason, it might be more appropriate to refer to
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
as a
linear ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
, but we will not do so.
Hence we have an associated Lie algebra
L(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})
over
k/\ell
. Note that
L(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})
may also be regarded as a Lie algebra over
k
whereas
(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})
cannot be regarded as a linear cubic norm pair over
k
: It is in general not possible to lift the trace
\bar{T}\colon J/U\times J^{\prime}/U^{\prime}\to\ell
to a map with image in
k
such that the defining identities of linear cubic norm pairs are satisfied. Further, the triple

(\pi_{k},\pi_{J},\pi_{J^{\prime}})\colon(k,J,J^{\prime})\to(k/\ell,J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})

is a
homomorphism of linear cubic norm pairs with base ring
in the sense that

\bar{T}\big(\pi_{J}(a),\pi_{J^{\prime}}(a^{\prime})\big)=\pi_{k}\big(T(a,a^{\prime})\big)

for all
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
\pi_{J}
,
\pi_{J^{\prime}}
are compatible with
\times
,
\bar{\times}
(as in
Definition

2.24
with
t=1
). This triple induces a homomorphism

L(\pi_{k},\pi_{J},\pi_{J^{\prime}})\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})

of Lie algebras over
k
that is defined just as in
Definition

5.4
(with
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}},t)=(\pi_{J},\pi_{J^{\prime}},1)
), except that
(\nu,0,0,\rho)_{\pm}
is mapped to
(\pi_{k}(\nu),c,c^{\prime},\pi_{k}(\rho))
for
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. This homomorphism of Lie algebras is surjective by construction. In the following, when the ideal
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
is clear from the context, we will denote
L(\pi_{k},\pi_{J},\pi_{J^{\prime}})
as well as the epimorphisms
\pi_{k}\colon k\to k/\ell
,
\pi_{J}\colon J\to J/U
and
\pi_{J^{\prime}}\colon J^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}/U^{\prime}
by
a\mapsto\bar{a}
.

We would like to associate to each ideal
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
an ideal
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
of
L
. While
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
canonically prescribes what
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})\cap L_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in G_{2}
should be, the same is not clear for
\alpha=(0,0)
. Indeed, we will define a minimal and a maximal ideal corresponding to
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
and show that every ideal of
L
is sandwiched between such a pair.

The following definition is motivated by
Lemma

4.19
.

Definition 6.18
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. The
radical of
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
is the
k
-submodule
\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
of
L_{0,0}
consisting of all elements of the form

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\xi}\xi+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta

where the sum runs over finitely many
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}},\lambda\in k
, such that the following properties are satisfied.

(1)

\lambda_{\zeta}+2\lambda_{\xi}\in\ell
.

(2)

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}T(a,a^{\prime})\equiv 3\lambda_{\xi}
modulo
\ell
.

(3)

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}\equiv 2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J}
modulo
U
. That is, the image of
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}-2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J}
is contained in
U
.

(4)

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}\equiv 2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J^{\prime}}
modulo
U^{\prime}
. That is, the image of
\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}-2\lambda_{\xi}\id_{J^{\prime}}
is contained in
U^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.19
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. Then we define the following subsets of
L
:

\displaystyle L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in G_{2}\text{ long}}\vartheta_{\alpha}(\ell)\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\sim(1,0)}\vartheta_{\alpha}(U)\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\sim(1,1)}\vartheta_{\alpha}(U^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\oplus\big(\ell\xi+\ell\zeta+\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in U,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\text{ or }a\in J,a^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}\rangle\big),

\displaystyle L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in G_{2}\text{ long}}\vartheta_{\alpha}(\ell)\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\sim(1,0)}\vartheta_{\alpha}(U)\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\sim(1,1)}\vartheta_{\alpha}(U^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\oplus\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime}).

Further, we put
L_{i}(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\sigma}\coloneq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\sigma}\cap L_{i}
for all
i\in\{-2,\ldots,2\}
and
\sigma\in\{\mathrm{min},\mathrm{max}\}
.

Remark 6.20
.

It is straightforward to check that
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
is contained in
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
.

Before we can turn to the classification of ideals of
L
, we need two alternative characterizations of the radical.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. Then the kernel of the epimorphism
\pi\colon L(J,J^{\prime})\to L(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})
from
Remark

6.17
is precisely
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
. In particular,
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
is an ideal of
L
and
L(\{0\},\{0\},\{0\})_{\mathrm{max}}=0
.

Proof.

Let
K
denote the kernel of
\pi
. By
Proposition

6.12
,
K=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}\pi_{\alpha}(K)
. By the definition of
\pi
, we have
\pi_{\alpha}(K)=\pi_{\alpha}(L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}})
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
. Now let
f\in L_{0,0}
be arbitrary and write

f=\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\xi}\xi+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta

where the sum runs over finitely many
a\in J
,
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}},\lambda\in k
. Then

\bar{f}=\sum\overline{\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}}\mathbf{d}_{\bar{a},\overline{a^{\prime}}}+\overline{\lambda_{\xi}}\bar{\xi}+\overline{\lambda_{\zeta}}\bar{\zeta}\in L(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime}).

By
Lemma

4.19
, we have
\bar{f}=0
if and only if
f\in\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
. The assertion follows.
∎

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
and let
\sigma\in\{\mathrm{min},\mathrm{max}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\{f\in L_{0,0}\mid[f,L]\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\sigma}\}

\displaystyle=\{f\in L_{0,0}\mid[f,L_{i}]\subseteq L_{i}(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\sigma}\text{ for }i\in\{-2,-1,1,1\}\}.

Proof.

Let

f=\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\lambda_{\xi}\xi+\lambda_{\zeta}\zeta

denote an arbitrary element of
L_{0,0}
. We have straightforward implications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[f,L]\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[f,L_{i}]\subseteq L_{i}(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\text{ for }i\in\{-2,-1,1,1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[f,L]\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[f,L_{i}]\subseteq L_{i}(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}\text{ for }i\in\{-2,-1,1,1\}}}

Further, by
Lemma

6.21
, we have
f\in\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
if and only if
\bar{f}=0
. By the definition of the 0-part of the Lie algebra, this is equivalent to the condition
[\bar{f},L_{i}(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})]=0
for all
i\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}
. Here

\displaystyle[\bar{f},L_{i}(J/U,J^{\prime}/U^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=[\bar{f},\overline{L_{i}}]=\overline{[f,L_{i}]}.

Thus
f\in\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
if and only if
[f,L_{i}]\subseteq\ker(\pi)=L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
for all
i\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}
. Hence

\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})=\{f\in L_{0,0}\mid[f,L_{i}]\subseteq L_{i}(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}\text{ for }i\in\{-2,-1,1,1\}\}.

Now assume that
f\in\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
. We must show that
[f,L]\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
. Since
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\cap L_{\alpha}=L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}\cap L_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
, it remains to show that
[f,L_{0,0}]\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
. Let
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
be arbitrary. Then, by the same computation as in
Corollary

4.13
,

\displaystyle[f,\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}\big(\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c),c^{\prime}\big)-\mathbf{d}\big(c,\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime})\big).

Here, by the definition of the radical,

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c)\in 2c+U\quad\text{and}\quad\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}D_{a^{\prime},a}(c^{\prime})\in 2c^{\prime}+U^{\prime}.

Hence
[f,\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}]\in\mathbf{d}(U,c^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(c,U^{\prime})\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
. Since also
[f,\xi]=0=[f,\zeta]
, we infer that
[f,L_{0,0}]\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
and let
V
be a
k
-submodule of
\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
. Then
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}+V
is an ideal of
L
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.22
, it suffices to show that
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
is an ideal of
L
. This follows from an inspection of
Table

1
, using the properties of ideals in
Definitions

6.13
and
6.16
.
∎

Proposition 6.24
.

Let
I
be an ideal of
L
. Then there is a unique ideal
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
such that

L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\subseteq I\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}.

Further, there exists a unique
k
-submodule
V
of
L_{0,0}
with

\big(\ell\xi+\ell\zeta+\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in U,a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\text{ or }a\in J,a^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}\rangle\big)\subseteq V\subseteq\Rad(\ell,U,U^{\prime})

such that
I=L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}+V
.

Proof.

Choose a long root
\alpha\in G_{2}
and short roots
\beta,\gamma\in G_{2}
with
\beta\sim(1,0)
and
\gamma\sim(1,1)
. We define subgroups
\ell
,
U
,
U^{\prime}
of
k
,
J
,
J^{\prime}
, respectively, by the property that

(18)

\vartheta_{\alpha}(\ell)=\pi_{\alpha}(I),\quad\vartheta_{\beta}(U)=\pi_{\beta}(U),\quad\vartheta_{\gamma}(U^{\prime})=\pi_{\gamma}(U^{\prime}).

By
Lemma

6.7
and because
I
is an ideal,
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
does not depend on the choice of
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
. Hence

I\cap L_{\alpha}=L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\cap L_{\alpha}=L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}\cap L_{\alpha}

for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
. Further,
\ell\xi=\varphi_{\alpha\to\xi}(\pi_{\alpha}(I))\subseteq I
and
\ell\zeta=\varphi_{\alpha\to\zeta}(\pi_{\alpha}(I))\subseteq I
. Now let
a\in U
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Then
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}=[a^{\prime},a]\in[L,I]\subseteq I
. Similarly, if
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
, then also
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\in I
. We conclude that
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\subseteq I
. Since

\displaystyle[V,L_{i}]\subseteq[I\cap L_{0},L_{i}]\subseteq I\cap L_{i}=L_{i}(\ell,U,U^{\prime})

for all
i\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}
, it follows from
Lemma

6.22
that
V\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
and hence

L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\subseteq I\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}.

Note that
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
is uniquely determined by this property because it must satisfy
Eq.

18
. Further, since
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
and
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
differ only in the
(0,0)
-component, there exists
V
as in the assertion such that
I=L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}+V
, and it is uniquely determined by
V=\pi_{(0,0)}(I)
.
∎

Proposition

6.24
has some straightforward consequences.

Theorem 6.25
.

Let
(J,J^{\prime})
be a cubic norm pair (or, more generally, a linear cubic norm pair) over a commutative ring
k
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

(k,J,J^{\prime})
is simple in the sense that it has only the trivial ideals
(0,0,0)
and
(k,J,J^{\prime})
.

(2)

L
is simple in the sense that it has only the trivial ideals
\{0\}
and
L
.

(3)

k
is simple (and hence a field) and
T
is non-degenerate (in the sense that the left and right radicals defined in
Example

6.15
are zero).

Proof.

First note that
L(0,0,0)_{\mathrm{max}}=0
by
Lemma

6.21
and
L(k,J,J^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}=L
. Thus it follows from
Proposition

6.24
that if
(k,J,J^{\prime})
is simple, then
L
is simple. Conversely, if
(k,J,J^{\prime})
has a non-trivial ideal
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
, then
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}
and
L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}
are non-trivial ideals of
L
. This proves the equivalence of the first two statements.

Assume that
k
is simple (and hence, being commutative, a field) and
T
is non-degenerate and let
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
be an ideal of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
. If
\ell=0
, then
T(u,b^{\prime})=0
for all
u\in U
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, so
U=0
because
T
is non-degenerate. Similarly,
U^{\prime}=0
in this case. If, however,
\ell=k
, then
U=J
and
U^{\prime}=J^{\prime}
because
\ell J\subseteq U
and
\ell J^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
.

Now assume that
(k,J,J^{\prime})
is simple. Since any ideal
\ell
of
k
induces an ideal
(\ell,\ell J,\ell J^{\prime})
of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
by
Example

6.14
,
k
must be simple. Further, since the radicals
R
,
R^{\prime}
of
T
induce an ideal
(0,R,R^{\prime})
of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
by
Example

6.15
,
T
must be non-degenerate.
∎

Remark 6.26
.

In
Section

9
, we will see that cubic Jordan matrix algebras over composition algebras over fields provide examples of pairs
(k,J,J^{\prime})
which satisfy the properties in
Theorem

6.25
. See
Remark

9.9
for details. Further, we remark that ideals of cubic Jordan matrix algebras (but not of the corresponding triples
(k,J,J)
) are classified in
[
17
, 37.26]
.

Lemma 6.27
.

The center of
L
is trivial.

Proof.

Since the center
Z(L)
is an ideal, there exists an ideal
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})
of
(k,J,J^{\prime})
such that

L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{min}}\subseteq Z(L)\subseteq L(\ell,U,U^{\prime})_{\mathrm{max}}.

However, it follows from
Lemma

6.7
that
Z(L)\cap L_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
. Thus
(\ell,U,U^{\prime})=(0,0,0)
. Since
L(0,0,0)_{\mathrm{max}}=0
by
Lemma

6.21
, it follows that
Z(L)=0
.
∎

7.
Exponential maps for the
G_{2}
-grading

We are now ready to pass from root graded Lie algebras to root graded groups by
“exponentiating”
the appropriate adjoint maps in the Lie algebra. However, since
2
and
3
are not necessarily invertible in the base ring
k
, we cannot define the exponential maps by the usual formula
\exp(a)\coloneq\sum_{i=0}^{4}\frac{1}{i!}\ad_{a}^{i}
(where it is easy to see from the grading that
\ad_{a}^{i}=0
for all
i\geq 5
). Instead, we will replace each term
\frac{1}{i!}\ad_{a}^{i}
by an appropriate endomorphism
\theta^{[i]}_{a}
of the Lie algebra, which coincides with
\frac{1}{i!}\ad_{a}^{i}
if
2
and
3
happen to be invertible in
k
. The definition of
\theta^{[i]}_{a}
involves the higher degree maps
N
and
\sharp
, so from now on and for the rest of this paper, we have to assume that
(J,J^{\prime})
is a cubic norm pair in the proper sense and not merely a linear cubic norm pair.

Since the exponential maps are defined by distinct, explicit formulas for each root
\alpha\in G_{2}
, we have to prove many basic properties
“by hand”
that would, over base rings in which
2
and
3
are invertible, follow from the expression
\exp(a)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i!}\ad_{a}^{i}
. Most importantly, this concerns the property that exponential automorphisms are indeed compatible with the Lie bracket, which we prove in
Appendix

A
using what we call
“higher Leibniz rules”
. Still, some properties can be established in a more abstract way, using the framework of
\alpha
-parametrizations that we introduce and investigate in
Section

7.1
. In
Section

7.2
, we construct the specific
\alpha
-parametrizations in
L(J,J^{\prime})
that we will be interested in, and we verify that they satisfy certain commutator formulas in
Section

7.3
. Further, we prove by a long but straightforward computation in
Appendix

B
that the reflections
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
from
Section

5
are Weyl elements if
J
contains an element with invertible norm, so we conclude that for every cubic norm structure
J
we have a
G_{2}
-graded group in
\Aut(L(J,J))
.

7.1.
General properties of exponential maps

In this subsection, we denote by
\Phi
an arbitrary (finite reduced crystallographic) root system and by
\bar{L}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\Phi^{0}}\bar{L}_{\beta}
a
\Phi
-graded Lie algebra over some commutative base ring
k
.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi
.
An
\alpha
-parametrization
is a map

\exp_{\alpha}\colon\bar{L}_{\alpha}\to\Aut(\bar{L})\colon a\mapsto e_{a}

of the form

e_{a}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\theta^{[i]}_{a},

where each
\theta^{[i]}_{a}
is a
k
-module endomorphism of
\bar{L}
, satisfying:

(1)

\theta^{[i]}_{a}(\bar{L}_{\beta})\leq\bar{L}_{\beta+i\alpha}
for all
\beta\in\Phi^{0}
,

(2)

\theta^{[0]}_{a}=\id
and
\theta^{[1]}_{a}=\ad_{a}
,

(3)

\theta^{[i]}_{\lambda a}=\lambda^{i}\theta^{[i]}_{a}
,

(4)

e_{\alpha}(a+b)=e_{\alpha}(a)e_{\alpha}(b)
,

for all
a,b\in L_{\alpha}
and all
\lambda\in k
.
The automorphisms in the image of
e_{\alpha}
are called
\alpha
-exponential automorphisms
.
By
Item

4
, the image
e_{\alpha}(\bar{L}_{\alpha})
forms an abelian group, which we will call a
root group
(corresponding to the root
\alpha
).

Remark 7.2
.

(1)

Contrary to what our notation might suggest, an
\alpha
-parametrization is typically not unique, even when
k
is, for example, a field of characteristic
0
. See also
[
10
, Theorem 3.13(v)]
.

(2)

If
\exp_{\alpha}\colon\bar{L}_{\alpha}\to\Aut(\bar{L})\colon a\mapsto e_{a}
is an
\alpha
-parametrization, then the
k
-linear endomorphisms
\theta^{[i]}_{a}
of
\bar{L}
satisfying
e_{a}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\theta^{[i]}_{a}
for all
a\in\bar{L}_{\alpha}
are uniquely determined by
\exp_{\alpha}
.

(3)

Let
\exp_{\alpha}
be an
\alpha
-parametrization, let
1\neq t\in k
be invertible and let
\mu_{t}\colon\bar{L}_{\alpha}\to\bar{L}_{\alpha}\colon a\mapsto ta
. Then
\mathord{\exp_{\alpha}}\circ\mu_{t}
satisfies all axioms of an
\alpha
-parametrization except that
\theta_{a}^{[1]}=\ad_{a}
.

We first show that parametrizations behave well with respect to reflections.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\bar{L}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\beta\in\Phi^{0}}\bar{L}_{\beta}^{\prime}
be a second
\Phi
-graded Lie algebra, let
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
, let
\varphi\colon\bar{L}\to\bar{L}^{\prime}
be a
\beta
-reflection (in the sense of
Definition

5.7
) and let
\exp_{\alpha}^{\prime}\colon\bar{L}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\to\Aut(\bar{L}^{\prime})
be an
\alpha
-parametrization (in
\bar{L}^{\prime}
). Then the map

\exp_{\alpha^{\sigma(\beta)}}\colon\bar{L}_{\alpha^{\sigma(\beta)}}\to\Aut(\bar{L})\colon a\mapsto\varphi^{-1}\circ\exp_{\alpha}^{\prime}\big(\varphi(a)\big)\circ\varphi

is an
\alpha^{\sigma(\beta)}
-parametrization (in
\bar{L}
).

Proof.

The axioms in
Definition

7.1
can be verified in a straightforward manner, using the linearity of
\sigma_{\beta}
for
Item

1
.
∎

Next we show that parametrizations corresponding to adjacent roots commute, which allows us to omit a few cases in the computation of commutator relations in
Proposition

7.23
.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system. A pair of roots
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
is called
adjacent
if they are non-proportional and the root interval
\mathord{]\alpha,\beta[}
from
Definition

1.5
Item

2
is empty.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system and let
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
be adjacent. Then for all
\gamma\in\Phi
and all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
, we have
\gamma+i\alpha+j\beta\not\in\Phi
.

Proof.

We normalize the root system
\Phi
in Euclidean space such that the short roots have Euclidean length
1
.
Assume, by contradiction, that
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
are such that
i\alpha+j\beta
is the sum of two roots
\gamma,\delta\in\Phi
,
so we have
i\alpha+j\beta=\gamma+\delta
.

We first observe that the angle between the adjacent roots
\alpha
and
\beta
is at most
90^{\circ}
.
Indeed,
\alpha
and
\beta
are contained in a common root subsystem of rank
2
, so it suffices to examine each of the possibilities
A_{1}\times A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
B_{2}=C_{2}
and
G_{2}
to see that a pair of roots at an angle in the interval
\mathord{]90^{\circ},180^{\circ}[}
can never be adjacent.
It now follows that the Euclidean length
\lVert i\alpha+j\beta\rVert
takes its minimum for
i=j=2
.
In other words, it suffices to show that

(19)

2\lVert\alpha+\beta\rVert>\lVert\gamma+\delta\rVert\quad\text{ for all }\gamma,\delta\in\Phi.

When
\Phi=G_{2}
there are only
3
possible configurations for adjacent roots (up to symmetry), namely either both
\alpha
and
\beta
are long and make an angle of
60^{\circ}
, or one is short and the other is long, making an angle of either
30^{\circ}
or
90^{\circ}
.
In any case, we have
\lVert\alpha+\beta\rVert>2
. Since roots have length at most
\sqrt{3}
, we have
\lVert\gamma+\delta\rVert\leq 2\sqrt{3}
, and (
19
) follows.

Now assume
\Phi\neq G_{2}
, so that all roots have length at most
\sqrt{2}
. In particular,

(20)

\lVert\gamma+\delta\rVert\leq 2\sqrt{2}.

On the other hand, since
\alpha
and
\beta
make an angle of at most
90^{\circ}
, we have

(21)

2\lVert\alpha+\beta\rVert\geq 2\sqrt{\lVert\alpha\rVert^{2}+\lVert\beta\rVert^{2}}.

Assume now that (
19
) is false. Then
\sqrt{\lVert\alpha\rVert^{2}+\lVert\beta\rVert^{2}}\leq\sqrt{2}
, from which we get
\lVert\alpha\rVert=\lVert\beta\rVert=1
, i.e., both
\alpha
and
\beta
are short roots; moreover, both inequalities (
20
) and (
21
) are equalities. This implies that on the one hand,
\gamma=\delta
is long, and on the other hand,
\alpha
and
\beta
make an angle of
90^{\circ}
.
The fact that we have (
19
) with an equality rather than an inequality also implies that
i=j=2
, so
2\alpha+2\beta=\gamma+\delta
. However, since
\gamma=\delta
, we now see that
\alpha+\beta=\gamma
, contradicting the fact that
\alpha
and
\beta
are adjacent.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
be adjacent and let

\exp_{\alpha}\colon\bar{L}_{\alpha}\to\Aut(\bar{L})\colon a\mapsto\sum_{i\geq 0}\theta^{[i,\alpha]}_{a}\quad\text{and}\quad\exp_{\beta}\colon\bar{L}_{\beta}\to\Aut(\bar{L})\colon b\mapsto\sum_{i\geq 0}\theta^{[i,\beta]}_{b}

be an
\alpha
-parametrization and a
\beta
-parametrization, respectively. Then for all
x_{\alpha}\in\bar{L}_{\alpha}
and
x_{\beta}\in\bar{L}_{\beta}
, the automorphisms
\exp_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha})
and
\exp_{\beta}(x_{\beta})
commute.

Proof.

Let
x_{\alpha}\in\bar{L}_{\alpha}
and
x_{\beta}\in\bar{L}_{\beta}
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\zeta\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
and
y\coloneq x_{\zeta}
, put
\delta^{i}_{\zeta}\coloneq\theta^{[i,\zeta]}_{y}
. Since

\exp_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha})\circ\exp_{\beta}(x_{\beta})=\left(\sum_{i\geq 0}\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\right)\circ\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}\delta^{j}_{\beta}\right)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\sum_{j\geq 0}\delta_{\alpha}^{i}\circ\delta_{\beta}^{j},

it suffices to show that
\delta_{\alpha}^{i}
and
\delta_{\beta}^{j}
commute for all
i,j\geq 0
. If
i,j\geq 2
, then it follows from
Lemma

7.5
that
\delta_{\alpha}^{i}\circ\delta_{\beta}^{j}
and
\delta_{\beta}^{j}\circ\delta_{\alpha}^{i}
are both zero on
L_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\Phi
. If
i=0
or
j=0
, then either
\delta_{\alpha}^{i}
or
\delta_{\beta}^{j}
is the identity map. It remains to consider the case that
i=1
or
j=1
, say
i=1
. Then
\delta_{\alpha}^{i}=\ad_{x_{\alpha}}
. By
Lemma

A.2
, the endomorphisms
\delta_{\beta}^{0},\delta_{\beta}^{1},\ldots
satisfy the higher Leibniz rules

(
∗
)

ℓ

. Hence for all
\gamma\in\Phi
, all
j\geq 0
and all
x_{\gamma}\in L_{\gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\beta}^{j}\circ\delta_{\alpha}^{i}(x_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\beta}^{j}([x_{\alpha},x_{\gamma}])=\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}[\delta_{\beta}^{\ell}(x_{\alpha}),\delta_{\beta}^{j-\ell}(x_{\gamma})].

Since
\delta_{\beta}^{\ell}(x_{\alpha})\in L_{\alpha+\ell\beta}
for all
\ell\geq 0
and
\alpha
,
\beta
are adjacent, all summands are zero except for
\ell=0
. Hence
\delta_{\beta}^{j}\circ\delta_{\alpha}^{i}(x_{\gamma})=[x_{\alpha},\delta_{\beta}^{j}(x_{\gamma})]=\delta_{\alpha}^{i}\circ\delta_{\beta}^{j}(x_{\gamma})
. We conclude that
\delta_{\alpha}^{i}
and
\delta_{\beta}^{j}
commute in all cases, and hence
\exp_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha})
and
\exp_{\beta}(x_{\beta})
commute.
∎

In
Appendix

B
, we will show that the reflections
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}
from
Definitions

5.8
and
5.18
are Weyl elements. For this we have to check that their conjugation action permutes the root groups accordingly. Since
\varphi_{\leftrightarrow}^{2}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}^{2}
are the parity automorphisms of certain gradings by
Remarks

5.13
and
5.19
, the following result allows us to deduce from
U_{\alpha}^{\varphi}=U_{\sigma(\alpha)}
that
U_{\sigma(\alpha)}^{\varphi}=U_{\alpha}^{\varphi^{2}}=U_{\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in G_{2}
,
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{\leftrightarrow},\varphi_{\operatorname{\rotatebox{120.0}{\!${}_{\leftrightarrow}$}}}\}
and
\sigma\colon G_{2}\to G_{2}
the reflection induced by
\varphi
. This cuts the amount of necessary computations by half. Explicitly, see
Lemmas

B.4
,
B.10
,
B.18
,
B.19
,
B.20
,
B.22
and
B.23
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that
\Phi
has a
(2p+1)
-grading, that is, a decomposition
\Phi=\bigsqcup_{i=-p}^{p}\Phi_{i}
such that
(\Phi_{i}+\Phi_{j})\cap\Phi\subseteq\Phi_{i+j}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
(where
\Phi_{i}\coloneq\emptyset
if
\lvert i\rvert>p
) and
\Phi_{-i}=-\Phi_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Put
\bar{L}_{i}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi_{i}}\bar{L}_{\alpha}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that
(\bar{L}_{i})_{-p\leq i\leq p}
is a
(2p+1)
-grading of
\bar{L}
. Let
\exp_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}\to\Aut(\bar{L})
be an
\alpha
-parametrization for some
\alpha\in\Phi
and let
\varphi\colon\bar{L}\to\bar{L}
denote the parity automorphism of
L
with respect to the
(2p+1)
-grading, so that
\varphi(x_{i})=(-1)^{i}x_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x_{i}\in\bar{L}_{i}
. Then for all
a\in\bar{L}_{\alpha}
, we have

\varphi^{-1}\circ\exp_{\alpha}(a)\circ\varphi=\exp_{\alpha}\big(\varphi(a)\big).

Proof.

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\beta\in\Phi_{i}
, we put
\epsilon_{\beta}\coloneq(-1)^{i}
, and also
\epsilon_{0}\coloneq 1
. Then for all
\beta,\gamma\in\Phi
with
\beta+\gamma\in\Phi
, we have
\epsilon_{\beta+\gamma}=\epsilon_{\beta}\epsilon_{\gamma}
. Since root strings in
\Phi
are unbroken, it follows that
\epsilon_{\beta+i\gamma}=\epsilon_{\beta}\epsilon_{\gamma}^{i}
for all
\beta,\gamma\in\Phi^{0}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\beta+i\gamma\in\Phi^{0}
. Now let
\beta\in\Phi^{0}
and
b\in\bar{L}_{\beta}
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\varphi^{-1}\circ\exp_{\alpha}(a)\circ\varphi(b)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\beta}\sum_{i\geq 0}\epsilon_{\beta+i\alpha}\theta_{a}^{[i]}(b)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\exp_{\alpha}\big(\varphi(a)\big)(b)

\displaystyle=\exp_{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\alpha}a)(b)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\theta_{\epsilon_{\alpha}a}^{[i]}(b)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\epsilon_{\alpha}^{i}\theta_{a}^{[i]}(b).

Since
\epsilon_{\beta}\epsilon_{\beta+i\alpha}=\epsilon_{\beta}^{2}\epsilon_{\alpha}^{i}=\epsilon_{\alpha}^{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the assertion follows.
∎

The following general result will allow us to establish Axiom
1.5
Item

4
of
\Phi
-graded groups for both
\Phi=G_{2}
and
\Phi=F_{4}
. The argument is the same as in the context of Chevalley groups (see, for instance, Corollary 3 of Lemma 18 in
[
26
]
)

Proposition 7.8
.

For each
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
\exp_{\alpha}\colon\bar{L}_{\alpha}\to\Aut(\bar{L})
be an
\alpha
-parametrisation and let
U_{\alpha}
denote the corresponding root group. Further, let
\Pi
be any positive system of roots in
\Phi
and denote by
U_{+}
and
U_{-}
the groups generated by
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Pi}
and
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in-\Pi}
, respectively. Then
U_{+}\cap U_{-}=1
. In particular, the root groups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
satisfy Axiom
1.5
Item

4
.

Proof.

Denote by
\rootht\colon\Phi\to\mathbb{Z}
the height function with respect to
\Pi
, which we extend to a function on
\Phi^{0}\coloneq\Phi\cup\{0\}
by setting
\rootht(0)\coloneq 0
. Put
\ell\coloneq\lvert\Phi^{0}\rvert
and choose an ordering
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell})
of the elements of
\Phi^{0}
such
\rootht(\alpha_{i})<\rootht(\alpha_{j})
implies
i<j
for all
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
.
For all
\alpha\in\Pi
and all
\phi\in U_{\alpha}
, we have the following property because
\exp_{\alpha}
is an
\alpha
-parametrization and
\rootht(\alpha)>0
: There exists a
k
-linear map
\phi^{\prime}\colon\bar{L}\to\bar{L}
such that
\phi=\id_{\bar{L}}+\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi^{\prime}(\bar{L}_{\alpha_{m}})\subseteq\sum_{m<j\leq\ell}\bar{L}_{\alpha_{j}}
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
. Since this holds for all
\alpha\in\Pi
, we infer that all
\phi\in U_{+}
have the same property. By similar arguments, all
\phi\in U_{-}
can be written as
\phi=\id_{\bar{L}}+\phi^{\prime}
for some
\phi^{\prime}\in\End(\bar{L})
with
\phi^{\prime}(\bar{L}_{\alpha_{m}})\subseteq\sum_{1\leq j<m}\bar{L}_{\alpha_{j}}
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
. Since the sum
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell}\bar{L}_{\alpha_{j}}
is direct, it follows that
U_{+}\cap U_{-}=\{\id_{\bar{L}}\}
.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

Let
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell})
be as in the proof of
Proposition

7.8
. Then any element of
\Aut(L)
has a
“matrix”
with respect to the direct sum decomposition
L=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\ell}L_{\alpha_{j}}
. The argument in the proof above says precisely that this matrix is lower triangular unipotent for all elements of
U_{+}
and upper triangular unipotent for all elements of
U_{-}
.

7.2.
Definition of exponential maps in
L

For the rest of this section, we assume that
(J,J^{\prime})
is a cubic norm pair over
k
and let
L=L(J,J^{\prime})
denote the
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra from
Section

4
. Our next goal is to construct an
\alpha
-parametrization
L_{\alpha}\to\Aut(L)\colon a\mapsto e_{a}
in
L
for each root
\alpha\in G_{2}
. We do so by giving explicit formulas involving the maps
N
and
\sharp
, so our construction does not work for the more general linear cubic norm pairs introduced in
Section

2.2
. As for the reflections defined in
Section

5
, the well-definedness of these maps is not evident and will be checked in
Lemma

7.17
(see also
Remark

5.3
).

Remark 7.10
.

For the scope of this remark, we identify
G_{2}^{0}
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as in
Remark

4.4
. Let
\alpha\in G_{2}
and fix an isomorphism of
k
-modules
\vartheta\colon P_{\alpha}\cong L_{\alpha}
(not necessarily the one from
Definition

6.4
). A map
\exp_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to\Aut(L)
may be called an
\alpha
-parametrization with respect to
\vartheta
if
\mathord{\exp_{\alpha}}\circ\vartheta^{-1}
is an
\alpha
-parametrization. Note that properties
Items

1
,
3
and
4
in
Definition

7.1
are independent of the choice of
\vartheta
, but
Item

2
is not. In our study of the
G_{2}
-grading of
L
, we will always (implicitly) choose the
“natural”
isomorphisms from
Definition

6.4
, so the distinction between parametrizations as maps on
P_{\alpha}
and parametrizations as maps on
L_{\alpha}
is barely visible. When we refine the
G_{2}
-grading to an
F_{4}
-grading, however, it will be more natural to choose non-trivial isomorphisms between
L_{\beta}
(for
\beta\in F_{4}
) and the corresponding parametrising structure
P_{\beta}
, and then the distinction between
L_{\beta}
and
P_{\beta}
will become crucial. For details, see
Section

10.3
.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
\lambda\in k
. We define endomorphisms
e_{\lambda x}
and
e_{\lambda y}
of the
k
-module
L
by setting

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x,

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime},

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}},\quad e_{\lambda x}(\xi)\coloneq\xi+2\lambda x,\quad e_{\lambda x}(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta+\lambda x,

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c,

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}-\lambda(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-},

\displaystyle e_{\lambda x}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y+\lambda\xi+\lambda^{2}x

and

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x-\lambda\xi+\lambda^{2}y,

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}+\lambda(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+},

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime},

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}},\quad e_{\lambda y}(\xi)\coloneq\xi-2\lambda y,\quad e_{\lambda y}(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta-\lambda y,

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c,

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+},

\displaystyle e_{\lambda y}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in k
,
b\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, put
a\coloneq(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\in L_{1}
and assume that at most one of the elements
\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu
is non-zero. We define an endomorphism
e_{a}
of the
k
-module
L
by setting

\displaystyle e_{a}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x+(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}-b^{\sharp}-(b^{\prime})^{\sharp}+\bigl(-N(b),0,0,N(b^{\prime})\bigr)_{+},

\displaystyle e_{a}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\lambda c^{\prime}-c\times b+\nu b^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\big(\rho\lambda-T(b,c^{\prime})\big)(\xi-\zeta)+\big(T(c,b^{\prime})-\nu\mu\big)\zeta

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\rho b-c^{\prime}\times b^{\prime}+\mu c

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\bigl(-\rho\lambda^{2}-T(b^{\sharp},c),U_{b}(c^{\prime})+\nu(b^{\prime})^{\sharp},

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\rho b^{\sharp}-U_{b^{\prime}}(c),T(c^{\prime},(b^{\prime})^{\sharp})+\nu\mu^{2}\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\big(-\rho N(b)-\nu N(b^{\prime})\big)y,

\displaystyle e_{a}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime}+\big(T(b,c^{\prime}),c^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},\mu c^{\prime},0\big)_{+}-T((b^{\prime})^{\sharp},c^{\prime})y,

\displaystyle e_{a}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}+\bigl(\lambda T(c,c^{\prime}),-D_{c,c^{\prime}}(b)+T(c,c^{\prime})b,

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptD_{c^{\prime},c}(b^{\prime})-T(c,c^{\prime})b^{\prime},-\mu T(c,c^{\prime})\bigr)_{+},

\displaystyle e_{a}(\xi)

\displaystyle\coloneq\xi-a,

\displaystyle e_{a}(\zeta)

\displaystyle\coloneq\zeta+(\lambda,0,-b^{\prime},-2\mu)_{+},

\displaystyle e_{a}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c-\big(0,\lambda c,c\times b,T(c,b^{\prime})\big)_{+}-T(c,b^{\sharp})y,

\displaystyle e_{a}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}+\big(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\big)y,

\displaystyle e_{a}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.13
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in k
,
b\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, put
a\coloneq(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-}\in L_{-1}
and assume that at most one of the elements
\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu
is non-zero. We define an endomorphism
e_{a}
of the
k
-module
L
by setting

\displaystyle e_{a}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x,

\displaystyle e_{a}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}+\big(T(b,c^{\prime})-T(c,b^{\prime})+\mu\nu-\lambda\rho\big)x,

\displaystyle e_{a}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime}+\big(T(b,c^{\prime}),c^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},\mu c^{\prime},0\big)_{-}-T((b^{\prime})^{\sharp},c^{\prime})x,

\displaystyle e_{a}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}+\bigl(\lambda T(c,c^{\prime}),-D_{c,c^{\prime}}(b)+T(c,c^{\prime})b,

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptD_{c^{\prime},c}(b^{\prime})-T(c,c^{\prime})b^{\prime},-\mu T(c,c^{\prime})\bigr)_{-},

\displaystyle e_{a}(\xi)

\displaystyle\coloneq\xi+(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{-},

\displaystyle e_{a}(\zeta)

\displaystyle\coloneq\zeta+(2\lambda,b,0,-\mu)_{-},

\displaystyle e_{a}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c-\big(0,\lambda c,c\times b,T(c,b^{\prime})\big)_{-}-T(c,b^{\sharp})x,

\displaystyle e_{a}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\lambda c^{\prime}+b\times c-\nu b^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\big(\lambda\rho-T(b,c^{\prime})\big)\cdot\zeta+\big(T(c,b^{\prime})-\mu\nu\big)\cdot(\xi-\zeta)

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\rho b+b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}-\mu c

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\bigl(\rho\lambda^{2}+T(c,b^{\sharp}),-U_{b}(c^{\prime})-\nu(b^{\prime})^{\sharp},

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\rho b^{\sharp}+U_{b^{\prime}}(c),-T((b^{\prime})^{\sharp},c^{\prime})-\mu^{2}\nu\bigr)_{-}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\big(\rho N(b)+\nu N(b^{\prime})\big)x,

\displaystyle e_{a}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y-(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}-b^{\sharp}-(b^{\prime})^{\sharp}+\big(-N(b),0,0,N(b^{\prime})\big)_{-}

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
,
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.14
.

Let
b\in J\subseteq L_{0}
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\subseteq L_{0}
. We define endomorphisms
e_{b}
and
e_{b^{\prime}}
of the
k
-module
L
by setting

\displaystyle e_{b}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y,

\displaystyle e_{b}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq\big(\nu,c+\nu b,c^{\prime}+b\times c+\nu b^{\sharp},\rho+T(b,c^{\prime})+T(c,b^{\sharp})+\nu N(b)\big)_{\pm},

\displaystyle e_{b}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime}-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+U_{b}(c^{\prime}),

\displaystyle e_{b}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}-D_{c,c^{\prime}}(b),\quad e_{b}(\xi)\coloneq\xi,\quad e_{b}(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta-b,

\displaystyle e_{b}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c,

\displaystyle e_{b}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x,

and

\displaystyle e_{b^{\prime}}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneq y,

\displaystyle e_{b^{\prime}}\big((\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle\coloneq\bigl(\nu-T(c,b^{\prime})+T((b^{\prime})^{\sharp},c^{\prime})-\rho N(b^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c-b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}+\rho(b^{\prime})^{\sharp},c^{\prime}-\rho b^{\prime},\rho\bigr)_{\pm},

\displaystyle e_{b^{\prime}}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneq c^{\prime},

\displaystyle e_{b^{\prime}}(\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{d}_{c,c^{\prime}}+D_{c^{\prime},c}(b^{\prime}),\quad e_{b^{\prime}}(\xi)\coloneq\xi,\quad e_{b^{\prime}}(\zeta)\coloneq\zeta+b^{\prime},

\displaystyle e_{b^{\prime}}(c)

\displaystyle\coloneq c+\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}+U_{b^{\prime}}(c),

\displaystyle e_{b^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq x,

for all
\nu,\rho\in k
,
c\in J
and
c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

Remark 7.15
.

The formulas in
Definitions

7.11
,
7.12
,
7.13
and
7.14
have been obtained by applying the usual definition of an exponential map

e_{a}=\id+\ad_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\ad_{a}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\ad_{a}^{3}+\tfrac{1}{24}\ad_{a}^{4},

simplifying the result using the facts
b\times b=2b^{\sharp}
,
D_{b,b^{\prime}}(b)=2U_{b}(b^{\prime})
and
T(b,b\times b)=6N(b)
for all
b\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and interpreting the resulting formula over the integers.
In particular, it is obvious from this procedure that the properties
Items

1
,
2
and
3
in
Definition

7.1
are satisfied. To show that the maps
a\mapsto e_{a}
are parametrizations, it remains to show that property
Item

4
is satisfied (which implies that
e_{a}
is invertible with inverse
e_{-a}
) and that, most importantly, all maps of the form
e_{a}
lie in
\End(L)
(that is, are compatible with the Lie bracket).

Remark 7.16
.

If necessary, we will write
e_{a}^{(J,J^{\prime})}
(where
a\in L(J,J^{\prime})_{ij}
for some
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
) to clarify that we consider the map
e_{a}
defined on the Lie algebra
L(J,J^{\prime})
. We will use a similar notation for the reflections defined in
Section

5
. Note that if
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})\to(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})
is a surjective homomorphism of cubic norm pairs, then

L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\circ e_{a_{1}}^{(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})}=e_{a_{2}}^{(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})}\circ L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})

for all
a_{1}\in L(J_{1},J_{1}^{\prime})_{ij}
where
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
and
a_{2}\coloneq L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})(a_{1})
. In particular,

e_{a_{1}}^{(J_{1},J_{1})}=L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})^{-1}\circ e_{a_{2}}^{(J_{2},J_{2}^{\prime})}\circ L(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})

if
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})
is bijective.

Lemma 7.17
.

The maps
e_{(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}}
,
e_{\lambda x}
,
e_{\lambda y}
,
e_{b}
and
e_{b^{\prime}}
are well-defined for all
\lambda,\mu\in k
,
b\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that at most one of
\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu
is non-zero, respectively.

Proof.

In this proof, all summations are understood to run over
a\in J
and
a^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. We proceed as in the proof of
Lemma

5.14
. We have to show that if

\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}=\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)

for certain, finitely many scalars
\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}},\lambda_{\zeta},\lambda_{\xi}\in k
, then both sides of this equation evaluate to the same term under the assignments defining
e_{(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}}
and
e_{\rho x}
. They are both fixed by the assignments defining
e_{\rho x}
, so
e_{\rho x}
is well defined. Under the assignments defining
e_{(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}}
, they evaluate to

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}+\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}t_{a,a^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\xi}\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)+\lambda_{\xi}(3\lambda,b,-b^{\prime},-3\mu)_{+},

respectively, where

t_{a,a^{\prime}}\coloneq\big(\lambda T(a,a^{\prime}),-D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)+T(a,a^{\prime})b,D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})-T(a,a^{\prime})b^{\prime},-\mu T(a,a^{\prime})\big).

By
Lemma

4.19
, we have

\displaystyle\sum\lambda_{a,a^{\prime}}t_{a,a^{\prime}}=\big(3\lambda\lambda_{\xi},-2\lambda_{\xi}b+3\lambda_{\xi}b,2\lambda_{\xi}b^{\prime}-3\lambda_{\xi}b^{\prime},-3\lambda_{\xi}\mu\big).

We conclude that also
e_{(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{+}}
is well-defined. The well-definedness of the remaining maps can be verified in a similar way.
∎

Remark 7.18
.

In fact, the well-definedness of the maps that we left out in the proof of
Lemma

7.17
does not have to be verified explicitly: We will see in
Proposition

B.14
that all remaining maps are conjugate to maps for which we have verified the well-definedness in the proof of
Lemma

7.17
, and hence they are well-defined themselves.

Remark 7.19
.

Since
[x,L_{1}]=L_{-1}
,
[y,L_{-1}]=L_{1}
,
[L_{i},L_{i}]=L_{2i}
for
i\in\{1,-1\}
and
[L_{1},L_{-1}]=L_{0}
, the sets
\{x\}\cup L_{1}
and
\{y\}\cup L_{-1}
are generating sets of the Lie algebra
L
. For all
f,g\in\End(L)
, it follows that
f=g
if and only if
f
and
g
agree on one of these sets.

Proposition 7.20
.

Identity
G_{2}
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as in
Remark

4.4
, so that
L_{\alpha}
is defined for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
. Then for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
, the map
\exp_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}\to\Aut(L)\colon a\mapsto e_{a}
(with
e_{a}
as given by
Definitions

7.11
,
7.12
,
7.13
and
7.14
) is a well-defined
\alpha
-parametrization in
L
.

Proof.

By
Remark

7.15
, we only have to show that for all
\alpha\in G_{2}
, the map
\exp_{\alpha}
is additive and has image in
\Aut(L)
.
In
Appendix

A
, we show that the maps
e_{\rho x}
for
\rho\in k
and
e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}
for
b\in J
are endomorphisms of the Lie algebra
L
. We proceed to show that
\exp_{\alpha}
is additive for
\alpha\in\{(-2,-1),(1,0)\}
. For all
s,t\in k
, we have
e_{(s+t)x}=e_{sx}\circ e_{tx}
because both sides act in the same way on the generating set
\{y\}\cup L_{-1}
of
L
. In particular,
e_{st}
is invertible for all
s\in k
, so the image of
\exp_{(-2,-1)}
lies in
\Aut(L)
. We conclude that the map
L_{-2,-1}\to\Aut(L)
is a
(-2,-1)
-parametrization.

Similarly, the identity
e_{(0,b+c,0,0)_{+}}=e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(0,c,0,0)_{+}}
for
b,c\in J
follows from the computations

\displaystyle(e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(0,c,0,0)_{+}})(x)

\displaystyle=e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\big(x+(0,c,0,0)_{-}-c^{\sharp}+(-N(c),0,0,0)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle=x+(0,b,0,0)_{-}-b^{\sharp}+\big(-N(b),0,0,0\big)_{+}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+(0,c,0,0)_{-}-b\times c+\big(-T(b^{\sharp},c),0,0,0\big)_{+}

\displaystyle\qquad{}-c^{\sharp}+\big(-T(b,c^{\sharp}),0,0,0\big)_{+}+\big(-N(c),0,0,0\big)_{+}

\displaystyle=x+(0,b+c,0,0)_{-}-(b^{\sharp}+c^{\sharp}+b\times c)

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\big(-N(b)-N(c)-T(b^{\sharp},c)-T(b,c^{\sharp}),0,0,0\big)_{+}

\displaystyle=x+(0,b+c,0,0)_{-}-(b+c)^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\big(-N(b+c),0,0,0\big)_{+}

and

\displaystyle(e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(0,c,0,0)_{+}})\big((\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\big)

\displaystyle=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)+T(c,a^{\prime})y+T(b,a^{\prime})y

for
(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{+}\in L_{1}
. Hence the map
L_{1,0}\to\Aut(L)
is a
(1,0)
-parametrization.
For arbitrary
\alpha\in G_{2}
, we can now deduce from
Proposition

B.14
that
\exp_{\alpha}
is additive with image in
\Aut(L)
, as desired.
∎

Definition 7.21
.

For all
\alpha\in G_{2}
, we will in the following always denote by

\exp_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}\to\Aut(L)\colon a\mapsto e_{a}

the
\alpha
-parametrization from
Proposition

7.20
and we denote by
U_{\alpha}\coloneq\exp_{\alpha}(L_{\alpha})
the associated root group.

7.3.
Constructing the
G_{2}
-graded group

We now show that the parametrizations previously defined satisfy some commutator relations. At first, we introduce a notation that is analogous to the one used in
[
28
, (16.8)]
.

Notation 7.22
.

Identifying
G_{2}
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as in
Remark

4.4
, we consider the following roots:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneq\delta=(1,0),

\displaystyle\alpha_{6}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma=(-2,-1),

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneq(1,-1)=\gamma+3\delta,

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,-1)=\gamma+2\delta,

\displaystyle\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle\coloneq(-1,-2)=2\gamma+3\delta,

\displaystyle\alpha_{5}

\displaystyle\coloneq(-1,-1)=\gamma+\delta.

Note that
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{6}\}
is a system of simple roots with associated system
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{6}\}
of positive roots. For all
i\in\{1,\ldots,6\}
, we define a map

x_{i}\colon P_{\alpha_{i}}\to\Aut(L)\colon a\mapsto\exp_{\alpha_{i}}\big(\vartheta_{\alpha_{i}}(a)\big)

where
P_{\alpha}\in\{J,J^{\prime},k\}
is the space parametrising
L_{\alpha}
and
\vartheta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L_{\alpha}
is the
“natural”
isomorphism from
Definition

6.4
.

Proposition 7.23
.

For all
\lambda,\mu\in k
,
b,d\in J
and
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, we have the following commutator relations between the positive root groups
U_{\alpha}
.

(22)

\displaystyle[x_{1}(b),x_{3}(b^{\prime})]=x_{2}(T(b,b^{\prime})),

(23)

\displaystyle[x_{3}(b^{\prime}),x_{5}(b)]=x_{4}(-T(b,b^{\prime})),

(24)

\displaystyle[x_{2}(\lambda),x_{6}(\mu)]=x_{4}(\lambda\mu),

(25)

\displaystyle[x_{1}(b),x_{5}(d)]=x_{2}(T(d,b^{\sharp}))\circ x_{3}(-b\times d)\circ x_{4}(T(b,d^{\sharp})),

(26)

\displaystyle[x_{1}(b),x_{6}(\lambda)]=x_{2}(-\lambda N(b))\circ x_{3}(\lambda b^{\sharp})\circ x_{4}(\lambda^{2}N(b))\circ x_{5}(\lambda b).

All other commutator relations are trivial.

Proof.

Observe first that the trivial commutator relations are all dealt with by
Lemma

7.6
.
For each of the remaining five cases, we will prove the equality using
Remark

7.19
.

To prove (
22
), we have to show that

e_{(0,-b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{-b^{\prime}}\circ e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{b^{\prime}}=e_{(T(b,b^{\prime}),0,0,0)_{+}}.

When we apply both sides of this equation on
x
, we get the same result
x+\bigl(T(b,b^{\prime}),0,0,0\bigr)_{-}
.
By
Remark

7.19
, it remains to show that both sides give the same result when applied on an element
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}\in L_{1}
.
A short computation shows that we twice get the result
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}-\rho T(b,b^{\prime})y
.

To prove (
23
), we have to show that

e_{-b^{\prime}}\circ e_{(0,-b,0,0)_{-}}\circ e_{b^{\prime}}\circ e_{(0,b,0,0)_{-}}=e_{(-T(b,b^{\prime}),0,0,0)_{-}}.

When we apply both sides of this equation on
y
, we get the same result
y+\bigl(T(b,b^{\prime}),0,0,0\bigr)_{+}
.
By
Remark

7.19
, it remains to show that both sides give the same result when applied on an element
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\in L_{-1}
.
A short computation shows that we twice get the result
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}+\rho T(b,b^{\prime})x
.

To prove (
24
), we have to show that

e_{(-\lambda,0,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{-\mu x}\circ e_{(\lambda,0,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{\mu x}=e_{(\lambda\mu,0,0,0)_{-}}.

Applying both sides on
y
gives the same result
y-(\lambda\mu,0,0,0)_{+}

and applying both sides on
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\in L_{-1}
gives the same result
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}-\lambda\mu\rho x
.

The remaining two commutator relations are considerably more difficult, but the method is similar.
To prove (
25
), we have to show that

e_{(0,-b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(0,-d,0,0)_{-}}\circ e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(0,d,0,0)_{-}}\\
=e_{(T(d,b^{\sharp}),0,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{-b\times d}\circ e_{(T(b,d^{\sharp}),0,0,0)_{-}}.

When we apply both sides of this equation on
y
, we get the same result
y-\bigl(T(b,d^{\sharp}),0,0,0\bigr)_{+}
.
Again, by
Remark

7.19
, it remains to show that both sides give the same result when applied on an element
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\in L_{-1}
. Directly computing the left hand side now turns out to be challenging, so instead, we verify that

e_{(0,-d,0,0)_{-}}\circ e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(0,d,0,0)_{-}}(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\\
=e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{(T(d,b^{\sharp}),0,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{-b\times d}\circ e_{(T(b,d^{\sharp}),0,0,0)_{-}}(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}.

The left-hand side of this equation is equal to

e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}-\rho T(b,d^{\sharp})x\\
\begin{aligned} &+\bigl(T(d,c\times b)+T(U_{b}(c^{\prime}),d^{\sharp})-\rho N(b)N(d)+T(d,c^{\prime})T(d,b^{\sharp}),\\
&\hskip 51.6665pt-D_{b,c^{\prime}}(d)+T(b,c^{\prime})d+T(d,c^{\prime})b+\rho U_{d}(b^{\sharp}),\ \rho b\times d,\ 0\bigr)_{-}\\
&+\bigl(-d\times U_{b}(c^{\prime})+\rho N(b)d^{\sharp}-T(d,c^{\prime})b^{\sharp}\bigr)+\rho\mathbf{d}_{d,b^{\sharp}}-\rho T(d,b^{\sharp})\zeta\\
&+\bigl(-T(d,c^{\prime})N(b),\ -\rho N(b)d,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}\end{aligned}

and the right-hand side is equal to

e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}-\rho T(b,d^{\sharp})x\\
\begin{aligned} &+\bigl(-\rho T(b,d^{\sharp})T(d,b^{\sharp})+T(c,b\times d)+T((b\times d)^{\sharp},c^{\prime})+\rho N(b\times d),\\
&\hskip 51.6665pt-\rho T(b,d^{\sharp})b+(b\times d)\times c^{\prime}+\rho(b\times d)^{\sharp},\ \rho b\times d,\ 0\bigr)_{-}\\
&+\bigl(T(d,b^{\sharp})(c^{\prime}+\rho b\times d)+\rho T(b,d^{\sharp})b^{\sharp}-b\times(c^{\prime}\times(b\times d))-\rho(b\times d)^{\sharp}\times b\bigr)\\
&\hskip 51.6665pt+\rho T(d,b^{\sharp})(\xi-\zeta)-\rho\mathbf{d}_{b,b\times d}-\rho T(b,b\times d)(\xi-\zeta)\\
&+\bigl(T(d,b^{\sharp})T(b,c^{\prime}+\rho b\times d)-\rho T(d,b^{\sharp})^{2}+\rho T(b,d^{\sharp})N(b)\\
&\hskip 51.6665pt-T((b\times d)\times c^{\prime}+\rho(b\times d)^{\sharp},b^{\sharp}),\ -\rho T(d,b^{\sharp})b+\rho U_{b}(b\times d),\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}.\end{aligned}

The equality of these two expressions now follows from
Definition

2.7
, identities
Items

7
,
10
,
16
,
26
and
29
in
Lemma

2.8
,
Lemma

4.17
Item

3
and
Items

4
,
5
and
6
in
Lemma

2.19
.

Finally, to prove (
26
), we have to show that

e_{(0,-b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{-\lambda x}\circ e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{\lambda x}\\
=e_{(-\lambda N(b),0,0,0)_{+}}\circ e_{\lambda b^{\sharp}}\circ e_{(\lambda^{2}N(b),0,0,0)_{-}}\circ e_{(0,\lambda b,0,0)_{-}}.

When we apply both sides of this equation on
y
, we get the same result

y+\bigl(2\lambda^{2}N(b),-\lambda b,0,0\bigr)_{+}-\lambda^{2}b^{\sharp}+\bigl(-\lambda^{3}N(b),0,0,0\bigr)_{-},

where we have used
T(b,b^{\sharp})=3N(b)
,
N(\lambda b)=\lambda^{3}N(b)
and
(\lambda b)^{\sharp}=\lambda^{2}b^{\sharp}
.
Again, by
Remark

7.19
, it remains to show that both sides also give the same result when applied on an element
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}\in L_{-1}
.
For the left-hand side, this gives

(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}+\bigl(\lambda T(b,c^{\prime})-\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)\bigr)x\\
\begin{aligned} &+\bigl(-\lambda T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ -\lambda T(b,c^{\prime})b+\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)b+\lambda U_{b}(c^{\prime}),\ -\rho\lambda b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{-}\\
&+\bigl(-\lambda T(b,c^{\prime})b^{\sharp}+\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)b^{\sharp}+\lambda U_{b}(c^{\prime})\times b\bigr)\\
&\hskip 51.6665pt-\rho\lambda\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\sharp}}+\rho\lambda N(b)\xi-\rho\lambda T(b,b^{\sharp})(\xi-\zeta)\\
&+\bigl(\lambda T(b,c^{\prime})N(b)-\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)^{2}-\lambda T(U_{b}(c^{\prime}),b^{\sharp}),\ \rho\lambda N(b)b-\rho\lambda U_{b}(b^{\sharp}),\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}\end{aligned}

while the right-hand side results in

(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}+\bigl(\lambda T(b,c^{\prime})-\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)\bigr)x\\
\begin{aligned} &+\bigl(-\lambda T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ -\lambda b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime}+\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)b,\ -\rho\lambda b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{-}\hskip 86.11084pt\\
&+\bigl(-\lambda N(b)c^{\prime}+\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)b^{\sharp}\bigr)-\rho\lambda N(b)(\xi-\zeta)\\
&+\bigl(-\rho\lambda^{2}N(b)^{2},\ 0,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}.\end{aligned}

The equality of these two expressions now follows from
Definition

2.7
,
Lemma

4.17
Item

1
and
Items

1
,
2
and
3
in
Lemma

2.19
.
∎

Theorem 7.24
.

Let
(J,J^{\prime})
be a cubic norm pair over the commutative ring
k
. Then the family
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
from
Definition

7.21
satisfies Axioms
1.5
Items

2
and
4
. If there exists
p\in J
such that
N(p)
is invertible (e.g., if
(J,J^{\prime})
is a cubic norm structure), then it is a
G_{2}
-grading of the subgroup of
\Aut(L(J,J^{\prime}))
that it generates.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions

7.23
,
7.8
and
B.25
.
∎

8.
The Tits index
F_{4}\to G_{2}

In this section, we describe how to obtain
G_{2}
-gradings of groups and Lie algebras from
F_{4}
-gradings of the same objects (
Propositions

8.10
and
8.13
). This purely combinatorial construction is similar to that of twisted Chevalley groups (see
[
9
, Chapter 13]
) and also to the construction of
H_{\ell}
-graded groups for
\ell\in\{2,3,4\}
in
[
5
, Section 4]
. It relies on a certain surjective map
F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
(
Lemma

8.4
) which is closely related to the Tits index that realises the Weyl group of
G_{2}
as a subgroup of the Weyl group of
F_{4}
. In the formal setup of
[
20
, 20.1]
, this Tits index is the tuple
(F_{4},\{\id\},\{3,4\})
, which is also encoded by
Fig.

2
(following the conventions of
[
27
]
, see also
[
20
, 34.2]
). We will informally refer to
(F_{4},\{\id\},\{3,4\})
by the name
“
F_{4}\to G_{2}
”
. While this Tits index and the associated embedding of Weyl groups are known, we are not aware of any reference that describes the associated map
F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
of root systems.

In
Sections

10
and
11
, we will reverse the construction in this section by showing that certain (but, of course, not all)
G_{2}
-gradings can be refined to obtain
F_{4}
-gradings.

1
2
3
4

Figure 2.
The Tits index
(F_{4},\{\id\},\{3,4\})
, which we refer to as
“
F_{4}\to G_{2}
”
.

Notation 8.1
.

Throughout this section, we denote by
\Delta_{F}=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4})
a standard system of simple roots in
F_{4}
(in the sense of
Definition

1.9
) and by
\Delta_{G}=(g_{1},g_{2})
a system of simple roots in
G_{2}
such that
g_{1}
is short and
g_{2}
is long. Further, we denote by
\Pi_{F}
,
\Pi_{G}
the corresponding positive systems of roots.

Remark 8.2
.

For some of our arguments, it will be helpful to have a list of the roots in
F_{4}
expressed as linear combinations of the vectors in
\Delta_{F}
at hand. Such a list can be read of from the first column of
Table

3
on page
3
. Further,

G_{2}=\{\pm(ag_{1}+bg_{2})\mid(a,b)\in\{(0,1),(1,0),(1,1),(2,1),(3,1),(3,2)\}\}.

Remark 8.3
.

If the roots of
G_{2}
and
F_{4}
are normalized so that short roots have length
\sqrt{2}
, then the inner products are given as follows:

\displaystyle f_{1}\cdot f_{2}=-2=f_{2}\cdot f_{3},\quad f_{3}\cdot f_{4}=-1,\quad f_{1}\cdot f_{1}=4=f_{2}\cdot f_{2},\quad f_{3}\cdot f_{3}=2=f_{4}\cdot f_{4},

\displaystyle g_{1}\cdot g_{2}=-3,\qquad g_{1}\cdot g_{1}=2,\qquad g_{2}\cdot g_{2}=6.

We begin with a characterization of the map
\pi\colon F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
, which depends on the choices of
\Delta_{F}
and
\Delta_{G}
.

Lemma 8.4
.

(1)

There exists a unique map
\pi\colon F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
such that

\pi(f_{1})=g_{2},\quad\pi(f_{2})=g_{1},\quad\pi(f_{3})=0=\pi(f_{4})

and such that
\pi
is induced by a linear map of the vector spaces surrounding
F_{4}
and
G_{2}
. In particular, for all
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
with
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
, we have
\pi(\alpha)+\pi(\beta)=\pi(\alpha+\beta)
(and hence
\pi(\alpha)+\pi(\beta)\in G_{2}^{0}
).

(2)

The map
\pi
is surjective and maps
\Pi_{F}
onto
\Pi_{G}\cup\{0\}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of
\pi
follows from the assumption that it is induced by a linear map. To prove existence, consider the 4-dimensional Euclidean space
V
surrounding
F_{4}
and the subspaces

V_{1}\coloneq\langle f_{1},3f_{2}+4f_{3}+2f_{4}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\quad\text{and}\quad V_{2}\coloneq\langle f_{3},f_{4}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}.

Then
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
and, by
Remark

8.3
, this decomposition is orthogonal. Denote by
\tilde{\pi}\colon V\to V_{1}
the orthogonal projection onto
V_{1}
and put
\bar{g}_{1}\coloneq\tilde{\pi}(f_{2})=f_{2}+\frac{4}{3}f_{3}+\frac{2}{3}f_{4}
,
\bar{g}_{2}\coloneq\tilde{\pi}(f_{1})=f_{1}
. Using
Remark

8.2
, we see that

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}(F_{4})=\{0_{V}\}\cup\{\pm(a\bar{g}_{1}+b\bar{g}_{2})\mid(a,b)\in\{(0,1),(1,0),(1,1),(2,1),(3,1),(3,2)\}\}.

Further, by
Remark

8.3
,

\bar{g}_{1}\cdot\bar{g}_{1}=\frac{4}{3},\qquad\bar{g}_{2}\cdot\bar{g}_{2}=4,\qquad\bar{g}_{1}\cdot\bar{g}_{2}=-3.

We conclude that
\tilde{\pi}(F_{4})\setminus\{0_{V}\}
is a root system of type
G_{2}
. Thus by identifying
(\bar{g}_{1},\bar{g}_{2})
with
(g_{1},g_{2})
, we obtain the desired map
\pi\colon F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
. We also see from our construction that
\pi
is surjective and
\pi(\Pi_{F})=\Pi_{G}\cup\{0\}
.
∎

The Tits index
F_{4}\to G_{2}
mentioned above yields an embedding
u\colon\Weyl(G_{2})\to\Weyl(F_{4})
. The following
Lemma

8.6
characterizes this embedding and describes its connection to
\pi
.

Remark 8.5
.

For any subset
I
of
\{1,2,3,4\}
, we denote by
w_{I}
the longest element in the Coxeter system spanned by
\{\sigma(f_{i})\mid i\in I\}
. Then

\displaystyle w_{\{1\}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{f_{1}},\qquad w_{\{3,4\}}=\sigma_{f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{4}}\sigma_{f_{3}},\qquad w_{\{1,3,4\}}=w_{\{1\}}w_{\{3,4\}},

\displaystyle w_{\{2,3,4\}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{f_{2}}(\sigma_{f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{2}}\sigma_{f_{3}})(\sigma_{f_{4}}\sigma_{f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{2}}\sigma_{f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{4}}).

The last assertion can be verified, for example, by calling

¯¯julia> W = weyl_group(:C, 3); f2,f3,f4 = W[3],W[2],W[1];
¯¯julia> longest_element(W) == f2*(f3*f2*f3)*(f4*f3*f2*f3*f4)
¯

in the computer algebra system OSCAR
[
23
,
11
]
. Note that

\displaystyle w_{\{2,3,4\}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{f_{2}}\sigma_{f_{2}}^{\sigma(f_{3})}\sigma_{f_{2}}^{\sigma(f_{3})\sigma(f_{4})}=\sigma_{f_{2}}\sigma_{f_{2}+2f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{2}+2f_{3}+2f_{4}}.

Lemma 8.6
.

The following hold, where
U\coloneq\langle\sigma(f_{3}),\sigma(f_{4})\rangle\leq\Weyl(F_{4})
.

(1)

There exists a unique homomorphism
u\colon\Weyl(G_{2})\to\Weyl(F_{4})
such that, in the notation of
Remark

8.5
,

u(\sigma_{g_{1}})=w_{\{2,3,4\}}w_{\{3,4\}},\qquad u(\sigma_{g_{2}})=w_{\{1\}}=\sigma(f_{1}).

Further,
u
is injective and its image centralizes
w_{\{3,4\}}
.

(2)

For all
w\in\Weyl(G_{2})
, all
\tilde{w}\in u(w)U
and all
\gamma\in F_{4}
, we have

\pi\big(\gamma^{\tilde{w}}\big)=\pi(\gamma)^{w}.

Proof.

Put
S\coloneq\{w_{\{2,3,4\}}w_{\{3,4\}},w_{\{1\}}\}
. By
[
20
, 20.32]
,
(\langle S\rangle,S)
is a Coxeter system. Is is a straightforward computation to show that the order of the product
w_{\{2,3,4\}}w_{\{3,4\}}w_{\{1\}}
is
6
, so this Coxeter system is of type
G_{2}
. Further,
u(\sigma_{g_{1}})
and
u(\sigma_{g_{2}})
commute with
w_{\{3,4\}}
by
[
20
, 20.11(i)]
. Assertion
1
follows.

It suffices to prove
2
for the case that
w\in\{\sigma(g_{1}),\sigma(g_{2})\}
and
\gamma\in\Delta_{F}
. Further, for all
\beta\in F_{4}
and
u\in U
, we have
\beta^{u}-\beta\in\langle f_{3},f_{4}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
and hence
\pi(\beta^{u})=\pi(\beta)
. Thus we may also assume that
\tilde{w}=u(w)
. We conclude that there are only eight cases to consider, and each of them is covered by a short computation.
∎

We can now describe the fibers of
\pi
, which will be crucial for our construction of
G_{2}
-gradings from
F_{4}
-gradings, and give an alternative description of
u
in terms of these fibers. For
\beta\in G_{2}^{0}
, we will always write
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
for
\{\alpha\in F_{4}^{0}\mid\pi(\alpha)=\beta\}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

The following hold.

(1)

For all
\beta\in G_{2}
and
w\in\Weyl(G_{2})
, we have
\pi^{-1}(\beta^{w})=\pi^{-1}(\beta)^{u(w)}
.

(2)

\pi^{-1}(0)=F_{4}^{0}\cap\langle f_{3},f_{4}\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}=\{\pm f_{3},\pm f_{4},\pm(f_{3}+f_{3})\}\cong A_{2}^{0}
.

(3)

For every long root
\beta\in G_{2}
, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
contains exactly one element, and this element is long in
F_{4}
.

(4)

For every short root
\beta\in G_{2}
, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
contains exactly three long roots in
F_{4}
and three short roots in
F_{4}
, and the subsystem
\langle\pi^{-1}(\beta)\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}\cap F_{4}
spanned by this fiber is of type
C_{3}
. If we denote the three long roots by
2e_{1}
,
2e_{2}
,
2e_{3}
, then
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
is an orthogonal basis of
\langle\pi^{-1}(\beta)\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
and the three short roots in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
are
e_{1}+e_{2}
,
e_{2}+e_{3}
,
e_{1}+e_{3}
.

(5)

Let
\beta\in G_{2}
and denote by
L(\beta)
the set of long roots in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
. Then

u(\sigma(\beta))=\begin{cases}\prod_{\alpha\in L(\beta)}\sigma_{\alpha}&\text{if }\beta\text{ is long},\\
(\prod_{\alpha\in L(\beta)}\sigma_{\alpha})\sigma_{f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{4}}\sigma_{f_{3}}&\text{if }\beta\text{ is short}\end{cases}

where the product over
L(\beta)
may be taken in any order.

Proof.

Assertions
1
and
2
follow from
Lemma

8.6
Item

2
and
Lemma

8.4
Item

1
, respectively. Using
Remark

8.2
, we can compute that

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(g_{1})

\displaystyle=\{f_{2}+f_{3},f_{2}+f_{3}+f_{4},f_{2}+2f_{3}+f_{4},f_{2},f_{2}+2f_{3},f_{2}+2f_{3}+2f_{4}\},

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(g_{2})

\displaystyle=\{f_{1}\}.

By a short computation, the long roots in
\pi^{-1}(g_{1})
are
f_{2},f_{2}+2f_{3},f_{2}+2f_{3}+2f_{4}
and their pairwise sums are precisely the remaining roots in
\pi^{-1}(g_{1})
multiplied by
2
. We infer that assertions
3
and
4
hold for
\beta\in\{g_{1},g_{2}\}
. By
1
, it follows that they hold for all
\beta\in G_{2}
.

Assertion
5
is satisfied for the long root
\beta=g_{2}
because
L(g_{2})=\{f_{1}\}
. Since
L(g_{1})=\{f_{2},f_{2}+2f_{3},f_{2}+2f_{3}+2f_{4}\}
, it follows from
Remark

8.5
that
w_{\{2,3,4\}}=\prod_{\alpha\in L(g_{1})}\sigma_{\alpha}
and hence
5
holds for
\beta=g_{1}
as well. Since all roots in
G_{2}
are conjugate to
g_{1}
or
g_{2}
and since the image of
u
centralizes
w_{\{3,4\}}=\sigma_{f_{3}}\sigma_{f_{4}}\sigma_{f_{3}}
by
Lemma

8.6
Item

1
, the general assertion follows by an application of
1
.
∎

Remark 8.8
.

A
3
-grading
of a root system
\Phi
is a decomposition
\Phi=\Phi_{-1}\sqcup\Phi_{0}\sqcup\Phi_{1}
such that
(\Phi_{i}+\Phi_{j})\cap\Phi\subseteq\Phi_{i+j}
for all
i,j\in\{-1,0,1\}
and some other properties are satisfied (see
[
19
, 14.1]
for details). The root system
C_{3}
has the following
3
-grading
[
19
, 14.5(c)]
:

\displaystyle(C_{3})_{\pm 1}

\displaystyle=\{\pm 2e_{i}\mid i\in\{1,2,3\}\}\cup\{\pm(e_{i}+e_{j})\mid i\neq j\in\{1,2,3\}\},

\displaystyle(C_{3})_{0}

\displaystyle=\{e_{i}-e_{j}\mid i\neq j\in\{1,2,3\}\}.

Note that the set
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
in
Lemma

8.7
Item

4
is precisely the set
(C_{3})_{1}
of
1
-graded roots while
\pi^{-1}(-\beta)=-\pi^{-1}(\beta)
is the set
(C_{3})_{-1}
of
(-1)
-graded roots.

Remark 8.9
.

It follows from
Lemma

8.7
Item

5
that
\prod_{\alpha\in L(\beta)}\sigma_{\alpha}\in u(\sigma_{\beta})U
for all
\beta\in G_{2}
where
U\coloneq\langle\sigma_{f_{3}},\sigma_{f_{4}}\rangle
. Hence we may always choose
\tilde{w}=\prod_{\alpha\in L(\beta)}\sigma_{\alpha}
for
w=\sigma_{\beta}
in
Lemma

8.6
Item

2
.

We can now apply our observations to construct
G_{2}
-gradings. For Lie algebras, the distributive law makes this fairly straightforward.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
L=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in F_{4}^{0}}L_{\alpha}
be an
F_{4}
-graded Lie algebra over a commutative ring
k
. For all
\beta\in G_{2}^{0}
, we define

L_{\beta}^{\prime}\coloneq\langle L_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(\{\beta\})\rangle_{k}.

Then
(L_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta\in G_{2}^{0}}
is a
G_{2}
-grading of
L
.

Proof.

Since
L_{\beta}^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)}L_{\alpha}

for all
\beta\in G_{2}^{0}
, we have
L=\bigoplus_{\beta\in G_{2}^{0}}L_{\beta}^{\prime}
.
Now let
\beta,\bar{\beta}\in G_{2}^{0}
and let
x_{\beta}\in L_{\beta}^{\prime}
,
y_{\bar{\beta}}\in L_{\bar{\beta}}^{\prime}
. Write

x_{\beta}=\sum_{\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)}x_{\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad y_{\bar{\beta}}=\sum_{\bar{\alpha}\in\pi^{-1}(\bar{\beta})}y_{\bar{\alpha}}

where
x_{\alpha}\in L_{\alpha}
and
y_{\bar{\alpha}}\in L_{\bar{\alpha}}
. Thus

[x_{\beta},y_{\bar{\beta}}]=\sum_{\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)}\sum_{\bar{\alpha}\in\pi^{-1}(\bar{\beta})}[x_{\alpha},y_{\bar{\alpha}}].

For all
\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)
,
\bar{\alpha}\in\pi^{-1}(\bar{\beta})
, there are two possibilities:

(1)

If
\alpha+\bar{\alpha}\notin F_{4}
, then
[x_{\alpha},y_{\bar{\alpha}}]=0
.

(2)

If
\alpha+\bar{\alpha}\in F_{4}
, then
\pi(\alpha+\bar{\alpha})=\pi(\alpha)+\pi(\bar{\alpha})=\beta+\bar{\beta}
by
Lemma

8.4
Item

1
. Hence
\beta+\bar{\beta}\in G_{2}^{0}
and
\alpha+\bar{\alpha}\in\pi^{-1}(\beta+\bar{\beta})
in this case, which implies that
[x_{\alpha},y_{\bar{\alpha}}]\in L_{\alpha+\bar{\alpha}}\leq L^{\prime}_{\beta+\bar{\beta}}
.

We infer that
[x_{\beta},y_{\bar{\beta}}]=0
if
\beta+\bar{\beta}\notin G_{2}^{0}
and
[x_{\beta},y_{\bar{\beta}}]\in L^{\prime}_{\beta+\bar{\beta}}
otherwise. It follows that
(L_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta\in G_{2}^{0}}
is a
G_{2}
-grading of
L
.
∎

The proof of
G_{2}
-commutator relations in
F_{4}
-graded groups is more difficult due to the non-linear nature of groups.
We can use the criterion
[
31
, 2.1.29]
(which is an application of
[
19
, 3.9]
), but for this we first need to establish
Lemma

8.12
.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\Delta_{G}^{\prime}=(g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime})
be a system of simple roots in
G_{2}
such that
g_{1}
is short and let
\Pi_{G}^{\prime}
be the corresponding positive system in
G_{2}
. Then there exists a standard system
\Delta_{F}^{\prime}=(f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime},f_{3}^{\prime},f_{4}^{\prime})
of simple roots in
F_{4}
with corresponding positive system
\Pi_{F}^{\prime}
such that
\pi(\Pi_{F}^{\prime})=\Pi_{G}^{\prime}\cup\{0\}
and
\pi(\Delta_{F}^{\prime})=(g_{2}^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime},0,0)
.

Proof.

If
\Delta_{G}^{\prime}=\Delta_{G}
, then we can choose
\Delta_{F}\coloneq\Delta_{F}^{\prime}
by
Lemma

8.4
. In the general case, there exists
w\in\Weyl(G_{2})
such that
\Delta_{G}^{\prime}=\Delta_{G}^{w}
. Put
\Delta_{F}^{\prime}\coloneq\Delta_{F}^{u(w)}
. Then it follows from
Lemma

8.6
Item

2
that

\displaystyle\pi(\Delta_{F}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\pi\big(\Delta_{F}^{u(w)}\big)=\pi(\Delta_{F})^{w}=(g_{2},g_{1},0,0)^{w}=(g_{2}^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime},0,0).

A similar computation shows that
\pi(\Pi_{F}^{\prime})=\Pi_{G}^{\prime}\cup\{0\}
.
∎

Lemma 8.12
.

For all non-proportional
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
, there exists a positive system
\Pi_{F}^{\prime}
in
F_{4}
which contains
\pi^{-1}(\{\alpha,\beta\})
.

Proof.

Choose a system
\Delta_{G}^{\prime}=\{g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime}\}
of simple roots in
G_{2}
such that
g_{1}
is short and such that
\alpha,\beta
lie in the positive system
\Pi_{G}
corresponding to
\Delta_{G}^{\prime}
. Let
\Delta_{F}^{\prime}=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4})
be a standard system of simple roots in
F_{4}
chosen as in
Lemma

8.11
. We show that the corresponding positive system
\Pi_{F}^{\prime}
contains
\pi^{-1}(\{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\})
. It suffices to show that
\pi^{-1}(\{\alpha,\beta\})
contains no root in
F_{4}\setminus\Pi_{F}^{\prime}=-\Pi_{F}^{\prime}
. This is satisfied because
