\pi(-\Pi_{F}^{\prime})=-\Pi_{G}^{\prime}\cup\{0\}
, a set which contains neither
\alpha
nor
\beta
.
∎

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
G
be a group with an
F_{4}
-grading
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
. For all
\beta\in G_{2}^{0}
, we define

U_{\beta}^{\prime}\coloneq\langle U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)\rangle.

Then
(G,(U_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta\in G_{2}})
satisfies Axioms
1.5
Items

1
,
2
and
3
, and hence it is a
G_{2}
-graded group if it satisfies Axiom
1.5
Item

4
.

Proof.

For Axiom
1.5
Item

1
, we show that the group generated by
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(0)}
is contained in the group generated by
(U_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta\in G_{2}}
. Recall from
Lemma

8.7
Item

2
that

\pi^{-1}(0)=\{\pm f_{3},\pm f_{4},\pm(f_{3}+f_{3})\}.

Observe that
f_{3}=\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
and
f_{4}=\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}
where

\displaystyle\delta_{1}\coloneq f_{2}+2f_{3}+f_{4},\qquad\delta_{2}\coloneq-(f_{2}+f_{3}+f_{4}),

\displaystyle\delta_{3}\coloneq f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3}+f_{4},\qquad\delta_{4}\coloneq-(f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3}).

Here
\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}
and
\{\delta_{3},\delta_{4}\}
span subsystems of type
A_{2}
. By
[
31
, 5.4.7]
, which is an elementary observation in the study of
A_{2}
-graded groups, it follows that
U_{\varepsilon f_{3}}=[U_{\varepsilon\delta_{1}},U_{\varepsilon\delta_{2}}]
,
U_{\varepsilon f_{4}}=[U_{\varepsilon\delta_{3}},U_{\varepsilon\delta_{4}}]
and
U_{\varepsilon(f_{3}+f_{4})}=[U_{\varepsilon f_{3}},U_{\varepsilon f_{4}}]
for all
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Hence the root groups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi^{-1}(0)}
are contained in the group generated by
(U_{\delta_{i}})_{i\in\{1,2,3,4\}}
.
Since
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\delta_{4}
lie in
\pi^{-1}(G_{2})
, we infer that
G
is generated by
(U_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta\in G_{2}}
.

For the existence of Weyl elements, let
\beta\in G_{2}
be arbitrary and let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
denote the pairwise distinct long roots in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, choose an
\alpha_{i}
-Weyl element
w_{i}=a_{-\alpha_{i}}b_{\alpha_{i}}c_{-\alpha_{i}}
where
a_{-\alpha_{i}},c_{-\alpha_{i}}\in U_{-\alpha_{i}}
and
b_{\alpha_{i}}\in U_{\alpha_{i}}
. By
Lemma

8.7
Items

4
and
3
, we have
\mathord{]\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}[}=\emptyset=\mathord{]\alpha_{i},-\alpha_{j}[}
and hence
[U_{\alpha_{i}},U_{\pm\alpha_{j}}]=1
for all distinct
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Together with
Remark

1.7
, this implies that
w_{\beta}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}
may be written as

w_{\beta}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}a_{-\alpha_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}b_{\alpha_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}c_{-\alpha_{i}}\in U_{-\beta}^{\prime}U_{\beta}^{\prime}U_{-\beta}^{\prime}.

Further, by
Lemma

8.6
Item

2
,
Remark

8.9
and because
\sigma_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\alpha_{m}}
commute pairwise, we have

U_{\gamma}^{w_{\beta}}=U_{\gamma^{\sigma(\alpha_{1})\cdots\sigma(\alpha_{m})}}\subseteq U_{\pi(\gamma)^{\sigma(\beta)}}^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{\gamma}^{w_{\beta}^{-1}}=U_{\gamma^{\sigma(\alpha_{m})\cdots\sigma(\alpha_{1})}}\subseteq U_{\pi(\gamma)^{\sigma(\beta)}}

for all
\gamma\in F_{4}
. This implies that
(U_{\delta}^{\prime})^{w_{\beta}}\subseteq U_{\delta^{\sigma(\beta)}}^{\prime}\subseteq(U_{\delta}^{\prime})^{w_{\beta}}
for all
\delta\in G_{2}
. We conclude that
w_{\beta}
is a
\beta
-Weyl element.

The commutator relations with respect to
(U_{\beta}^{\prime})_{\beta\in G_{2}}
follow from
[
31
, 2.1.29]
, whose assumptions are satisfied by
Lemma

8.12
. (More precisely,
loc. cit.
is formulated only for maps
\pi\colon\Phi\to\Phi^{\prime}
between root systems, but the same proof remains valid if the image of
\pi
lies in
\Phi^{\prime}\cup\{0\}
.)
∎

9.
Cubic Jordan matrix algebras

Now and for the rest of this paper, we move on to
F_{4}
-gradings. We do so by specializing to a specific kind of cubic norm pairs (in fact, cubic norm
structures
), which are known as
cubic Jordan matrix algebras
. In this section, we briefly introduce these objects and the so-called
multiplicative conic alternative algebras
over which they are defined, and collect all the properties that we will need later. We closely follow the exposition in
[
17
, Sections 36, 37]
.

Convention 9.1
.

For a commutative ring
k
, all
k
-algebras are understood to be unital but not necessarily associative or commutative. We recall that a
k
-algebra
A
is called
alternative
if
(aa)b=a(ab)
and
(ab)b=a(bb)
for all
a,b\in A
, which by Artin’s Theorem implies that the subalgebra generated by any two elements (and their inverses, if they exist) is associative (see, for example,
[
17
, 14.5]
).

9.1.
Notation and setup

Notation 9.2
.

From now on (and for the rest of this paper), we denote by
k
a commutative ring and by
(C,n)
a multiplicative conic alternative
k
-algebra in the sense of
[
17
, 16.1, 17.1]
. This means that
C
is an alternative
k
-algebra equipped with a quadratic form
n\colon C\to k
, called the
norm of
C
, such that
n(1_{C})=1_{k}
,
n(ab)=n(a)n(b)
for all
a,b\in C
and

a^{2}-n(1_{C},a)a+n(a)1_{C}=0_{C}

for all
a\in C
. Here
n(a,b)\coloneq n(a+b)-n(a)-n(b)
denotes the linearization of
n
for
a,b\in C
. Further, we denote by

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\colon C\to k\colon a\mapsto n(1_{C},a),

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\colon C\times C\to k\colon(a,b)\mapsto t(ab),

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle\colon C\to C\colon a\mapsto\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\coloneq t(a)1_{C}-a

the (linear) trace, the bilinear trace and the conjugation of
C
, respectively.
On a purely typographical level, we point out that the expression
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muac\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
for
a,c\in C
will always stand for
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(ac)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and not for
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.
We will usually refer to
C
as a multiplicative conic alternative algebra, dropping
n
from the notation.

Example 9.3
.

Composition algebras over rings (in the sense of
[
17
, 19.5]
), such as octonion or quaternion algebras, are the most prominent examples of multiplicative conic alternative algebras. Further, by
[
17
, 16.4]
any
k
-algebra that is projective of rank
2
as a
k
-module is a multiplicative conic algebra that is commutative and associative. This provides examples of multiplicative conic alternative algebras that are not composition algebras, such as the dual numbers
k[\epsilon]\coloneq k[t]/(t^{2})
. The crucial difference between composition algebras and multiplicative conic alternative algebras is that the latter need not be finitely generated projective as a
k
-module and that their norm need not satisfy any
“regularity assumption”
(such as non-degeneracy). For a detailed characterization of composition algebras in terms of conic alternative algebras, see
[
17
, 19.13]
. For a discussion of the subtleties involving different
“regularity assumptions”
that coincide for finite-dimensional vector spaces over fields of characteristic not
2
but not over base rings, see
[
17
, 11.9, 11.11]
.

Remark 9.4
.

For all
a,b,c\in C
, the following identities hold.

(1)

The conjugation is an involution:
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua+b\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muab\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=a
.
[
17
, 17.2]

(2)

The conjugation involution is scalar:
a\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mua=n(a)1_{C}
and
a+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=t(a)1_{C}
.
[
17
, (16.5.6)]

(3)

n
and
t
are invariant under conjugation:
n(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=n(a)
and
t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=t(a)
.
[
17
, (16.5.7)]

(4)

Kirmse’s identities
:
a(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mub)=n(a)b=(b\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)a
and, thus, the linearization
a(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mub)+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(cb)=n(a,c)b=(b\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)c+(b\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)a
.
[
17
, (17.4.1)]

(5)

t(a^{2})=t(a)^{2}-2n(a)
.
[
17
, (16.5.8)]

(6)

n(a,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=t(a)t(b)-n(a,b)
.
[
17
, (16.5.10)]

(7)

C
is norm-associative:
n(a,ba)=t(b)n(a)
,
n(a,ab)=t(b)n(a)
,
n(ab,c)=n(a,c\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=n(b,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muc)
,
t(a,b)=n(a,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
.
[
17
, 16.12, 17.2]

(8)

t
is an associative linear form (in the sense of
[
17
, 7.9]
):
t(ab)=t(ba)
and
t((ab)c)=t(a(bc))\eqcolon t(abc)
.
[
17
, (16.13.1)]

Notation 9.5

(
[
17
, 36.1]
)
.

We will often say that
“a sum is taken over all cyclic permutations
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
”
to mean that an expression
\sum x_{ijl}
stands for
x_{123}+x_{231}+x_{312}
.

Notation 9.6
.

We fix a (not necessarily invertible) diagonal matrix
\Gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
in
\Mat_{3}(k)
and we denote by
J
the
cubic Jordan matrix algebra

\Her_{3}(C,\Gamma)
, as in
[
17
, 36.4]
. For the reader’s convenience, we spell out what this means. As a
k
-module,
J
is isomorphic to
k^{3}\oplus C^{3}
and consists of all formal expressions

\sum\bigl(\xi_{i}e_{ii}+u_{i}[jl]\bigr)

with
\xi_{i}\in k
,
u_{i}\in C
, where the sum is taken over all cyclic permutations
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
.
We will write

\displaystyle e_{i}\coloneq e_{ii}\in J,\quad u[lj]\coloneq\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muu\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[jl],\quad 1_{J}\coloneq e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3},

\displaystyle J_{ii}\coloneq ke_{i},\quad J_{jl}\coloneq J_{lj}\coloneq C[jl]

for any cyclic permutation
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
and all
\xi\in k
,
u\in C
. Thus we have a decomposition

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=J_{11}\oplus J_{22}\oplus J_{33}\oplus J_{23}\oplus J_{31}\oplus J_{12}.

For arbitrary elements

x=\sum\big(\xi_{i}e_{i}+u_{i}[jl]\big)\quad\text{and}\quad y=\sum\big(\eta_{i}e_{i}+v_{i}[jl]\big)

of
J
, we define

\displaystyle x^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum\Big(\big(\xi_{j}\xi_{l}-\gamma_{j}\gamma_{l}n(u_{i})\big)e_{i}+(-\xi_{i}u_{i}+\gamma_{i}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(u_{j}u_{l})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)[jl]\big),

\displaystyle N(x)

\displaystyle\coloneq\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}-\sum\gamma_{j}\gamma_{l}\xi_{i}n(u_{i})+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}t(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}),

\displaystyle x\times y

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum\Bigl(\big(\xi_{j}\eta_{l}+\eta_{j}\xi_{l}-\gamma_{j}\gamma_{l}n(u_{i},v_{i})\big)e_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\big(-\xi_{i}v_{i}-\eta_{i}u_{i}+\gamma_{i}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu(u_{j}v_{l}+v_{j}u_{l})\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big)[jl]\Bigr),

\displaystyle T(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum\big(\xi_{i}\eta_{i}+\gamma_{j}\gamma_{l}n(u_{i},v_{i})\big)

where all sums are taken over all cyclic permutations
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
. This defines maps
\sharp
,
N
,
\times
,
T
that, by
[
17
, 36.5]
, equip
J
with the structure of a cubic norm structure with base point
1_{J}
.

Notation 9.7
.

Being a cubic norm structure,
J
induces a cubic norm pair that we denote by
(J,J^{\prime})
. Thus
J^{\prime}
is an isomorphic copy of
J
and we let

\iota\colon J\to J^{\prime}\colon a\mapsto a^{\iota}

be a fixed isomorphism between
J
and
J^{\prime}
, with inverse

{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}\colon J^{\prime}\to J\colon a\mapsto a^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}.

Hence
(\iota,{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}})\colon(J,J^{\prime})\to(J^{\prime},J)
is an involution of
(J,J^{\prime})
. Further, we denote by
e_{ii}^{\prime}=e_{i}^{\prime}
,
1_{J^{\prime}}
,
J_{ii}^{\prime}
and
J_{jl}^{\prime}
the images of the corresponding elements and subspaces of
J
under
\iota
.

Remark 9.8
.

Assume that
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
are invertible, put
p\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{3}\gamma_{i}e_{i}\in J
and denote by
\Her_{3}(C)
the cubic Jordan matrix algebra defined with respect to the diagonal matrix
\bar{\Gamma}=(1_{k},1_{k},1_{k})
. Then by
[
17
, 37.23]
, the
p
-isotope of
J
(in the sense of
Remark

2.17
) is isomorphic to
\Her_{3}(C)
. In particular, if we restrict to the case that
\Gamma
is invertible, then the isotopy type of
(J,J^{\prime})
(and hence, by
Proposition

5.16
, the isomorphism type of the Lie algebra
L(J,J^{\prime})
) does not depend on the choice of
\Gamma
.

Remark 9.9
.

It follows from the formula defining
T
that
T
is non-degenerate (in the sense that the radical defined in
Example

6.15
is zero, i.e.,
T(a,J)=\{0\}
implies
a=0
) if and only if the linearization of
n
is non-degenerate (in a similar sense). Thus if
k
is a field and the linearization of
n
is non-degenerate, then
L(J,J^{\prime})
is simple by
Theorem

6.25
. In particular, these conditions are satisfied if
k
is field and
C
is a composition algebra in the sense of
[
17
, 19.5]
. Under these assumptions,
J
is a Freudenthal algebra by
[
17
, 39.9(d)]
. If, in addition,
C
is an octonion algebra, then
J
is an Albert algebra by
[
17
, 39.19(a)]
.

Remark 9.10
.

By
[
17
, 37.8, 37.9]
, the triple
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
is an elementary frame in the sense of
[
17
, 37.5]
, and it is connected if and only if
\Gamma
is invertible. With this setup, we may apply the results of
[
17
, Section 37]
to
J
(with the additional assumption that
\Gamma
is invertible if a co-ordinate system is required).

9.2.
Identities in cubic Jordan matrix algebras

We begin by collecting some results which break down the long formulas in
9.6
into more succinct identities. For the next lemma, recall the Jordan pair structure on
(J,J^{\prime})
from
Definition

2.7
.

Lemma 9.11

(Multiple Peirce decomposition)
.

The following assertions hold for all
i,j,l,m,p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
.

(1)

\{J_{ij},J_{jl}^{\prime},J_{lm}\}\subseteq J_{im}
and
U_{J_{ij}}(J_{ij}^{\prime})\subseteq J_{ij}
.

(2)

\{J_{ij},J_{lm}^{\prime},J_{pq}\}=0
and
U_{J_{ij}}(J_{lm}^{\prime})=0
if the indices
i,j,l,m,p,q
(respectively,
i,j,l,m
) cannot be pairwise reordered in the above form. By this we mean that, in the sense of
[
17
, 32.14]
, the Peirce triple
(ij,lm,pq)
(respectively,
(ij,lm,ij)
) is not connected.

Similar statements hold if the roles of
J
and
J^{\prime}
are interchanged.

Proof.

By
[
17
, 37.8]
, the spaces
J_{ij}
for
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
are precisely the Peirce components of the cubic norm structure
J
. Hence the assertion follows from the Peirce decomposition in Jordan algebras, see
[
17
, 32.15]
.
∎

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
a\in J
and write

a=\xi_{1}e_{1}+\xi_{2}e_{2}+\xi_{3}e_{3}+c_{1}[23]+c_{2}[31]+c_{3}[12]

with each
\xi_{i}\in k
and each
c_{i}\in C
. Let
(i\,j\,l)
be any (not necessarily cyclic) permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
. Then the following formulas (and their analogues for
T^{\prime}
in place of
T
) hold.

(1)

T(a,e_{i}^{\prime})=\xi_{i}
.

(2)

T(a,d_{l}[ij]^{\iota})=T(c_{l}[ij],d_{l}[ij]^{\iota})=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mud_{l})
for all
d_{l}\in C
.

(3)

The Peirce decomposition of
(J,J^{\prime})
is orthogonal with respect to
T
in the sense that
T(b_{pq},b^{\prime}_{rs})=0
for all
b_{pq}\in J_{pq}
,
b^{\prime}_{rs}\in J_{rs}^{\prime}
with
\{p,q\}\neq\{r,s\}
.

Proof.

These are special cases of the formulas defining and
T
in
9.6
. Note that we may assume that
(i\,j\,l)
is a cyclic permutation, the general case being a consequence of this case (because
t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mud_{l})=t(c_{l}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
by
Remark

9.4
Items

3
and
8
). Alternatively, we may cite
[
17
, (37.13.5)]
for
Item

1
and the first equation in
Item

2
and
[
17
, 37.4(c)]
for
Item

3
.
∎

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
a\in J
and write

a=\xi_{1}e_{1}+\xi_{2}e_{2}+\xi_{3}e_{3}+c_{1}[23]+c_{2}[31]+c_{3}[12]

with each
\xi_{i}\in k
and each
c_{i}\in C
. Put
c_{ij}\coloneq c_{l}[ij]
for all cyclic permutations
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
. Let
(i\,j\,l)
and
(i^{\prime}\,j^{\prime}\,l^{\prime})
be permutations of
(1\,2\,3)
such that
(i\,j\,l)
is cyclic. Then the following formulas (and their analogues for
T^{\prime},\mathord{\times}^{\prime}
in place of
T,\times
) hold.

(1)

a\times e_{i}=\xi_{l}e_{j}^{\prime}+\xi_{j}e_{l}^{\prime}-c_{i}[jl]^{\iota}
. In particular,
e_{i^{\prime}}\times e_{j^{\prime}}=e_{l^{\prime}}^{\prime}
and
c_{i^{\prime}}[j^{\prime}l^{\prime}]\times e_{i^{\prime}}=-c_{i^{\prime}}[j^{\prime}l^{\prime}]^{\iota}
.

(2)

c_{l^{\prime}}[i^{\prime}j^{\prime}]\times d_{i^{\prime}}[j^{\prime}l^{\prime}]=\gamma_{j^{\prime}}c_{l^{\prime}}d_{i^{\prime}}[il]
for all
d_{i^{\prime}}\in C
.

(3)

a\times d_{ij}=-T(c_{ij},d_{ij})e_{l}^{\prime}-\xi_{l}d_{ij}^{\iota}+c_{li}\times d_{ij}+d_{ij}\times c_{jl}
for all
d_{ij}\in J_{ij}
.

Proof.

These are special cases of the formulas defining
\times
and
T
in
9.6
. Again, the general case follows from the cyclic one because

\displaystyle e_{l}\times e_{j}

\displaystyle=e_{j}\times e_{l}=e_{i},

\displaystyle c_{l}[ji]\times e_{l}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ij]\times e_{l}=-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ij]^{\iota}=-c_{l}[ji]^{\iota}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle c_{i}[lj]\times d_{l}[ji]

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ij]\times\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[jl]=\gamma_{j}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[il]=\gamma_{j}c_{i}d_{l}[li].

Alternatively, we may cite
[
17
, (37.13.4)]
for
Items

1
and
3
.
∎

Lemma 9.14
.

For all pairwise distinct
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3\}
, the following hold:

(1)

J_{ij}\times J_{jl}\subseteq J_{il}^{\prime}
.

(2)

J_{ij}\times J_{ij}\subseteq J_{ll}^{\prime}
and
J_{ij}\times J_{ll}\subseteq J_{ij}^{\prime}
.

(3)

J_{ij}\times J_{jj}=0=J_{jj}\times J_{jj}
.

Proof.

If
(i\,j\,l)
is a cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
, then by
Lemma

9.13
, all assertions hold by
Lemma

9.13
. The general case follows because
\times
is symmetric and
J_{ij}=J_{ji}
. Further,
Item

1
is
[
17
, (37.13.1)]
.
∎

We can also compute explicit formulas for the operators
D_{a,a^{\prime}}=D(a,a^{\prime})
from
Definition

2.7
, which together with
Lemma

9.11
characterize these operators. In practice, we will only need a few of these formulas, but we state them all for completeness. We can already see at this point that for many (though not all) possible choices of
i,j,p,q
, the operator
D(a[ij],b[pq])
depends only on the product
ab
. This will later lead to the similar statements in
Lemmas

10.9
and
10.10
about
\mathbf{d}(a[ij],b[pq])
. However, only
Lemma

10.9
will be proven by using the explicit formulas in
Lemma

9.15
, while we derive
Lemma

10.10
from
Lemma

4.17
by specializing to cubic Jordan matrix algebras.

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3\}
be pairwise distinct, let
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in C
and let
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in k
. Then the following hold:

(1)

The operator
D(a_{1}[jl],a_{2}[li]^{\iota})
satisfies

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[jl],a_{2}[li]^{\iota},a_{3}[ij]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}\gamma_{l}t(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})e_{j},

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[jl],a_{2}[li]^{\iota},a_{3}[il]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{l}(a_{1}a_{2})a_{3}[jl],

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[jl],a_{2}[li]^{\iota},s_{3}e_{i}\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{l}s_{3}a_{1}a_{2}[ji].

(2)

The operator
D(s_{1}e_{j},a_{2}[ji]^{\iota})
satisfies

\displaystyle\{s_{1}e_{j},a_{2}[ji]^{\iota},a_{3}[ij]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}s_{1}t(a_{2}a_{3})e_{j},

\displaystyle\{s_{1}e_{j},a_{2}[ji]^{\iota},a_{3}[il]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}s_{1}a_{2}a_{3}[jl]

\displaystyle\{s_{1}e_{j},a_{2}[ji]^{\iota},s_{3}e_{i}\}

\displaystyle=s_{1}s_{3}a_{2}[ji].

(3)

The operator
D(a_{1}[ji],s_{2}e_{i}^{\prime})
satisfies

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[ji],s_{2}e_{i}^{\prime},a_{3}[ij]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}s_{2}t(a_{1}a_{3})e_{j},

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[ji],s_{2}e_{i}^{\prime},a_{3}[il]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}s_{2}a_{1}a_{3}[jl],

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[ji],s_{2}e_{i}^{\prime},s_{3}e_{i}\}

\displaystyle=s_{2}s_{3}a_{1}[ji].

(4)

The operator
D(s_{1}e_{i},s_{2}e_{i})
satisfies

\displaystyle\{s_{1}e_{i},s_{2}e_{i}^{\prime},s_{3}e_{i}\}

\displaystyle=2s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}e_{i},

\displaystyle\{s_{1}e_{i},s_{2}e_{i}^{\prime},a_{3}[ij]\}

\displaystyle=s_{1}s_{2}a_{3}[ij].

(5)

The operator
D(a_{1}[ij],a_{2}[ji])
satisfies

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[ij],a_{2}[ji]^{\iota},s_{3}e_{i}\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}s_{3}t(a_{1}a_{2})e_{i},

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[ij],a_{2}[ji]^{\iota},a_{3}[ij]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}\big(t(a_{2}a_{3})a_{1}+t(a_{1}a_{2})a_{3}-t(a_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{3}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{2}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big)[ij],

\displaystyle\{a_{1}[ij],a_{2}[ji]^{\iota},a_{3}[il]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}a_{1}(a_{2}a_{3})[il].

Further, the same statements hold if all elements
b\in J
and
c\in J^{\prime}
are replaced by
b^{\iota}\in J^{\prime}
and
c^{\mathord{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\iota$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\iota$}}}}}\in J
, respectively.

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation, see
Section

9.3
for details. Note that, since
\{a,b^{\prime},c\}=\{c,b^{\prime},a\}
for
a,c\in J
,
b^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
and
a[ij]=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ji]
for
a\in C
and
i\neq j
, some of the formulas are redundant. For the second formula in
Item

1
, we first prove by a straightforward computation that

\{a_{1}[jl],a_{2}[li]^{\iota},a_{3}[il]\}=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{l}\big(t(a_{2}a_{3})a_{1}-(a_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{3}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{2}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big),

and then the desired formula follows by an application of
Remark

9.4
Item

4
.
∎

9.3.
Computations in cubic Jordan matrix algebras

The computations in
J
that are required in the proof of
Lemma

9.15
, and similar computations in
L
and
\Aut(L)
that we will need later, boil down to the following two steps: First, we unravel the definition of all involved operators (such as
D
,
U
,
N
,
\sharp
,
\times
,
T
and the Lie bracket) to see that the desired assertion is equivalent to a set of equations in
C
and
k
. Second, we show that these equations follow from known identities on multiplicative conic alternative algebras, such as the ones in
Remark

9.4
. While this procedure is straightforward in principle, the required computations can be rather long. In particular, the verification that certain elements in
\Aut(L)
are Weyl elements (
Proposition

11.11
) seems too extensive to be manageable by hand. For this reason, we have performed these computations with the computer algebra system GAP
[
16
]
. Our code is available at
[
32
]
. In the following, we provide a few details on how these computations are performed.

The key problem consists in working with an
arbitrary
multiplicative conic alternative algebra. In other words, we have to work in a
free
multiplicative conic algebra, whose generators we may think of as indeterminates. Say that we want to prove identities involving at most
p
elements in the conic algebra and at most
q
elements in the commutative ring. We introduce corresponding indeterminates (that is, abstract symbols)
a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{q}
. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, we also introduce abstract symbols
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
n(a_{i})=n(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
, which do not (yet) have any relation to the symbol
a_{i}
. Similarly, we define an abstract symbol
t(a)
for each
a
in the free magma
A^{*}
generated by the symbols in
A\coloneq\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{p},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{1}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{p}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\}
.

Using all the symbols above, we define
\tilde{k}
to be the polynomial ring over
\mathbb{Z}
in indeterminates
t_{1},\ldots,t_{q},n(a_{1}),\ldots,n(a_{p})
and
t(a)
for
a\in A
. Similarly, we define
\tilde{C}
to be the free nonassociative algebra over
\tilde{k}
generated by the symbols
a_{1},\ldots,a_{p},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{1}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{p}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
. We can now give meaning to the symbol names introduced earlier: Define
t\colon\tilde{C}\to\tilde{k}
to be the unique
\tilde{k}
-linear map which sends
a\in A^{*}\subseteq\tilde{C}
to
t(a)\in\tilde{k}
and define
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\colon\tilde{C}\to\tilde{C}
to be the unique
k
-linear map which sends
a_{i}
to
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
to
a_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
and such that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muab\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
for all
a,b\in A^{*}\subseteq\tilde{C}
. We also define
n\colon A^{*}\to\tilde{k}
to be the unique homomorphism of magmas that maps
a\in A
to the abstract symbol
n(a)
. Choosing an arbitrary total order
<
on
A^{*}
(for example, the lexicographic order), we can extend
n
to a map
n\colon\tilde{C}\to\tilde{k}
(which depends on the choice of
<
) by putting

(27)

n\Bigl(\sum_{a\in A^{*}}\lambda_{a}a\Bigr)\coloneq\sum_{a\in A^{*}}\lambda_{a}^{2}n(a)+\sum_{a<b\in A^{*}}\lambda_{a}\lambda_{b}t(a\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)

for any family
(\lambda_{a})_{a\in A^{*}}
of scalars in
\tilde{k}
of which only finitely many are non-zero. In other words,
n
is the unique
\tilde{k}
-quadratic map
\tilde{C}\to\tilde{k}
mapping
a_{i}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
to
n(a_{i})=n(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
and such that
n(a+b)=n(a)+n(b)+t(a\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
for all
a<b\in A^{*}
. The objects and maps
\tilde{k}
,
\tilde{C}
,
t
,
n
,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
are straightforward to implement in GAP. Hence we can also implement the maps
N
,
T
,
\times
and
\sharp
defined on
\tilde{J}\coloneq\tilde{C}^{3}\oplus\tilde{k}^{3}
.

By construction,
\tilde{k}
and
\tilde{C}
satisfy the following universal property: For arbitrary
b_{1},\ldots,b_{p}\in C
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{q}\in k
, there exist unique homomorphisms
\pi_{k}\colon\tilde{k}\to k
and
\pi_{C}\colon\tilde{C}\to C
such that
\pi_{k}(t_{i})=s_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
,
\pi_{C}(a_{j})=b_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
and

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(t(c))=t_{C}\big(\pi_{C}(c)\big),\quad\pi_{k}(n(c))=n_{C}\big(\pi_{C}(c)\big),\quad\pi_{C}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi_{C}(c)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

for all
c\in\tilde{C}
where
n_{C}
,
t_{C}
denote the norm and trace on
C
. In particular, while
n\colon\tilde{C}\to\tilde{k}
depends on the choice of the total order
<
on
A^{*}
, the map
\pi_{k}\circ n\colon\tilde{C}\to k
is independent of this choice because it equals
n_{C}\circ\pi_{C}
.
We say that a
simplification map
is a map
\alpha\colon\tilde{C}\to\tilde{C}
(or
\alpha\colon\tilde{k}\to\tilde{k}
) such that for all possible choices of
k
,
C
,
b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}\in C
and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in k
, we have
\alpha(c)-c\in\ker(\pi_{C})
for all
c\in\tilde{C}
(or
\alpha(s)-s\in\ker(\pi_{k})
for all
s\in\tilde{k}
). For example, for any total order
\prec
on
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{p},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{1}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,\ldots,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{p}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\}
, the map which replaces all occurrences of
t(bc)
for
c\prec b\in A
by
t(cb)
is a simplification map by
Remark

9.4
Item

8
. In order to prove identities
\ell=r
such as those in
Lemma

9.15
, we compute both sides
\ell
,
r

“in
\tilde{J}
”
(using one of the indeterminates
a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}
for each variable in
C
that occurs, and similarly using
t_{1},\ldots,t_{q}
for variables in
k
) and apply a certain (fixed) sequence of simplification maps to the difference
\ell-r
. If the result is
0
, then we have proven that the identity holds for arbitrary
C
and
k
. If not, we obtain no information. The main challenge now lies in finding and implementing simplification maps that are computationally feasible but still
“sufficiently powerful”
to cover all identities that we want to prove. The correctness of these simplification maps relies on the identities in
Remark

9.4
.

From this point, the implementation of a suitable object
\tilde{L}
is straightforward except for the zero component
\tilde{L}_{0,0}
. Again, we use (linear combinations of) abstract symbols
\zeta
,
\xi
and
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
for
a,a^{\prime}\in\tilde{J}
to represent elements of
\tilde{L}_{0,0}
and suitable
“simplification maps”
to (try to) prove equality of terms. These simplification maps rely on the identities in
Lemmas

10.10
,
10.9
and
10.13
. Finally, we may define exponential automorphisms by the formulas in
Section

7
and prove equality of products of such automorphisms by evaluating them on
\tilde{L}_{-2}\cup\tilde{L}_{1}
, whose image in
L
is a generating set of the Lie algebra.

In a few cases, the simplification maps do not reduce an element of
\tilde{C}
to
0
but instead to a rather short expression. In such cases, we simply prove by hand that the
“simplified”
expression lies in
\ker(\pi_{C})
for all possible choices of
C
(instead of implementing a potentially complicated simplification map which would prove this automatically).

Note that the strategy described in this section applies only to cubic Jordan matrix algebras and not to arbitrary cubic norm pairs. While a similar strategy could certainly work for cubic norm pairs, it would be more complicated to implement due to the higher number of maps that are part of the structure of a cubic norm pair. Moreover, the fact that the norm
N
is cubic and not merely quadratic would complicate this endeavor even further. At the same time, there is less need for computer assistance because the number of root spaces in
G_{2}
is more manageable.

We have also considered an approach using
“normal forms”
of elements of
\tilde{k}
,
\tilde{C}
and
\tilde{L}_{0,0}
instead of using simplification maps. To some extent, the simplification maps that we use transform elements
“of short (product) length”
to something close to a normal form, but the task of finding a normal form for arbitrary elements seems much more challenging.

10.
Refining the
G_{2}
-grading of the Lie algebra

We keep up the notation from
9.2
,
9.6
and
9.7
and denote by
L\coloneq L(J,J^{\prime})
the Lie algebra from
Section

4
with respect to the cubic norm pair
(J,J^{\prime})
coming from a cubic Jordan matrix algebra.
Our goal is to further refine the
G_{2}
-grading of
L
from
Proposition

4.28
to an
F_{4}
-grading.

10.1.
Construction of the refinement

In order to construct this refinement of the grading, we will first construct three new gradings.
They arise from the
“hyperbolic pairs”

(e_{i},e^{\prime}_{i})
, where the
e_{i}
and
e^{\prime}_{i}
are as in
Section

9
.
When
k
is a field, we can indeed directly follow the approach from
[
10
]
and construct the gradings from these hyperbolic pairs,
but in our setting, we will reverse the process by first describing the different pieces of the grading (
Definition

10.15
), and then verifying that this does indeed define a
5
-grading (
Proposition

10.21
).

The hardest part to control is the “middle” subspace
Z
(see
Definition

4.9
). We rely on the multiple Peirce decomposition to introduce certain subspaces
Z_{i\to j}
of
Z
and we examine how these subspaces interact with various components in
Lemmas

10.5
,
10.6
and
10.7
. In
Proposition

10.14
, we will shows that
Z_{i\to j}
is isomorphic to the conic algebra
C
.

Definition 10.1
.

(1)

For each subspace
A\leq J
and each subspace
A^{\prime}\leq J^{\prime}
, we write

Z(A,A^{\prime}):=\left\langle\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}\mid a\in A,a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\right\rangle\leq Z.

(2)

Using the Peirce subspaces
J_{ij}
from
Lemma

9.11
, we write

Z_{ij,lm}:=Z(J_{ij},J_{lm}^{\prime}),

for all
i,j,l,m\in\{1,2,3\}
. (We allow
i=j
or
l=m
.)

(3)

Finally, for all
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
, not necessarily distinct, we write

Z_{i\to j}:=Z_{i1,1j}+Z_{i2,2j}+Z_{i3,3j}.

Remark 10.2
.

Note that by
Lemma

9.11
and
Proposition

4.14
,
Z_{ij,lm}=0
if (and only if)
\{i,j\}\cap\{l,m\}=\emptyset
.

The following notation will be useful to avoid some case distinctions.

Definition 10.3
.

Let
I
be a subspace of
J
,
J^{\prime}
or
Z
and let
P
be a logical statement. We define
I^{P}\coloneq I
if
P
is true and
I^{P}\coloneq 0
if
P
is false.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
i,j,l,p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
be arbitrary.
Let
a\in J_{il}
and
a^{\prime}\in J_{lj}^{\prime}
.
Then:

(1)

If
i\neq j
, then

D_{a,a^{\prime}}(J_{pq})\subseteq J_{iq}^{p=j}+J_{ip}^{q=j}.

(2)

If
i=j
, then

D_{a,a^{\prime}}(J_{pq})\subseteq J_{pq}^{\{p,q\}\cap\{i,l\}\neq\emptyset}.

The same assertions hold if we interchange the roles of
J
and
J^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate corollary of
Lemma

9.11
.
∎

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
i,j,p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
with
i\neq j
. Then the following hold:

(1)

[Z_{i\to j},J_{pq}]\subseteq J_{iq}^{p=j}+J_{ip}^{q=j}
and
[Z_{i\to j},J_{pq}^{\prime}]\subseteq(J_{jq}^{\prime})^{p=i}+(J_{jp}^{\prime})^{q=i}
.

(2)

[Z_{i\to i},J_{pq}]\subseteq J_{pq}
and
[Z_{i\to i},J_{pq}^{\prime}]\subseteq J_{pq}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
i,j,p,q,l\in\{1,2,3\}
. (We do not assume that
i\neq j
.) Let
a\in J_{il}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{lj}
,
c_{pq}\in J_{pq}
and
c_{pq}^{\prime}\in J_{pq}^{\prime}
. By
Proposition

4.14
,

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},c_{pq}]=D_{a,a^{\prime}}(c_{pq})\quad\text{and}\quad[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},c_{pq}^{\prime}]=-D_{a^{\prime},a}(c_{pq}^{\prime}).

The assertion now follows from
Lemma

10.4
.
∎

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
i,j,p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
with
i\neq j
. Then the actions of
Z_{i\to j}
and
Z_{i\to i}
on
L_{\pm 1}
respect the following formulas:

(1)

[Z_{i\to j},(k,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},k)_{\pm}]\subseteq(0,J_{iq}^{p=j}+J_{ip}^{q=j},(J_{jq}^{\prime})^{p=i}+(J_{jp}^{\prime})^{q=i},0)_{\pm}
.

(2)

[Z_{i\to i},(0,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}]\subseteq(0,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions

4.12
and
10.5
. For the first assertion, we also use that for all
a\in J_{il}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{lj}
with
l\in\{1,2,3\}
(i.e., for the set of
(a,a^{\prime})
for which the corresponding
\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}}
generate
Z_{i\to j}
), we have
T(a,a^{\prime})=0
by
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
.
∎

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3\}
be arbitrary.

(1)

Z_{i\to j}
is a Lie subalgebra of
Z
, which is abelian if
i\neq j
.

(2)

[Z_{i\to j},Z_{j\to i}]\subseteq Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}
.

(3)

The Lie algebra
Z_{i\to i}
normalizes
Z_{j\to l}
, i.e.,
[Z_{i\to i},Z_{j\to l}]\subseteq Z_{j\to l}
.

(4)

[Z_{i\to j},Z_{j\to l}]\subseteq Z_{i\to l}
when
i,j,l
are pairwise distinct.

(5)

[Z_{i\to j},Z_{i\to l}]=0
and
[Z_{i\to j},Z_{l\to j}]=0
when
i,j,l
are pairwise distinct.

Proof.

Recall from
Corollary

4.13
that

(28)

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}]=\mathbf{d}_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b),b^{\prime}}-\mathbf{d}_{b,D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})}.

Throughout this proof, we will assume that
i,j,l,p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
are arbitrary.
We first observe that by
Lemma

10.4
Item

2
and
Eq.

28
,

Z_{i\to i}
normalizes each
Z_{jl,pq}
, so in particular,

(29)

[Z_{i\to i},Z_{p\to q}]\subseteq Z_{p\to q}.

(1)

Assume first that
i=j
.
Then by
Eq.

29
with
p=q=i
,
Z_{i\to i}
is indeed a Lie subalgebra of
Z
.

Assume next that
i\neq j
.
We have to show that, for all
a\in J_{ip}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{pj}^{\prime}
,
b\in J_{iq}
,
b^{\prime}\in J_{qj}^{\prime}
, we have
[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}]=0
.
By
Lemma

10.4
Item

1
, we have

D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b)\in J_{ii}^{q=j}\quad\text{and}\quad D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})\in{J_{jj}^{\prime}}^{q=i}.

By
Lemma

9.11
, however, we have
Z_{ii,jj}=0
, which implies that
\mathbf{d}_{D_{a,a^{\prime}}(b),b^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{d}_{b,D_{a^{\prime},a}(b^{\prime})}
are trivial also when
q=j
or
q=i
.

(2)

We may assume that
i\neq j
.
Let
a\in J_{ip}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{pj}^{\prime}
,
b\in J_{jq}
,
b^{\prime}\in J_{qi}^{\prime}
.
Then by
Lemma

10.4
Item

1
and
Eq.

28
, we have

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}]\in Z_{iq,qi}+Z_{jq,qj}\subseteq Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}.

(3)

This is
Eq.

29
.

(4)

Let
a\in J_{ip}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{pj}^{\prime}
,
b\in J_{jq}
,
b^{\prime}\in J_{ql}^{\prime}
.
Then by
Lemma

10.4
Item

1
and
Eq.

28
, we have

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}]\in Z_{iq,ql}+Z_{qj,jl}^{q=i}\subseteq Z_{i\to l}.

(5)

Let
a\in J_{ip}
,
a^{\prime}\in J_{pj}^{\prime}
,
b\in J_{iq}
,
b^{\prime}\in J_{ql}^{\prime}
.
Then by
Lemma

10.4
Item

1
and
Eq.

28
, we have

[\mathbf{d}_{a,a^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\prime}}]\in Z_{ii,ql}^{q=j}+Z_{iq,jl}^{q=i}\subseteq Z_{ii,jl}=0.

The proof of the other identity is completely similar.
∎

Our next goal is to show that each of the spaces
Z_{i\to j}
is isomorphic to the conic algebra
C
for
i\neq j
(
Proposition

10.14
). Clearly, we have a surjective homomorphism
C\to Z_{ii,ij}\colon a\mapsto\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},a[ij]^{\iota}}
(using that
i\neq j
), so the main property to show is that
Z_{ii,ij}=Z_{i\to j}
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
be distinct and let
a\in C
. Then

D(a[ij],1_{C}[ji]^{\iota})=D(1_{C}[ij],a[ji]^{\iota})\quad\text{and}\quad D(a[ij]^{\iota},1_{C}[ji])=D(1_{C}[ij]^{\iota},a[ji]).

Proof.

We only prove the first statement, the proof of the second one being similar. It is immediate from
Lemma

9.11
and
Lemma

9.15
Item

5
that both operators act in the same way on all components of
J
except possibly on
J_{ij}
. For the action on
J_{ij}
, we compute for arbitrary
b\in C
:

\displaystyle\{a[ij],1_{C}[ji]^{\iota},b[ij]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}\big(t(b)a+t(a)b-t(a\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)1_{C}\big)[ij],

\displaystyle\{1_{C}[ij],a[ji]^{\iota},b[ij]\}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}\big(t(ab)1_{C}+t(a)b-t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\big)[ij].

Recall from
Remark

9.4
Items

3
,
6
and
7
that
t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=t(b)
and

t(a\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=n(a,b)=t(a)t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)-n(a,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=t(a)t(b)-t(ab).

Hence the difference between the two expressions above is

\displaystyle t(b)a-t(a)t(b)1_{C}+t(b)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu,

which is zero because
t(a)1_{C}=a+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.
∎

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
be distinct and let
a\in C
. Then
\mathbf{d}(a[ij],1_{C}[ji]^{\iota})=\mathbf{d}(1_{C}[ij],a[ji]^{\iota})
.

Proof.

Since
T(a[ij],1_{C}[ji]^{\iota})=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}t(a)=T(1_{C}[ij],a[ji]^{\iota})
, this follows from
Propositions

4.12
and
10.8
.
∎

Lemma 10.10
.

Let
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3\}
be pairwise distinct and let
a,b\in C
. Then the following hold.

(1)

\mathbf{d}_{e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{3},e_{3}^{\prime}}=2\zeta-\xi
.

(2)

\mathbf{d}(e_{j},a[ji]^{\iota})=\mathbf{d}(a[ji],e_{i}^{\prime})
.

(3)

\begin{aligned} \mathbf{d}(a[jl],b[li]^{\iota})&=\mathbf{d}(1_{C}[jl],ab[li]^{\iota})=\mathbf{d}(ab[jl],1_{C}[li]^{\iota})\\
&=\gamma_{l}\mathbf{d}(e_{j},ab[ji]^{\iota})=\gamma_{l}\mathbf{d}(ab[ji],e_{i}^{\prime}).\end{aligned}

(4)

\mathbf{d}(a[ij],\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ji]^{\iota})=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}n(a)\big(\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},e_{i}^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{j},e_{j}^{\prime}}\big)
.

(5)

\mathbf{d}(a[ij],\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ji]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(b[ij],\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ji]^{\iota})=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}t(a\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mub\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\big(\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},e_{i}^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{j},e_{j}^{\prime}}\big)
.

Proof.

Recall from
Lemma

4.17
Item

5
that for all
p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{p^{\prime}\times q^{\prime},r^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{q^{\prime}\times r^{\prime},p^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{r^{\prime}\times p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}=T(r^{\prime},p^{\prime}\times q^{\prime})\cdot(2\zeta-\xi).

We will denote this term by
\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})
and prove the assertions by specializing to well-chosen values of
p^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}
.
We will also use
Lemmas

9.13
and
9.12
to compute terms such as
p^{\prime}\times\tilde{b^{\prime}}
or
T(r^{\prime},p^{\prime}\times q^{\prime})
. For
p^{\prime}\coloneq e_{1}^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}\coloneq e_{2}^{\prime}
, and
r^{\prime}\coloneq e_{3}^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}(e_{1}^{\prime}\times e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(e_{2}^{\prime}\times e_{3}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(e_{3}^{\prime}\times e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{3},e_{3}^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T(e_{3}^{\prime},e_{1}^{\prime}\times e_{2}^{\prime})(2\zeta-\xi)=T(e_{3}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime})(2\zeta-\xi)=2\zeta-\xi.

This implies
Item

1
. For
p^{\prime}\coloneq e_{l}^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}\coloneq e_{i}^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}\coloneq a[ji]^{\iota}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}(e_{l}^{\prime}\times e_{i}^{\prime},a[ji]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(e_{i}^{\prime}\times a[ji]^{\iota},e_{l}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(a[ji]^{\iota}\times e_{l}^{\prime},e_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}(e_{j},a[ji]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(0,e_{l}^{\prime})-\mathbf{d}(a[ji]^{\iota},e_{i}^{\prime})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T(a[ji]^{\iota},e_{l}^{\prime}\times e_{i}^{\prime})(2\zeta-\xi)=T(a[ji]^{\iota},e_{j})(2\zeta-\xi)=0.

Identity
Item

2
follows, which also implies the final equality sign in
Item

3
. For
p^{\prime}\coloneq e_{i}^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}\coloneq 1[jl]^{\iota}
,
r^{\prime}\coloneq ab[li]^{\iota}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}(e_{i}^{\prime}\times 1[jl]^{\iota},ab[li]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(1[jl]^{\iota}\times ab[li]^{\iota},e_{i}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(ab[li]^{\iota}\times e_{i}^{\prime},1[jl]^{\iota})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{d}(1[jl],ab[li]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(\gamma_{l}ab[ji],e_{i}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(0,1[jl]^{\iota})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T(ab[li]^{\iota},e_{i}^{\prime}\times 1[jl]^{\iota})(2\zeta-\xi)=T(ab[li]^{\iota},-1[jl])(2\zeta-\xi)=0.

It follows that
\mathbf{d}(1[jl],ab[li]^{\iota})=\gamma_{l}\mathbf{d}(ab[ji],e_{i}^{\prime})
. For
p^{\prime}\coloneq e_{i}^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}\coloneq ab[jl]^{\iota}
,
r^{\prime}\coloneq 1[li]^{\iota}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}(e_{i}^{\prime}\times ab[jl]^{\iota},1[li]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(ab[jl]^{\iota}\times 1[li]^{\iota},e_{i}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(1[li]^{\iota}\times e_{i}^{\prime},ab[jl]^{\iota})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{d}(ab[jl]^{\iota},1[li])+\mathbf{d}(\gamma_{l}ab[ji],e_{i}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(0,ab[jl])\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\varphi(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=T(1[li]^{\iota},e_{i}^{\prime}\times ab[jl])(2\zeta-\xi)=T(1[li]^{\iota},-ab[jl]^{\iota})(2\zeta-\xi)=0.

It follows that
\mathbf{d}(ab[jl],1[li])=\gamma_{l}\mathbf{d}(ab[ji],e_{i}^{\prime})
. For
p\coloneq a[jl]
,
q\coloneq b[li]
,
r\coloneq e_{j}\in J
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{p,q\times r}+\mathbf{d}_{q,r\times p}+\mathbf{d}_{r,p\times q}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{d}(a[jl],b[li]^{\iota})+\mathbf{d}(b[li],0)+\mathbf{d}(e_{j},\gamma_{l}ab[ji]),

\displaystyle T(r,p\times q)(2\zeta-\xi)

\displaystyle=T(e_{j},a[jl]\times b[li])(2\zeta-\xi)=T(e_{j},\gamma_{l}ab[ji])(2\zeta-\xi)=0.

Thus it follows from
Lemma

4.17
Item

6
that
\mathbf{d}(a[jl],b[li]^{\iota})=\gamma_{l}\mathbf{d}(e_{j},ab[ji])
, which finishes the proof of
Item

3
.

For
Item

4
, we put
p\coloneq e_{l}
and
q\coloneq a[ij]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{p,q^{\sharp}}+\mathbf{d}_{q,p\times q}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}(e_{l},-\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}n(a)e_{l}^{\prime})+\mathbf{d}(a[ij],-a[ij]^{\iota}),

\displaystyle T(p,q^{\sharp})(2\zeta-\xi)

\displaystyle=T(e_{l},-\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}n(a)e_{l})(2\zeta-\xi)=-\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}n(a)(2\zeta-\xi)

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}n(a)(\mathbf{d}_{e_{1},e_{1}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{2},e_{2}}+\mathbf{d}_{e_{3},e_{3}})

by
Item

1
. Now
Item

4
follows from
Lemma

4.17
Item

3
. Finally,
Item

5
is the linearization of
Item

4
.
∎

Remark 10.11
.

The identities in
Lemma

10.10
also hold with
D
in place of
\mathbf{d}
(and then as identities in
J
or
J^{\prime}
), except that
Item

1
should be read as

\sum_{i=1}^{3}D(e_{i},e_{i}^{\prime})=2\id_{J}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{3}D(e_{i}^{\prime},e_{i})=2\id_{J^{\prime}}.

Remark 10.12
.

Let
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3\}
be pairwise distinct. We want to show that we do
not
have
\mathbf{d}(a[il],b[li]^{\iota})=\mathbf{d}(1[il],ab[li]^{\iota})
in general. Suppose for a contradiction that
\mathbf{d}(a[il],b[li])=\mathbf{d}(1[il],ab[li]^{\iota})
for all
a,b\in C
, and let
a,b,c\in C
be arbitrary. Since

\displaystyle T(a[il],b[li])=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}t(ab)=T(ab[il],1[li])

by
Lemma

9.12
Item

2
, it follows from
Proposition

4.12
that

D(a[il],b[li])=D(1[il],ab[li]).

Thus

\displaystyle\{a[jl],b[li]^{\iota},c[il]\}

\displaystyle=\{c[il],b[li]^{\iota},a[jl]\}=\{1[il],cb[li]^{\iota},a[jl]\}

\displaystyle=\{a[jl],cb[li]^{\iota},1[il]\}=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{l}a(cb)[jl].

by
Lemma

9.15
Item

1
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\{a[jl],b[li]^{\iota},c[il]\}=\gamma_{i}\gamma_{l}(ab)c[jl]

Assuming additionally that
\gamma_{i}
,
\gamma_{j}
are invertible, it follows that
(ab)c=a(cb)
for all
a,b,c\in C
. In other words,
C
is commutative and associative, which is not the case for arbitrary multiplicative conic alternative algebras.

Lemma 10.13
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in C
. Then

\sum\gamma_{i}\mathbf{d}_{a_{i}[jl],\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[jl]}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}t(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})\cdot(2\zeta-\xi)

where the sum runs over all cyclic permutations
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.17
, we have

\sum\mathbf{d}_{a_{i}[jl],a_{j}[li]\times a_{l}[ij]}=T(a_{2}[31],a_{3}[12]\times a_{1}[23])\cdot(2\zeta-\xi).

Recall from
Lemma

9.14
Item

1
that

\displaystyle a_{i}[jl]\times a_{j}[li]=\gamma_{l}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ij]

for all cyclic permutations
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
. Hence

\sum\mathbf{d}_{a_{i}[jl],a_{j}[li]\times a_{l}[ij]}=\sum\gamma_{i}\mathbf{d}_{a_{i}[jl],\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{l}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{j}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[jl]}.

Further,

\displaystyle T(a_{2}[31],a_{3}[12]\times a_{1}[23])

\displaystyle=T(a_{2}[31],\gamma_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{1}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua_{3}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[31])=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}t(a_{2}a_{3}a_{1})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}t(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})

by
Lemma

9.14
Item

1
and
Lemma

9.12
Item

2
. The assertion follows.
∎

Proposition 10.14
.

Let
i\neq j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then the map

\theta\colon C\to Z_{i\to j},a\mapsto\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},a[ij]^{\iota}}

is an isomorphism of
k
-modules.

Proof.

The map is clearly a homomorphism and surjective by
Lemma

10.10
Items

2
and
3
.
Now let
a\in C
with
\theta(a)=0
. Then by
Proposition

4.12
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=[\theta(a),(0,e_{j},0,0)_{+}]=\big(0,D_{e_{i},a[ij]^{\iota}}(e_{j}),0,0\big)_{+}.

Since
D_{e_{i},a[ij]^{\iota}}(e_{j})=a[ij]
by
Lemma

9.15
Item

2
, we infer that
a=0
. Thus
\theta
is injective.
∎

We are now ready to define three new gradings on our Lie algebra
L
.
The verification that these are, in fact, gradings, will occupy us until
Proposition

10.21
.

Definition 10.15
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and let
(i\,j\,l)
be the unique cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
. We will define a decomposition

L=L^{(i)}_{-2}\oplus L^{(i)}_{-1}\oplus L^{(i)}_{0}\oplus L^{(i)}_{1}\oplus L^{(i)}_{2}

with
L^{(i)}_{-2}=ke^{\prime}_{i}
and
L^{(i)}_{2}=ke_{i}
.
We set

\displaystyle L^{(i)}_{-1}

\displaystyle:=\begin{multlined}0\ \oplus\ (k,\ ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl},\ ke_{i}^{\prime},\ 0)_{-}\\
\oplus\ \Bigl((J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime})\oplus Z^{(i)}_{-1}\oplus 0\Bigr)\\
\oplus\ (k,\ ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl},\ ke_{i}^{\prime},\ 0)_{+}\ \oplus\ 0\,,\end{multlined}

\displaystyle L^{(i)}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\begin{multlined}kx\ \oplus\ (0,\ J_{ij}+J_{li},\ J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime},\ 0)_{-}\\
\oplus\ \Bigl((ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime})\oplus Z^{(i)}_{0}\oplus(ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl})\Bigr)\\
\oplus\ (0,\ J_{ij}+J_{li},\ J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime},\ 0)_{+}\ \oplus\ ky\,,\end{multlined}

\displaystyle L^{(i)}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\begin{multlined}0\ \oplus\ (0,\ ke_{i},\ ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime},\ k)_{-}\\
\oplus\ \Bigl(0\oplus Z^{(i)}_{1}\oplus(J_{ij}+J_{li})\Bigr)\\
\oplus\ (0,\ ke_{i},\ ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime},\ k)_{+}\ \oplus\ 0\,,\end{multlined}

where

\displaystyle Z^{(i)}_{-1}

\displaystyle:=Z_{j\to i}+Z_{l\to i}\,,

\displaystyle Z^{(i)}_{0}

\displaystyle:=Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}+Z_{l\to l}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}+k\xi+k\zeta,

\displaystyle Z^{(i)}_{1}

\displaystyle:=Z_{i\to j}+Z_{i\to l}.

10.2.
Verification of the grading property

Proposition 10.16
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then:

(1)

We have
Z=Z_{-1}^{(i)}\oplus Z_{0}^{(i)}\oplus Z_{1}^{(i)}
as
k
-modules.

(2)

The decomposition from
Item

1
defines a
3
-grading on
Z
.

Proof.

Let
(i\,j\,l)
denote the unique cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
starting from
i
. By
Definition

4.9
, we have
Z=Z_{-1}^{(i)}+Z_{0}^{(i)}+Z_{1}^{(i)}
. Now let
d_{-1}\in Z_{-1}^{(i)}
,
d_{0}\in Z_{0}^{(i)}
and
d_{1}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
such that
d_{-1}+d_{0}+d_{1}=0
; we have to show that
d_{-1}=d_{0}=d_{1}=0
. Recall from
Definition

4.1
that an element of
M
is zero if and only if it acts trivially on
\bigoplus_{m\neq 0}L_{m}
. By
Proposition

4.12
,
d_{-1}
and
d_{1}
act trivially on
L_{\pm 2}
, so
d_{0}=-d_{-1}-d_{1}
does the same. Similarly,
d_{-1}
and
d_{1}
act trivially on the subspace
(k,0,0,k)_{\pm}
of
L_{\pm 1}
by
Lemma

10.6
Item

1
, so the same holds for
d_{0}
.

It remains to show that for all
p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
,
d_{-1}
,
d_{1}
and
d_{0}
act trivially on the subspaces
(0,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
of
L_{\pm 1}
. It follows from
Lemma

10.6
and
Definition

4.1
Item

4
that
d_{-1}
,
d_{1}
and
d_{0}
map
(0,J_{ii},J_{ii}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
to

(0,J_{ij}+J_{il},0,0)_{\pm},\qquad(0,0,J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{il}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\quad\text{and}\quad(0,J_{ii},J_{ii}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

respectively. Since the sum of these three spaces is direct and
d_{-1}+d_{0}+d_{1}=0
, we deduce that
d_{-1}
,
d_{1}
and
d_{0}
all act trivially on
(0,J_{ii},J_{ii}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
.

Similarly,
d_{-1}
,
d_{1}
and
d_{0}
map the spaces
(0,J_{pp},J_{pp}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
,
(0,J_{ip},J_{ip}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
and
(0,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
(for any
(p,q)\in\{(j,l),(l,j)\}
) to

\displaystyle(0,0,J_{pi}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

\displaystyle(0,J_{pi},0,0)_{\pm},

\displaystyle(0,J_{pp}+J_{pq},J_{pp}^{\prime}+J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

\displaystyle(0,J_{pp}+J_{pq},J_{ii}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

\displaystyle(0,J_{ii},J_{pp}^{\prime}+J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

\displaystyle(0,J_{ip}+J_{iq},J_{ip}^{\prime}+J_{iq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

\displaystyle(0,0,J_{iq}^{\prime}+J_{pi}^{\prime},0),

\displaystyle(0,J_{iq}+J_{pi},0,0),

\displaystyle(0,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm},

respectively. Again, the three subspaces in each row form a direct sum, and we conclude that
d_{-1}
,
d_{1}
and
d_{0}
act trivially on each of the subspaces of the form
(0,J_{pq},J_{pq}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
with
p,q\in\{1,2,3\}
.
It follows that indeed
d_{-1}=d_{0}=d_{1}=0
, and this proves
Item

1
.
Now
Item

2
follows immediately from
Lemmas

10.7
and
4.11
.
∎

Lemma 10.17
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and let
(i\,j\,l)
be the unique cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
starting from
i
.
Consider the
k
-module homomorphisms

\theta^{(i)}_{\pm}\colon L_{\pm 1}\to L_{\pm 1}\colon z\mapsto[e_{i},z].

Let
z=(\lambda,b,b^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\in L_{\pm 1}
and write

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}e_{1}+\xi_{2}e_{2}+\xi_{3}e_{3}+c_{23}+c_{31}+c_{12}\in J,

\displaystyle b^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\xi_{1}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}+\xi_{2}^{\prime}e_{2}^{\prime}+\xi_{3}^{\prime}e_{3}^{\prime}+c_{23}^{\prime}+c_{31}^{\prime}+c_{12}^{\prime}\in J^{\prime},

with each
\xi_{i},\xi_{i}^{\prime}\in k
and each
c_{jl}\in J_{jl}
,
c_{jl}^{\prime}\in J_{jl}^{\prime}
.
Then

[e_{i},z]=\bigl(0,\ \lambda e_{i},\ \xi_{l}e_{j}^{\prime}+\xi_{j}e_{l}^{\prime}-c_{jl}^{\iota},\ \xi_{i}^{\prime}\bigr)_{\pm}.

In particular, the image and kernel of
\theta^{(i)}_{\pm}
are given by

\displaystyle\im\theta^{(i)}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ ke_{i},\ ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime},\ k\bigr)_{\pm}\leq L_{1}^{(i)},

\displaystyle\ker\theta^{(i)}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ ke_{i}+J_{ij}+J_{li},\ ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime},\ k\bigr)_{\pm}\leq L_{0}^{(i)}+L_{1}^{(i)},

respectively.

Proof.

By
Lemma

9.12
, we have

e_{i}\times b=\xi_{l}e_{j}^{\prime}+\xi_{j}e_{l}^{\prime}-c_{jl}^{\iota}\quad\text{and}\quad T(e_{i},b^{\prime})=\xi_{i}^{\prime}.

Therefore, by
Definition

4.7
, we have

\displaystyle[e_{i},z]

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ \lambda e_{i},\ e_{i}\times b,\ T(e_{i},b^{\prime})\bigr)_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ \lambda e_{i},\ \xi_{l}e_{j}^{\prime}+\xi_{j}e_{l}^{\prime}-c_{jl}^{\iota},\ \xi_{i}^{\prime}\bigr)_{\pm},

and the result follows.
∎

We now arrive at a sequence of lemmas dealing with some of the more involved cases required to check that
Definition

10.15
gives rise to
5
-gradings of
L
. We advise the reader to have a copy of
Definition

10.15
ready, to be able to follow our computations.
The different cases and subcases in our proofs correspond to the different
L_{i}
-components for each of the three subspaces occuring in
Definition

10.15
.

Lemma 10.18
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then
[L_{-1}^{(i)},L_{1}^{(i)}]\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

Let
(i\,j\,l)
denote the unique cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
starting from
i
. Let
z_{1}\in L_{-1}^{(i)}
and
z_{2}\in L_{1}^{(i)}
. We will write

z_{1}=(\lambda,b,\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\quad\text{or}\quad z_{2}=(0,\mu_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}

if
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
, respectively, lies in
L_{\pm 1}
, where
\lambda,\lambda_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{i},\mu\in k
,
b\in ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
and
c^{\prime}\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
.

First, we consider the case that
z_{1}\in L_{\pm 1}
. As the first subcase, we assume that
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
as well. If
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are both in
L_{1}
or both in
L_{-1}
, then
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in ky\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}
or
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in kx\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}
, respectively. If
z_{1}\in L_{-1}
and
z_{2}\in L_{1}
, then by
Definition

4.22
,

[z_{1},z_{2}]=\big(-\lambda c^{\prime}+b\times\mu_{i}e_{i}\big)\\
\begin{aligned} &+\big((\lambda\mu-T(b,c^{\prime}))\zeta+T(\mu_{i}e_{i},\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime})(\xi-\zeta)+\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{\mu_{i}e_{i},\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime}}\big)\\
&+\big(-\mu b+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}\big).\end{aligned}

Now

\displaystyle b\times\mu_{i}e_{i}\in ke_{l}^{\prime}+ke_{j}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}\in ke_{l}+ke_{j}+J_{jl},

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}\in Z_{j\to j}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to l}+Z_{l\to j}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mathbf{d}_{\mu_{i}e_{i},\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime}}\in Z_{i\to i},

by
Lemma

9.13
Item

1
and
Definition

10.1
, respectively. Hence
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
in this subcase. The subcase
z_{1}\in L_{1}
,
z_{2}\in L_{-1}
is completely similar.

We now turn to the subcase
z_{2}\in L_{0}
. If
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
, then by
Definition

4.7
,

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=-[z_{2},z_{1}]=-\big(0,\lambda z_{2},z_{2}\times b,T(z_{2},\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime})\big)

where
z_{2}\times b\in J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{il}^{\prime}
by
Lemma

9.14
and
T(z_{2},\lambda_{i}^{\prime}e_{i}^{\prime})=0
by
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
. Hence
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
in this subcase. In the final subcase
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, we have

[z_{1},z_{2}]\in(0,J_{ij}+J_{il},J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{il}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}

by
Lemma

10.6
Item

1
.

Second, we consider the case that
z_{1}\in J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime}
. If
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
, then
Definition

4.7
yields

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=-\big(T(\mu_{i}e_{i},z_{1}),z_{1}\times c^{\prime},\mu z_{1},0\big)_{\pm}

where
T(\mu_{i}e_{i},z_{1})=0
by
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
and
z_{1}\times c^{\prime}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
by
Lemma

9.14
. If
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
, then

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{z_{2},z_{1}}\in Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}.

If
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, then by
Lemma

10.5
Item

1
,
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in J_{jj}^{\prime}+J_{ll}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}
, which finishes the case
z_{1}\in J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime}
.

Finally, we assume that
z_{1}\in Z_{-1}^{(i)}
. If
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
, then we infer from
Lemma

10.6
Item

1
that

[z_{1},z_{2}]\in(0,J_{ji}+J_{li},J_{ji}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}.

If
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
, then
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in J_{jj}+J_{ll}+J_{lj}
by
Lemma

10.5
Item

1
.
If finally
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, then

[z_{1},z_{2}]\in Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}+Z_{l\to l}\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}

by
Lemma

10.7
(as we already observed in
Proposition

10.16
Item

2
).
∎

Lemma 10.19
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then
[L_{0}^{(i)},L_{1}^{(i)}]\subseteq L_{1}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

Let
(i\,j\,l)
denote the unique cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
starting from
i
. Let
z_{1}\in L_{0}^{(i)}
and
z_{2}\in L_{1}^{(i)}
. We will write

z_{1}=(0,b_{ij}+b_{li},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\quad\text{or}\quad z_{2}=(0,\mu_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}

if
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
, respectively, lies in
L_{\pm 1}
, where
\mu_{i},\mu\in k
,
b_{ij}\in J_{ij}
,
b_{li}\in J_{li}
,
b_{ij}^{\prime}\in J_{ij}^{\prime}
,
b_{li}^{\prime}\in J_{li}^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
.

First assume that
z_{1}\in\{x,y\}
. If
z_{2}=(0,\mu_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}\in L_{\pm 1}
, then

[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\{0,(0,\mu_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime},\mu)_{\mp},-(0,\mu_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime},\mu)_{\mp}\}\subseteq L_{1}^{(i)}

by
Definition

4.22
. If
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
or
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, we have
[z_{1},z_{2}]=0
by
Definition

4.7
or
Proposition

4.12
, respectively.

Second, we consider the case
z_{1}\in L_{\pm 1}
. We begin with the subcase
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
. If
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
both lie in
L_{1}
, then by
Definition

4.22
and
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
,

[z_{1},z_{2}]=\big(T(b_{ij}+b_{li},c^{\prime})-T(\mu_{i}e_{i},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime})\big)x=(0-0)x=0.

The same holds if
z_{1},z_{2}
both lie in
L_{-1}
. If
z_{1}\in L_{-1}
and
z_{2}\in L_{1}
, then
Definition

4.22
gives us

[z_{1},z_{2}]=(b_{ij}+b_{li})\times\mu_{i}e_{i}\\
\begin{aligned} &+\big(-T(b_{ij}+b_{li},c^{\prime})\zeta+T(\mu_{i}e_{i},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime})(\xi-\zeta)+\mathbf{d}_{b_{ij}+b_{li},c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{\mu_{i}e_{i},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime}}\big)\\
&+\big(-\mu(b_{ij}+b_{li})+(b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime})\times c^{\prime}\big).\end{aligned}

Now

\displaystyle(b_{ij}+b_{li})\times\mu_{i}e_{i}=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad(b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime})\times c^{\prime}\in J_{ij}+J_{il},

\displaystyle T(b_{ij}+b_{li},c^{\prime})=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad T(\mu_{i}e_{i},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime})=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{b_{ij}+b_{li},c^{\prime}}\in Z_{i\to j}+Z_{i\to l}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mathbf{d}_{\mu_{i}e_{i},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime}}\in Z_{i\to j}+Z_{i\to l},

by
Lemma

9.14
,
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
and
Definition

10.1
, respectively, so
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{1}^{(i)}
.
The case
z_{1}\in L_{1}
and
z_{2}\in L_{-1}
is similar.

We continue with the subcase
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
. By
Definition

4.7
,

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=-[z_{2},(0,b_{ij}+b_{li},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime},0)]=-\big(0,0,z_{2}\times(b_{ij}+b_{li}),T(z_{2},b_{ij}^{\prime}+b_{li}^{\prime})\big)_{\pm},

which is an element of
L_{1}^{(i)}
because
z_{2}\times(b_{ij}+b_{li})\in J_{ll}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}+J_{jj}^{\prime}
by
Lemma

9.14
.
In the last subcase
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, we have

[z_{1},z_{2}]\in(0,J_{ii},J_{jj}^{\prime}+J_{ll}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\subseteq L_{1}^{(i)}

by
Lemma

10.6
Item

1
.

Third, we turn to the case
z_{1}\in ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
, and we simultaneously consider the opposite case of an element
z_{1}^{\prime}\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}
. If
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
, then
Definition

4.7
yields

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=\big(0,0,z_{1}\times\mu_{i}e_{i},T(z_{1},c^{\prime})\big)_{\pm},\quad[z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}]=-\big(T(\mu_{i}e_{i},z_{1}^{\prime}),z_{1}^{\prime}\times c^{\prime},\mu z_{1}^{\prime},0\big)_{\pm}

where
z_{1}\times\mu_{i}e_{i}\in J_{ll}^{\prime}+J_{jj}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
and
z_{1}^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}\in J_{ii}
by
Lemma

9.14
and
T(\mu_{i}e_{i},z_{1}^{\prime})=0
by
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
. Hence
[z_{1},z_{2}],[z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}]\in L_{1}^{(i)}
in this subcase. If
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
, then
[z_{1},z_{2}]=0
because
J
is abelian and
[z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}]=\mathbf{d}_{z_{2},z_{1}^{\prime}}\in Z_{i\to j}+Z_{i\to l}\in L_{1}^{(i)}
. If
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, then it follows from
Lemma

10.5
that
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in J_{ij}+J_{il}
and
[z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}]=0
.

Finally, we assume that
z_{1}\in Z_{0}^{(i)}
. By
Definition

4.1
Item

4
and
Remark

4.11
, the assertion is satisfied for
z_{1}\in\{\xi,\zeta\}
, so we may assume that

z_{1}\in Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}+Z_{l\to l}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}.

If
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
, then

[z_{1},z_{2}]\in(0,ke_{i},ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime},k)_{\pm}\subseteq L_{1}^{(i)}

by
Lemma

10.6
. If
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
, then
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
by
Lemma

10.5
. In the final subcase
z_{2}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
, we have
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
by
Lemma

10.7
(as we already observed in
Proposition

10.16
Item

2
).
∎

Lemma 10.20
.

Let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then
[L_{0}^{(i)},L_{0}^{(i)}]\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

Let
(i\,j\,l)
denote the unique cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
starting from
i
. Let
z_{1},z_{2}\in L_{0}^{(i)}
. We will write

z_{1}=(0,b,b^{\prime},0)_{\pm}\quad\text{or}\quad z_{2}=(0,c,c^{\prime},0)_{\pm}

if
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
, respectively, lies in
L_{\pm 1}
, where
b,c\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
and
b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime}
.

First, we consider the case
z_{1}\in\{x,y\}
. We have
[x,x]=0=[y,y]
and
[x,y]=\xi
by
Definition

4.22
.
By
Definition

4.1
Item

4
and
Definition

4.7
, we have
[x,L_{0}]\leq kx
and
[y,L_{0}]\leq ky
.
This already covers the subcases
z_{2}\in\{x,y\}
and
z_{2}\in L_{0}
. For
z_{2}\in L_{1}
, we have
[y,z_{2}]=0
and
[x,z_{2}]=-(0,c,c^{\prime},0)_{-}\in L_{0}^{(i)}
by
Definition

4.22
. The subcase
z_{2}\in L_{-1}
is completely similar.

Second, we assume that
z_{1}\in L_{\pm 1}
. We begin with the subcase
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
.
Definition

4.22
yields that
[z_{1},z_{2}]
lies in
kx
or
ky
if
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
are both in
L_{1}
or both in
L_{-1}
, and that

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=b\times c+\big(-T(b,c^{\prime})\zeta+T(c,b^{\prime})(\xi-\zeta)+\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\big)+b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}

if
z_{1}\in L_{-1}
and
z_{2}\in L_{1}
. Now
b\times c\in J_{jj}^{\prime}+J_{ll}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}\in J_{jj}+J_{ll}+J_{jl}
by
Lemma

9.14
and
\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\prime}}\in Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}
by
Definition

10.1
, so
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
.

We now turn to the subcase
z_{2}\in ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
and we simultaneously consider the subcase
z_{2}^{\prime}\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
. By
Definition

4.7
,

\displaystyle[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle=-\big(0,0,z_{2}\times b,T(z_{2},b^{\prime})\big)_{\pm},\quad[z_{1},z_{2}^{\prime}]=\big(T(b,z_{2}^{\prime}),z_{2}^{\prime}\times b^{\prime},0,0\big)_{\pm}

where
T(z_{2},b^{\prime})=0
,
T(b,z_{2}^{\prime})=0
by
Lemma

9.12
and
z_{2}\times b\in J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{il}^{\prime}
,
z_{2}^{\prime}\times b^{\prime}\in J_{ij}+J_{il}
by
Lemma

9.14
. Hence
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
. Finally, if
z_{2}\in Z_{0}^{(i)}
, then
\ad_{z_{2}}
leaves
(0,J_{ij}+J_{li},J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime},0)_{\pm}
invariant by
Definitions

4.1
and
10.6
, so that
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
.

Third, we consider the cases
z_{1}\in ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
and
z_{1}^{\prime}\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
. For
z_{2}\in ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
, we have
[z_{1},z_{2}]=0
because
J
is abelian and

[z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}]=\mathbf{d}_{z_{2},z_{1}^{\prime}}\in Z_{j\to j}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}+Z_{l\to l}\subseteq L_{0}^{(i)}.

If
z_{2}\in Z_{0}^{(i)}
, then
\ad_{z_{2}}
leaves
ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
and
ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
invariant by
Remarks

4.11
and
10.5
, which implies
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
.

Fourth, in the case
z_{1}\in\{\xi,\zeta\}
and
z_{2}\in Z_{0}^{(i)}
, we have
[z_{1},z_{2}]=0
by
Remark

4.11
. Finally,
Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}+Z_{l\to l}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}
is a Lie subalgebra of
L
by
Lemma

10.7
, so
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in L_{0}^{(i)}
in each possible case.
∎

Proposition 10.21
.

For each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, the decomposition
L=\bigoplus_{p=-2}^{2}L^{(i)}_{p}
defines a
5
-grading of
L
.

Proof.

Denote the cyclic permutation of
(1\,2\,3)
starting with
i
by
(i\,j\,l)
.
We have to check that
[L^{(i)}_{p},L^{(i)}_{q}]\subseteq L^{(i)}_{p+q}
for all possible pairs
(p,q)
with
-2\leq p\leq q\leq 2
. This will, of course, require a case distinction. There is some obvious symmetry in the definitions, and the cases
(-1,1)
,
(0,1)
and
(0,0)
are already covered by
Lemmas

10.18
,
10.19
and
10.20
, leaving us with the following
6
cases for
(p,q)
to check:

\displaystyle(2,2),

\displaystyle(1,2),

\displaystyle(0,2),

\displaystyle(-1,2),

\displaystyle(-2,2)

\displaystyle(1,1).

Case
(2,2)
:

This case is trivial because
[e_{i},e_{i}]=0
.

Case
(1,2)
:

Let
z\in L^{(i)}_{1}
.
We have to check that
[z,e_{i}]=0
.
If
z\in L_{\pm 1}
, this holds by
Lemma

10.17
.
If
z\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
, it holds because
[J,J]=0
.
Finally, if
z\in Z_{i\to j}+Z_{i\to l}
, it holds by
Lemma

10.5
Item

1
with
p=q=i
.

Case
(0,2)
:

Let
z\in L^{(i)}_{0}
.
In most cases, we have
[z,e_{i}]=0
:
By
Definition

4.7
if
z\in kx+ky
;
by
Lemma

10.17
if
z\in L_{\pm 1}
;
by
Remark

10.2
if
z\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
;
by
Remark

4.11
if
z=\xi
;
and finally, because of
[J,J]=0
if
z\in ke_{j}+ke_{l}+J_{jl}
.
If
z\in Z_{i\to i}+Z_{j\to j}+Z_{l\to l}+Z_{j\to l}+Z_{l\to j}
, then
[z,e_{i}]\in ke_{i}
by
Lemma

10.5
.
Finally,
[\zeta,e_{i}]=e_{i}
by
Remark

4.11
, so
[z,e_{i}]\in ke_{i}=L^{(i)}_{2}
in all cases.

Case
(-1,2)
:

Let
z\in L^{(i)}_{-1}
.
If
z\in L_{\pm 1}
, then
[z,e_{i}]\in L_{1}^{(i)}
by
Lemma

10.17
.
If
z\in J_{ij}^{\prime}+J_{li}^{\prime}
, then

[z,e_{i}]=\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},z}\in Z_{ii,ij}+Z_{ii,il}\subseteq Z_{i\to j}+Z_{i\to l}=Z_{1}^{(i)}.

Finally, in the case
z\in Z^{(i)}_{-1}=Z_{j\to i}+Z_{l\to i}
, we have
[z,e_{i}]\in J_{ij}+J_{li}\subseteq L^{(i)}_{1}
by
Lemma

10.5
Item

1
.

Case
(-2,2)
:

This follows from the fact that
[e_{i}^{\prime},e_{i}]=\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},e_{i}^{\prime}}\in Z_{i\to i}\leq L^{(i)}_{0}
.

Case
(1,1)
:

Let
z_{1},z_{2}\in L_{1}^{(i)}
. We will write

z_{1}=(0,\lambda_{i}e_{i},b^{\prime},\lambda)_{\pm}\quad\text{or}\quad z_{2}=(0,\mu_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime},\mu)_{\pm}

if
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
, respectively, lies in
L_{\pm 1}
, where
\lambda_{i},\lambda,\mu_{i},\mu\in k
and
b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in ke_{j}^{\prime}+ke_{l}^{\prime}+J_{jl}^{\prime}
. In the case
z_{1},z_{2}\in L_{1}
, we have

[z_{1},z_{2}]=\big(T(\lambda_{i}e_{i},c^{\prime})-T(\mu_{i}e_{i},b^{\prime})\big)x=0

by
Definition

4.22
and
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
. The case
z_{1},z_{2}\in L_{-1}
is completely similar. If
z_{1}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
and
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
, then

[z_{1},z_{2}]=\big(0,0,z_{1}\times\mu_{i}e_{i},T(z_{1},c^{\prime})\big)_{\pm}=0

by
Definition

4.7
,
Lemma

9.14
and
Lemma

9.12
Item

3
. In the case
z_{1}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
,
z_{2}\in L_{\pm 1}
, we have
[z_{1},z_{2}]=0
by
Lemma

10.6
Item

1
. Finally, assume that
z_{1},z_{2}\in L_{0}
. Since
J
and
Z_{1}^{(i)}
are abelian, the latter by
Lemma

10.7
Item

1
, we may assume that
z_{1}\in Z_{1}^{(i)}
and
z_{2}\in J_{ij}+J_{li}
. Then
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in J_{ii}=L_{2}^{(i)}
by
Lemma

10.5
Item

1
.
∎

We now obtain our promised
F_{4}
-grading.

Remark 10.22
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}
denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
and put

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\pm 2e_{i}\mid i\in\{1,2,3,4\}\}\sqcup\{\epsilon_{1}e_{i}+\epsilon_{2}e_{j}\mid i\neq j\in\{1,2,3,4\},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}\}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\sqcup\{\epsilon_{1}e_{1}+\epsilon_{2}e_{2}+\epsilon_{3}e_{3}+\epsilon_{4}e_{4}\mid\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{4}\in\{\pm 1\}\}

and
\Phi^{0}\coloneq\Phi\cup\{(0,0,0,0)\}
. Further, let
(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{4})
be a standard system of simple roots in
F_{4}
(in the sense of
Definition

1.9
). Then there exists a unique group homomorphism
\omega\colon\mathbb{Z}F_{4}\to\mathbb{Z}^{4}
mapping

\delta_{1}\mapsto(1,1,-1,-1),\quad\delta_{2}\mapsto(-2,0,0,0),\quad\delta_{3}\mapsto(1,-1,0,0),\quad\delta_{4}\mapsto(0,1,1,0).

A straightforward computation shows that
\omega(\Phi)=F_{4}
. As we will now see in
Theorem

10.23
, the set
\Phi
is the natural description of the root system
F_{4}
in our context. Notice that this description is dual to the one in
[
8
, VI.4.9]
, but since
F_{4}
is a self-dual root system, this difference is irrelevant. We will occasionally identify
F_{4}^{0}
with the set
\Phi^{0}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{4}
to simplify notation, similarly as we did for
G_{2}
(see
Remark

4.4
).

Theorem 10.23
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring, let
C
be a multiplicative conic alternative algebra, let
\Gamma\in k^{3}
and let
(J,J^{\prime})
be the cubic norm pair induced by the cubic Jordan matrix algebra
\Her_{3}(C,\Gamma)
. Let
L=L(J,J^{\prime})
be the Lie algebra constructed in
Section

4
, let
\Phi,\Phi^{0}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{4}
be as in
Remark

10.22
and define
L_{p}^{(i)}
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
p\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
as in
Definition

10.15
.
For all
p,i,j,l\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
(in particular, for all
(p,i,j,l)\in\Phi^{0}
), we put

L_{pijl}\coloneq L_{p}\cap L_{i}^{(1)}\cap L_{j}^{(2)}\cap L_{l}^{(3)}.

Then
(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi^{0}}
is an
F_{4}
-grading of
L
with

L_{\alpha}\cong\begin{cases}(k,+)&\text{if }\alpha\text{ is long,}\\
(C,+)&\text{if }\alpha\text{ is short}\end{cases}

for all
\alpha\in F_{4}
. Further, if
J\neq 0\neq J^{\prime}
, then

\Phi^{0}=\{(p,i,j,l)\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}^{4}\mid L_{pijl}\neq 0\}.

Proof.

By examining the gradings introduced in
Definition

10.15
, it is not difficult to compute their intersections.
We have summarized the result in
Fig.

3
, where we have written
\overline{2}:=-2
and
\overline{1}:=-1
to save some space, and where we have written
J
and
J^{\prime}
in their “matrix form”

J=\begin{pmatrix}ke_{1}&J_{12}&J_{13}\\
&ke_{2}&J_{23}\\
&&ke_{3}\end{pmatrix},\qquad J^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}ke_{1}^{\prime}&J_{12}^{\prime}&J_{13}^{\prime}\\
&ke_{2}^{\prime}&J_{23}^{\prime}\\
&&ke_{3}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

The middle component
Z
decomposes into
7
parts: there are
6
components of the form
Z_{i\to j}
, which we denote as
Z_{0p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}
with
p_{i}=1
,
p_{j}=-1
and the remaining
p_{l}=0
;
the seventh component is
Z_{0000}=Z_{1\to 1}+Z_{2\to 2}+Z_{3\to 3}+k\xi+k\zeta
.

It is now a simple observation that
\Phi^{0}
coincides with the set of index tuples
(p,i,j,l)
for which
L_{pijl}
is
“generically non-trivial”
, that is, non-zero except possibly in the degenerate case that
J=0
or
J^{\prime}=0
.

Since each of the four gradings that we have used for the labelling is indeed a grading (by
Propositions

4.27
and
10.21
), this automatically implies that the resulting decomposition of the whole Lie algebra
L
is a
\Phi
-grading and hence an
F_{4}
-grading.
∎
