Figure 3.
Intersecting the four gradings

Remark 10.24
.

As in
Remarks

4.4
and
10.22
, we identify
G_{2}^{0}
and
F_{4}^{0}
with subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
, respectively, and we consider the systems of simple roots

\displaystyle g_{1}=(1,0),\qquad g_{2}=(-2,-1)\in G_{2},

\displaystyle f_{1}=(1,1,-1,-1),\quad f_{2}=(-2,0,0,0),\quad f_{3}=(1,-1,0,0),\quad f_{4}=(0,1,1,0)\in F_{4}.

Then the map
\pi\colon F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
from
Lemma

8.4
(defined with respect to the systems
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4})
and
(g_{1},g_{2})
of simple roots) is described by
Fig.

3
: For any
\alpha\in F_{4}^{0}
,
\pi(\alpha)
is given by the coordinates in the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-grid of the rectangular block in which
\alpha
is contained. This implies that the
G_{2}
-graded constructed from the
F_{4}
-grading
(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}^{0}}
as in
Proposition

8.10
is precisely the
G_{2}
-grading
(L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}^{0}}
from
Proposition

4.28
. Note that we can also describe
\pi
by the formula

\pi\big((p,i,j,l)\big)=\left(p,\,\frac{p+i+j+l}{2}\right)

for all
(p,i,j,l)\in F_{4}
.

Remark 10.25

(cf.
8.8
)
.

Keep the notation of
Remark

10.24
and let
\beta\in G_{2}
be short. Then by
Lemma

8.7
Item

4
, the preimage
C_{\beta}\coloneq\pi^{-1}(\{\beta,-\beta,0\})=C_{-\beta}
is a subsystem of
F_{4}
of type
C_{3}
. Thus
L^{\prime}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\alpha\in C_{\beta}}L_{\alpha}
is a
C_{3}
-graded subalgebra of
L
. Specifically, the three possibilities for
L^{\prime}
are

L_{-1,0}\oplus L_{0,0}\oplus L_{1,0},\quad L_{-1,-1}\oplus L_{0,0}\oplus L_{1,1},\quad L_{0,-1}\oplus L_{0,0}\oplus L_{0,1}.

10.3.
A Chevalley-type basis of the Lie algebra

By specializing the commutator formulas in
Table

1
to the case that
J
is a cubic Jordan matrix algebra, we obtain commutator formulas for the
F_{4}
-graded Lie algebra from
Theorem

10.23
. For example,

[(0,c[12],0,0)_{-},(0,0,d[12]^{\iota},0)_{-}]=T(c[12],d[12]^{\iota})x=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}t(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mud)

for all
c,d\in C
by
Lemma

9.12
Item

2
. It is not surprising that the constants
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
,
\gamma_{3}
may appear in these formulas because the structural maps of
(J,J^{\prime})
depend on them. However, we have already observed in
Remark

9.8
that the isomorphism type of the Lie algebra
L(J,J^{\prime})
does not depend on
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
as long as they are assumed to be invertible. Hence one should expect that, under this assumption, there exists a family
(\vartheta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
of root homomorphisms (each with domain
k
or
C
and codomain
L_{\alpha}
) such that the commutator relations with respect to this family do not involve
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
,
\gamma_{3}
. The goal of this subsection is precisely to find such a family of root homomorphisms. We construct this family by multiplying the
“natural”
root homomorphisms in
L
by appropriate products of
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
,
\gamma_{3}
and their inverses.

We will also observe that the elements
(\vartheta_{\alpha}(1))_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
defined by our root homomorphisms share several crucial properties with Chevalley bases of complex semisimple Lie algebras. To achieve this, some root homomorphisms will have to be multiplied not only with a product of
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
,
\gamma_{3}
and their inverses, but with
-1
as well. An immediate consequence of the existence of a
“Chevalley-type basis”
is that if
C
is faithful as a
k
-algebra, then
L
contains a copy of the split Chevalley Lie algebra of type
F_{4}
over
k
as a subalgebra, which implies that
L
is
F_{4}
-graded in the stronger sense of
[
6
]
. See
Remarks

1.3
and
10.34
for details.

In
Section

11
, we will
“exponentiate”
the root homomorphisms from this subsection to obtain root homomorphisms in the corresponding group of automorphisms of
L(J,J^{\prime})
(which will then proven to be
F_{4}
-graded as well). Consequently, the commutator formulas in this
F_{4}
-graded group will not involve
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
either (see
Proposition

11.13
). This aligns with the fact that in the coordinatization theorem for arbitrary
F_{4}
-graded groups (
Theorem

1.15
), the algebraic data coordinatising the group involves only a commutative ring and a multiplicative conic alternative algebra but no choice of constants in the commutative ring. Further, using a Chevalley-type basis has benefits concerning the description of Weyl elements in the automorphism group, which will become apparent in
Remark

11.6
.

Notation 10.26
.

From now on, we assume that
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
are invertible. In the following, we will always use the convention from
Remarks

4.4
and
10.22
to identify
G_{2}^{0}
and
F_{4}^{0}
with subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
, respectively. We will sometimes use the convention from
Fig.

3
to write, e.g.,
11\bar{1}\bar{1}
as an abbreviation of
(1,1,-1,-1)
. Further, we denote by
\pi\colon F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
the map defined in
Remark

10.24
, so that
L_{\alpha}\leq L_{\pi(\alpha)_{1}}\cap L_{\pi(\alpha)_{2}}
for all
\alpha\in F_{4}^{0}
.
Finally, for all
\alpha\in F_{4}
, we put
P_{\alpha}\coloneq k
if
\alpha
is long and
P_{\alpha}\coloneq C
if
\alpha
is short, so that
L_{\alpha}\cong P_{\alpha}
as
k
-modules.

We begin by defining the root homomorphisms
(\vartheta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
. It turns out that they can be constructed step by step: We first define root homomorphisms in
J
and
J^{\prime}
, then root homomorphisms in
(k,J,J^{\prime})\cong L_{\pm 1}
and then root homomorphisms in
L
. Note that the root homomorphisms in
J^{\prime}
are defined in a different way than those in
J
even though
J\cong J^{\prime}
.

Definition 10.27

(Root homomorphisms in
J
and
J^{\prime}
)
.

For any cyclic permutation
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
, we define homomorphisms

\displaystyle\vartheta_{l}^{+}\colon(k,+)\to J

\displaystyle\colon t\mapsto\gamma_{i}^{-1}\gamma_{j}te_{l},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{l}^{-}\colon(k,+)\to J^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon t\mapsto\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}^{-1}te_{l}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{l}^{+}\colon(C,+)\to J

\displaystyle\colon a\mapsto\gamma_{j}^{-1}a[ij],

\displaystyle\vartheta_{l}^{-}\colon(C,+)\to J^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon a\mapsto\gamma_{i}^{-1}a[ij].

Note that we use the same name for the maps defined on
k
as for the maps defined on
C
, but there will be no danger of confusion in the sequel.

Definition 10.28

(Root homomorphisms in
(k,J,J^{\prime},k)
)
.

Let
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\{-1,0,1\}
with
(1,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in F_{4}
(or equivalently,
(-1,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in F_{4}
) and put
s\coloneq p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}
. We define a homomorphism
\vartheta_{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}
as follows:

(1)

If
s=-3
, define
\vartheta_{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}\colon k\to(k,J,J^{\prime},k)\colon t\mapsto(t,0,0,0)
.

(2)

If
s=-1
, then there is a unique cyclic permutation
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
with
p_{l}\neq p_{i}=p_{j}
, and we define

\vartheta_{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}\colon P_{(1,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})}=P_{(-1,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})}\to(k,J,J^{\prime},k)\colon a\mapsto(0,\vartheta_{l}^{+}(a),0,0)

(3)

If
s=1
, then there is a unique cyclic permutation
(i\,j\,l)
of
(1\,2\,3)
with
p_{l}\neq p_{i}=p_{j}
, and we define

\vartheta_{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}\colon P_{(1,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})}=P_{(-1,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})}\to(k,J,J^{\prime},k)\colon a\mapsto(0,0,\vartheta_{l}^{-}(a),0)

(4)

If
s=3
, define
\vartheta_{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}\colon k\to(k,J,J^{\prime},k)\colon t\mapsto(0,0,0,t)
.

Definition 10.29

(Root homomorphisms in
L
)
.

Let
\alpha=(p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in F_{4}
and put
\mathcal{S}\coloneq\mathcal{S}^{\text{sh}}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\text{lo}}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\text{lo}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\alpha\in F_{4}\mid\alpha\text{ is long}\}\setminus\{\bar{1}\bar{1}\bar{1}1,11\bar{1}1,1\bar{1}11,\bar{1}111\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\text{sh}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{10\bar{1}0,0101,0011,0\bar{1}\bar{1}0,\bar{1}100,\bar{1}001\}.

We first define an auxiliary map
\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L
as follows.

(1)

If
\pi(\alpha)_{1}=-2
, define
\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon k\to L\colon t\mapsto tx
.

(2)

If
\pi(\alpha)_{1}=\varepsilon 1
for
\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{+},\mathord{-}\}
, define
\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L\colon a\mapsto\vartheta_{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}(a)_{\varepsilon}
.

(3)

If
\pi(\alpha)=(0,\varepsilon 1)
for
\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{+},\mathord{-1}\}
, define
\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L\colon a\mapsto\vartheta_{l}^{\varepsilon}(a)\in J+J^{\prime}\leq L_{0}
where
l\in\{1,2,3\}
is the unique index with
|p_{l}|=2
if
\alpha
is long and
p_{l}=0
if
\alpha
is short.

(4)

If
\pi(\alpha)=(0,0)
, then there exist unique
i,j,l\in\{1,2,3\}
with
p_{i}=1
,
p_{j}=-1
,
p_{l}=0
and we define

(30)

\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon C\to L\colon a\mapsto\begin{cases}\lambda_{ij}\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},a[ij]}&\text{if }i<j,\\
\lambda_{ij}\mathbf{d}_{e_{i},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mua\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[ij]}&\text{if }i>j\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{12}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3},

\displaystyle\lambda_{13}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma_{2}^{-1},

\displaystyle\lambda_{23}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}^{-1},

\displaystyle\lambda_{21}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma_{3}^{-1},

\displaystyle\lambda_{31}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}^{-1},

\displaystyle\lambda_{32}

\displaystyle\coloneq\gamma_{1}^{-1}.

(5)

If
\pi(\alpha)_{1}=2
, define
\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon k\to L\colon t\mapsto ty
.

Further, the
root homomorphism in
L
with respect to
\alpha
is

\vartheta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L\colon a\mapsto\begin{cases}\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}(a)&\text{if }\alpha\notin\mathcal{S},\\
\bar{\vartheta}_{\alpha}(-a)&\text{if }\alpha\in\mathcal{S}.\end{cases}

The reason to use the case distinction in
Eq.

30
will only become apparent at a later point, see
Remark

11.12
.

The following definition is motivated by the classical notion of Chevalley bases in complex semisimple Lie algebras. See, for example,
[
18
, 25.2]
.

Definition 10.30
.

(1)

Recall from
Definition

4.1
that
M
denotes the Lie algebra
\bigoplus_{i\neq 0}\mathfrak{gl}(L_{i})
which contains
L_{0}
. For all
\alpha\in F_{4}
, we denote by
h_{\alpha}
the element of
M
which is defined by
[h_{\alpha},y_{\beta}]=\langle\beta|\alpha\rangle y_{\beta}
for all
y_{\beta}\in L_{\beta}
and all
\beta\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\beta)_{1}\neq 0
. Here
\langle\beta|\alpha\rangle\coloneq 2\frac{\beta\cdot\alpha}{\alpha\cdot\alpha}
denotes the Cartan integer.

(2)

A
Chevalley-type basis of
L
is a family
(x_{\alpha}\in L_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
with the following properties:

(a)

For all
\alpha\in F_{4}
, we have
[x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}]=h_{\alpha}
.

(b)

For all
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
with
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
, there exists
c_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
[x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}]=c_{\alpha,\beta}x_{\alpha+\beta}
.

(c)

It is possible to choose the constants
c_{\alpha,\beta}
above so that for all
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
with
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
, we have
c_{\alpha,\beta}=-c_{-\alpha,-\beta}
.

A family of integers
c_{\alpha,\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
with
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
which satisfies the properties above is said to be
conforming to
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
.

Remark 10.31
.

(1)

Note that a Chevalley-type basis
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
of
L
is not actually a basis of the
k
-module
L
: Its
k
-linear span intersects
L_{0000}
trivially and for any short root
\alpha\in F_{4}
, the
k
-span of
x_{\alpha}
is, in general, a proper submodule of
L_{\alpha}\cong C
.

(2)

Let
L^{\prime}
be a complex semisimple Lie algebra, let
H
denote a maximal toral subalgebra of
L^{\prime}
, let
\kappa
denote the Killing form on
L^{\prime}
, let
\Phi\subseteq H^{*}
denote the root system of
L^{\prime}
and let
h_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}\in L^{\prime}
for
\alpha\in\Phi
be defined as in
[
18
, 8.2, 8.3]
. Then for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
and all
y_{\beta}
in the
\beta
-root space
L^{\prime}_{\beta}
, we have

[h_{\alpha},y_{\beta}]=\frac{2}{\kappa(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha})}[t_{\alpha},y_{\beta}]=\frac{2}{\kappa(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha})}\beta(t_{\alpha})y_{\beta}=2\frac{\kappa(t_{\beta},t_{\alpha})}{\kappa(t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha})}y_{\beta}=\langle\beta|\alpha\rangle y_{\beta}.

Hence the elements
h_{\alpha}
in the classical theory have the same property as in
Definition

10.30
Item

1
. In fact, they are the unique elements of
H
with this property because
H
acts faithfully on
L^{\prime}
.

(3)

The numbers
c_{\alpha,\beta}
in
Definition

10.30
Item

2
are not uniquely determined in general because
L
may not be faithful as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. For example, this is the case if
k
is a field of positive characteristic. Even the image of
c_{\alpha,\beta}
in
k
need not be uniquely determined because
C
(and hence
L_{\gamma}
for all short
\gamma\in F_{4}
) need not be faithful as a
k
-module.

Remark 10.32
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
such that
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
. If
\beta
and
\alpha+\beta
are long, then
\alpha
is long as well. If
\beta
is long and
\alpha+\beta
is short, then
\alpha
must be short. It follows that, unless
\alpha,\beta
are short and
\alpha+\beta
is long, we always have a multiplication map
P_{\alpha}\times P_{\beta}\to P_{\alpha+\beta}
which is one of the maps
k\times k\to k
,
C\times C\to C
,
k\times C\to C
. In the situation that
\alpha,\beta
are short and
\alpha+\beta
is long (so that
P_{\alpha}=P_{\beta}=C
and
P_{\alpha+\beta}=k
), however, the multiplication defined on
P_{\alpha}\times P_{\beta}=C\times C
has image in
C
and not in
k=P_{\alpha+\beta}
.

We can now state the crucial property of the root homomorphisms
(\vartheta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
. We find not only that the commutator formulas in
L
are independent of
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
but also that
all commutator formulas look the same
up to possibly
“twisting”
the short root spaces by the conjugation
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
of
C
. This is the same kind of
“twisting”
that we have already seen in
Definition

1.10
.

Proposition 10.33
.

There exists a unique family
c=(c_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha,\beta\in F_{4},\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}}
of integers in
\{-2,\ldots,2\}
such that for all commutative rings
k
, all multiplicative conic alternative
k
-algebras
C
and all invertible
\Gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\in\Mat_{3}(k)
, the following hold:

(1)

Let
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
such that
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
. For each
\gamma\in F_{4}
, put
T_{\gamma}\coloneq\{\id_{C},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\}\leq\Aut(P_{\gamma})
if
\gamma
is short and
T_{\gamma}\coloneq\{\id_{k}\}\leq\Aut(P_{\gamma})
if
\gamma
is long. If
\alpha,\beta
are short and
\alpha+\beta
is long, then
c_{\alpha,\beta}
is even and there exists
\mathord{*}\in T_{\alpha}=\{\id_{C},\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\}
such that the following commutator relation is satisfied for all
c,d\in C
:

\bigl[\vartheta_{\alpha}(c),\vartheta_{\beta}(d)\bigr]=\vartheta_{\alpha+\beta}\big(c_{\alpha,\beta}/2\cdot t(c^{*}d)\big).

Otherwise, there exist
\mathord{*}\in T_{\alpha}
,
\mathord{\star}\in T_{\beta}
and
\sigma\in T_{\alpha+\beta}
such that the following commutator relation is satisfied for all
c\in P_{\alpha}
,
d\in P_{\beta}
:

\bigl[\vartheta_{\alpha}(c),\vartheta_{\beta}(d)\bigr]=\vartheta_{\alpha+\beta}\big(c_{\alpha,\beta}(c^{*}\cdot d^{\star})^{\sigma}\big).

(Here
c\cdot d\in P_{\alpha+\beta}
by
Remark

10.32
.)

(2)

The family
(\vartheta_{\alpha}(1))_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
(where
“
1
”
should be read as
1_{k}
or as
1_{C}
, depending on
\alpha
) is a Chevalley-type basis of
L
to which
c
conforms.

Further, for all
\alpha\in F_{4}
, the element
h_{\alpha}
lies in
L_{0}
and satisfies
[h_{\alpha},y_{\beta}]=\langle\beta|\alpha\rangle y_{\beta}
for all
y_{\beta}\in L_{\beta}
and all
\beta\in F_{4}
.

Proof.

A computer calculation (see
Section

9.3
) shows that for all
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
such that
\alpha+\beta\in F_{4}
, there exists
c_{\alpha,\beta}\in\{-2,\ldots,2\}
such that
[\vartheta_{\alpha}(1),\vartheta_{\beta}(1)]=\vartheta_{\alpha+\beta}(c_{\alpha,\beta})
for all possible choices of
k
,
C
,
\Gamma
. In particular, the validity of this equation for
k=C=\mathbb{C}
(or any field of characteristic
0
) shows that the family
c
is uniquely determined by this property (because then
L
is free as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module). A further computation shows that
[[x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}],y_{\beta}]=\langle\beta|\alpha\rangle y_{\beta}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
and
y_{\beta}\in L_{\beta}
. This implies that
h_{\alpha}=[x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}]\in[L_{\alpha},L_{-\alpha}]\subseteq L_{0}
for all
\alpha\in L
. The remaining assertions also follow from a computation.
∎

Remark 10.34
.

Recall from
Remark

1.3
the stronger notion of root graded Lie algebras from
[
6
]
. It follows from the existence of a Chevalley-type basis in
L
that
L
is
F_{4}
-graded in this stronger sense if
C
is faithful as a
k
-algebra. This additional condition, which by
[
17
, 11.14]
is always satisfied if
2
is invertible in
k
, is need to ensure that the
k
-submodule
kx_{\alpha}
for a short root
\alpha\in F_{4}
is isomorphic to
k
and not to a quotient of
k
.

Since an arbitrary cubic norm pair
(\bar{J},\bar{J}^{\prime})
need not contain a distinguished base point, there is no evident way to define a Chevalley-type basis (in the sense of
Definition

10.30
, but with
F_{4}
replaced by
G_{2}
) of the corresponding Lie algebra
L(\bar{J},\bar{J}^{\prime})
. Hence there is no reason to think that
L(\bar{J},\bar{J}^{\prime})
is always
G_{2}
-graded in the stronger sense. However, assume now that
(\bar{J},\bar{J})
is (induced by) a cubic norm structure and that its distinguished base point
1_{\bar{J}}
is unimodular (see
Remark

2.5
Item

2
). Denote by
(\vartheta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
the root homomorphisms in
L(\bar{J},\bar{J})
from
Definition

6.4
. For all roots
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
where
\alpha
is long and
\beta
is short, put

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{(-2,-1),(-1,-2)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{(-1,0),(0,-1)\},

\displaystyle x_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\coloneq\begin{cases}\vartheta_{\alpha}(-1_{k})&\text{if }\alpha\in\mathcal{S}_{1},\\
\vartheta_{\alpha}(1_{k})&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle x_{\beta}

\displaystyle\coloneq\begin{cases}\vartheta_{\beta}(-1_{\bar{J}})&\text{if }\alpha\in\mathcal{S}_{2},\\
\vartheta_{\beta}(1_{\bar{J}})&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

Using that
T(1_{\bar{J}},1_{\bar{J}})=3_{k}
and
D(1_{\bar{J}},1_{\bar{J}})=2\id_{\bar{J}}
, one can show that
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
is a Chevalley-type basis of
L(\bar{J},\bar{J})
of type
G_{2}
. Indeed, it is straightforward to see that there exist unique integers
c_{\alpha,\beta}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
with
\alpha+\beta\in G_{2}
and unique integers
d_{\alpha,\beta}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in G_{2}
such that for all cubic norm structures
(\bar{J},\bar{J})
, we have
[x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}]=c_{\alpha,\beta}x_{\alpha+\beta}
and
[[x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}],y_{\beta}]=d_{\alpha,\beta}y_{\beta}
. Proving that
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
is a Chevalley-type basis reduces to proving that these integers satisfy certain properties, so it suffices to show that it is a Chevalley-type basis for some cubic norm structure that is free as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. This can be done for cubic Jordan matrix algebras with the strategy outlined in
Section

9.3
.

11.
Refining the
G_{2}
-grading of the automorphism group

We keep up the notation from
Section

10
, including
10.26
. By
Theorem

7.24
, we have a
G_{2}
-grading
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
in the automorphism group of the Lie algebra
L=L(J,J^{\prime})
where
J=J^{\prime}=\Her_{3}(C,\Gamma)
is a cubic Jordan matrix algebra over the conic
k
-algebra
(C,n)
. We put
G\coloneq\langle U_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in G_{2}\rangle\leq\Aut(L)
. Our goal is to refine
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
to an
F_{4}
-grading
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
of
G
, using the
F_{4}
-grading of
L(J,J^{\prime})
constructed in
Section

10
.

The system
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4})
of simple roots in
F_{4}
that induces the map
\pi\colon F_{4}^{0}\to G_{2}^{0}
(see
Remark

10.24
) is impractical to work with because it contains two simple roots that map to zero. Instead, we will consider the following system of simple roots, which in addition has the property that three of its elements map to the positive system in
G_{2}
for which we have determined the commutator relations in
Proposition

7.23
.

Notation 11.1
.

We denote by
\Delta_{F}=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\delta_{4})
the standard system of simple roots in
F_{4}
given by

\delta_{1}=11\bar{1}\bar{1},\quad\delta_{2}=\bar{2}000,\quad\delta_{3}\coloneq 1\bar{1}00,\quad\delta_{4}\coloneq 0110.

Further, we denote by
T\coloneq\{\pm 1\}\times\{\pm 1\}
the twisting group of
(k,C)
, which acts on
k
and
C
(see
Definition

1.10
).

For roots
\alpha\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)\neq(0,0)
, we can define root homomorphisms in
\Aut(L)
by exponentiating the root homomorphisms in
L
from
Section

10.3
.

Definition 11.2
.

Let
\alpha\in F_{4}
such that
\pi(\alpha)\neq(0,0)
and let
\vartheta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L_{\alpha}\leq L_{\pi(\alpha)}
denote the root homomorphism from
Definition

10.29
. Then the
root homomorphism in
G
with respect to
\alpha
is

\theta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to G\colon a\mapsto\exp_{\pi(\alpha)}\big(\vartheta_{\alpha}(a)\big)

where
\exp_{\pi(\alpha)}\colon L_{\pi(\alpha)}\to\Aut(L)
is the
\pi(\alpha)
-parametrization (in the
G_{2}
-graded Lie algebra
L
) from
Proposition

7.20
. Further, the
root group associated to
\alpha
is
U_{\alpha}\coloneq\theta_{\alpha}(P_{\alpha})
.

For the remaining six roots that map to
0
in
G_{2}
, we define the corresponding root homomorphisms by conjugating root homomorphisms from
Definition

11.2
by appropriate reflections.

Definition 11.3
.

For all
\alpha\in F_{4}
such that
\pi(\alpha)\neq(0,0)
, we define

w_{\alpha}\coloneq\theta_{-\alpha}(-1_{\alpha})\theta_{\alpha}(1_{\alpha})\theta_{-\alpha}(-1_{\alpha})\in G\leq\Aut(L)

where
1_{\alpha}=1_{k}
if
\alpha
is long and
1_{\alpha}=1_{C}
if
\alpha
is short.

Lemma 11.4
.

For all
\alpha\in F_{4}
such that
\pi(\alpha)\neq(0,0)
, the element
w_{\alpha}\in\Aut(L)
is an
\alpha
-reflection.

Proof.

For all
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
, we have to show that
w_{\alpha}(L_{\beta})=L_{\beta^{\sigma(\alpha)}}
. We have verified these
48\cdot 48
assertions on a computer. See
Section

9.3
for details.
∎

Remark 11.5
.

We will only need the statement of
Lemma

11.4
for
\alpha\in\{\delta_{1},\delta_{4}\}
.

Remark 11.6
.

Let
\alpha\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)\neq(0,0)
. While the root group
U_{\alpha}=\exp_{\pi(\alpha)}(L_{\alpha})
does not depend on the choice of the root homomorphism
\vartheta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L_{\alpha}
in
L
, the root homomorphism
\theta_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to U_{\alpha}
in
G
does. In fact, the
“twisting”
of the
\vartheta_{\alpha}
by products of
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
,
\gamma_{3}
,
-1
in
Definition

10.29
is precisely what ensures that
w_{\alpha}
is indeed a reflection. If we had chosen a different map
\tilde{\vartheta}_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to L_{\alpha}
and defined
\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha}\colon P_{\alpha}\to G\colon a\mapsto\exp_{\pi(\alpha)}(\tilde{\vartheta}_{\alpha}(a))
accordingly, then the desired reflections might, for example, have the form
w_{\alpha}=\tilde{\theta}_{-\alpha}(1_{\alpha})\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha}(1_{\alpha})\tilde{\theta}_{-\alpha}(1_{\alpha})
(as in
Lemma

B.6
for the
G_{2}
-grading). Even worse, their expression in terms of
\tilde{\theta}_{\alpha}
,
\tilde{\theta}_{-\alpha}
may involve the structure constants
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
. It is this obstruction that originally prompted our search for a Chevalley-type basis of
L
.

Remark 11.7
.

Each of the six roots
\alpha\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)=(0,0)
can be expressed as
\alpha=\beta^{\sigma(\delta_{i})}
for some
i\in\{1,4\}
and some
\beta\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)\neq(0,0)
. Explicitly, we may choose
\beta
and
i
as in
Table

2
.

in

, in dependence of

Hence for each such

, it follows from

and

that

\exp_{\alpha}\colon L_{\alpha}\to\Aut(L)\colon b\mapsto w_{i}^{-1}\circ\exp_{\beta}\big(w_{i}(b)\big)\circ w_{i}

is an

Further, a computer calculation (see

) shows that
w_{i}(\vartheta_{\alpha}(a))=\vartheta_{\beta}(\eta_{\alpha}.a)
for all

where
\eta_{\alpha}\in T
is given by the last row in

.

Definition 11.8
.

Let
\alpha\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)=(0,0)
. Then the map
\theta_{\alpha}\colon C\to\Aut(L)
from
Remark

(with
\beta
and

chosen as in
Table

2
) is called the

\alpha
, and its image
U_{\alpha}
is called the

.

Remark 11.9
.

For all
\alpha\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)=(0,0)
, we have that
w_{i}
and
U_{\beta}
are contained in
G
, the group generated by
(U_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in G_{2}}
. Hence the group generated by
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
is exactly
G
(and not a bigger group).

Having defined all root homomorphisms
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
, we now verify that
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
satisfies the axioms of an
F_{4}
-grading of
G
. We begin with the existence of Weyl elements.

Notation 11.10

(cf.
1.14
)
.

We denote by
\eta^{\text{e}}\colon F_{4}\times\Delta_{F}\to T
the
\Delta_{F}
-parity map with values in
T
(in the sense of
Definition

1.11
) that is given by the values in
Table

3
. Note that the definition of
\eta^{\text{e}}
does not involve the actions of
T
on
k
and
C
, so
\eta^{\text{e}}
is independent of
k
,
C
and
\Gamma
.

Proposition 11.11
.

For all
\delta\in\Delta_{F}
,
\alpha\in F_{4}
and
a\in P_{\alpha}
, we have

(31)

w_{\delta}^{-1}\circ\theta_{\alpha}(a)\circ w_{\delta}=\theta_{\alpha^{\sigma(\delta)}}(\eta^{\text{e}}_{\alpha,\delta}.a).

In particular,
w_{\delta}
is a
\delta
-Weyl element with respect to
(U_{\beta})_{\beta\in F_{4}}
,
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
is a parametrization of
(U_{\beta})_{\beta\in F_{4}}
by
(k,C)
with respect to
(w_{\delta})_{\delta\in\Delta_{F}}
and
\eta^{\text{e}}
(in the sense of
Definition

1.12
) and
(U_{\beta})_{\beta\in F_{4}}
satisfies Axiom
1.5
Item

3
.

Proof.

The first assertion is a straightforward computation, but a very long one: For all
\alpha\in F_{4}
and for arbitrary
a\in P_{\alpha}
, we have to compute the action of
w_{\delta}^{-1}\circ\theta_{\alpha}(a)\circ w_{\delta}
and
\theta_{\alpha^{\sigma(\delta)}}(\eta^{\text{e}}_{\alpha,\delta}.a)
on a generating set of
L
(such as
L_{-2}\cup L_{1}
, which is spanned by
15

F_{4}
-root spaces) and show that the resulting expressions are identical. (This procedure is precisely how we obtained the values of
\eta^{\text{e}}
.) We have performed these calculations on a computer (see
Section

9.3
for details). By the definition of the root groups, we infer that
w_{\delta}
is a
\delta
-Weyl element. Since
\Delta_{F}
is a system of simple roots, it follows from
Remark

1.8
that Axiom
1.5
Item

3
is satisfied.
∎

Table 3.
The values of
\eta^{\text{e}}\colon F_{4}\times\Delta\to T
in

. In the first column, a sequence

represents
\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}\delta_{i}
. Further,
\eta^{\text{e}}(\alpha,\delta)=\eta^{\text{e}}(-\alpha,\delta)
for all

and
\delta\in\Delta
.

Remark 11.12
.

The map
\eta^{\text{e}}
in
Proposition

11.11
has the property that
\eta^{\text{e}}(\alpha,\delta)=\eta^{\text{e}}(-\alpha,\delta)
for all
\alpha\in F_{4}
and
\delta\in\Delta
. As in
Remark

11.6
, this property relies on our careful choice of the root homomorphisms
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
in
Definition

10.29
. In particular, the validity of this property for
\alpha\in F_{4}
with
\pi(\alpha)=(0,0)
relies on the case distinction in
Eq.

30
.

Proposition 11.13
.

The following commutator relations hold for all
t,s\in k
and
c,d\in C
:

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{1}}(t),\theta_{\delta_{2}}(s)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}(ts),

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{2}}(t),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(c)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(-tc)\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(tn(c)\big),

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(d)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(-t(c\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)\big),

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{2}}(t),\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}(s)]

\displaystyle=1_{G},

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{4}}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(d)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu).

In particular, Axiom
1.5
Item

2
is satisfied.

Proof.

The first four commutator formulas follow from the ones established in
Proposition

7.23
for arbitrary cubic norm pairs. For example, for
t,s\in k
:

\displaystyle[\theta_{\delta_{1}}(t),\theta_{\delta_{2}}(s)]

\displaystyle=\bigl[\exp_{1,0}\big(\vartheta_{11\bar{1}\bar{1}}(s)\big),\exp_{-2,-1}\big(\vartheta_{\bar{2}000}(t)\big)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl[\exp_{1,0}\big((0,-\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}se_{1},0,0)_{+}\big),\exp_{-2,-1}(tx)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\bigl[x_{1}\big(-\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}se_{1}\big),x_{6}(t)\bigr]=x_{5}\big(-t\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}se_{1}\big).

Here we have used that
b\coloneq-\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}se_{1}
satisfies
N(b)=0
and
b^{\sharp}=0
. This yields the desired commutator relation because

\displaystyle\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}(ts)

\displaystyle=\exp_{-1,-1}\big(\vartheta_{\bar{1}1\bar{1}\bar{1}}(ts)\big)=x_{5}\big(-t\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}se_{1}\big).

Alternatively, the commutator formulas follow from a direct computer calculation (see
Section

9.3
for details), and this is also how establish the last formula.

It follows from the above that the commutator relation
[U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}]\subseteq U_{\mathord{]\alpha,\beta[}}
is satisfied for all

(\alpha,\beta)\in X\coloneq\{(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}),(\delta_{2},\delta_{3}),(\delta_{2}+\delta_{3},\delta_{3}),(\delta_{2},\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}),(\delta_{3},\delta_{4})\}.

By similar arguments as in
Remark

1.16
, we infer that
[U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}]\subseteq U_{\mathord{]\alpha,\beta[}}
holds for all non-proportional
\alpha,\beta\in F_{4}
. Hence Axiom
1.5
Item

2
is satisfied.
∎

Proposition 11.14
.

Let
\Pi
be any positive system of roots in
F_{4}
and denote by
U_{+}
and
U_{-}
the groups generated by
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Pi}
and
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in-\Pi}
, respectively. Then
U_{+}\cap U_{-}=1
. In particular, the root groups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in G_{2}}
satisfy Axiom
1.5
Item

4

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

7.8
.
∎

We conclude with our final main result.

Theorem 11.15
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring and let
C
be a multiplicative conic alternative algebra over
k
. Let
\Gamma=\operatorname{diag}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\in\Mat_{3}(k)
be invertible, let
J\coloneq\Her_{3}(C,\Gamma)
denote the associated cubic Jordan matrix algebra (see
9.6
) and let
L\coloneq L(J,J)
denote the Lie algebra constructed in
Section

4
. Then the root groups
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
from
Definitions

11.8
and
11.2
constitute an
F_{4}
-grading of the group
G\leq\Aut(L)
that they generate. Further,
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
is a parametrization of
(G,(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}})
by
(k,C)
with respect to
(w_{\delta})_{\delta\in\Delta_{F}}
and
\eta^{\text{e}}
which satisfies the commutator formulas in
Proposition

11.13
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
Propositions

11.13
,
11.11
,
11.14
and
11.9
.
∎

Remark 11.16

(cf.
1.17
)
.

The commutator formulas in
Proposition

11.13
are the same as in
Theorem

1.15
only
“up to twisting”
, that is,
“up to signs and conjugation”
. Similarly, the parity map
\eta^{\text{e}}
in this section is not the same as the parity map
\eta^{\text{c}}
from
Remark

1.14
that arises in
Theorem

1.15
. In this remark, we illustrate how to modify the root homomorphisms
(\theta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
to obtain a parametrization of
G
which looks just like the one in
Theorem

1.15
.
Put
\Delta\coloneq\Delta_{F}
. Since we have shown in
Theorem

11.15
that
(G,(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}})
is an
F_{4}
-graded group, we can apply
Theorem

1.15
. Hence there exist a commutative ring
k^{\prime}
, a multiplicative conic alternative
k^{\prime}
-algebra
(C^{\prime},n^{\prime})
and a parametrization
(\theta_{\alpha}^{\prime})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
of
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
by
(k^{\prime},C^{\prime})
with respect to
(w_{\delta})_{\delta\in\Delta}
(the Weyl elements from
Definition

11.3
) and
\eta^{\text{c}}
. We aim to show that
k=k^{\prime}
(as rings) and
(C,n)=(C^{\prime},n^{\prime})
(as conic algebras).

By
[
31
, 4.5.17]
, we can achieve that
k=k^{\prime}
,
C=C^{\prime}
as additive groups and
\theta_{\delta_{i}}^{\prime}=\theta_{\delta_{i}}
for
i\in\{2,3\}
. Our main tool is the following observation: For all
i\in\{2,3\}
,
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{\ell}\in\Delta
,
p\in P_{\delta_{i}}
and
\alpha\coloneq\delta_{i}^{\sigma(\delta_{1})\cdots\sigma(\delta_{\ell})}
, we have (in the notation of
Definitions

1.11
and
1.13
):

(32)

\theta_{\alpha}(\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{i},\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{\ell}}.p)=\theta_{\delta_{i}}(p)^{w_{\delta_{1}}\cdots w_{\delta_{\ell}}}=\theta_{\delta_{i}}^{\prime}(p)^{w_{\delta_{1}}\cdots w_{\delta_{\ell}}}=\theta_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{i},\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{\ell}}.p).

Note that, while the twistings groups
T
and
T^{\prime}
of
(k,C)
and
(k^{\prime},C^{\prime})
are the same as abstracts groups and act on the same sets
k=k^{\prime}
and
C=C^{\prime}
, their actions on
C=C^{\prime}
are not (yet proven to be) the same because they involve the conjugation of
C
and of
C^{\prime}
, respectively. It is the action of
T
that appears on the left side of
Eq.

32
while the action of
T^{\prime}
appears on the right side. In the following, we will write
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\>\cdot\>\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
for the conjugation of
C
and never (need to) refer to the conjugation of
C^{\prime}
.

We first show that
k=k^{\prime}
as rings. We will frequently use some explicit values of
\eta^{\text{c}}
, such as

\displaystyle\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{2},\delta_{1}}=(-1,1)=\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2},\delta_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{2},\delta_{1}}=(1,1)=\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2},\delta_{2}},

which can be read of from
Table

3
and
[
31
, Figure 10.4]
, respectively. Using these values and
Definition

1.11
, we compute that
\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{2},\delta_{1}\delta_{2}}=(1,1)=\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{2},\delta_{1}\delta_{2}}
. Hence by
Eq.

32
, we have

\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}(t)=\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}^{\prime}(-t)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{\delta_{1}}(t)=\theta_{\delta_{1}}(t)

for all
t\in k
. This implies that

\displaystyle\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}(t\cdot_{k}s)

\displaystyle=[\theta_{\delta_{1}}(t),\theta_{\delta_{2}}(s)]=[\theta_{\delta_{1}}^{\prime}(t),\theta_{\delta_{2}}^{\prime}(s)]=\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}^{\prime}(-t\cdot_{k^{\prime}}s)=\theta_{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}(t\cdot_{k^{\prime}}s)

for all
t,s\in k
. Hence
k^{\prime}=k
.

Similarly, to show that
C=C^{\prime}
as (nonassociative) rings, we observe that

\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{3},\delta_{4}}=(-1,-1)=\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4},\delta_{3}}\quad\text{and}\quad\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{3},\delta_{4}}=(-1,1)=\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4},\delta_{3}}.

This implies that
\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{3},\delta_{4}\delta_{3}}=(1,1)=\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{3},\delta_{4}\delta_{3}}
and, by
Eq.

32
,

\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}^{\prime}(c)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{\delta_{4}}(c)=\theta_{\delta_{4}}^{\prime}(c)

for all
c\in C
. Thus

\displaystyle\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}^{\prime}(c\cdot_{C}d)

\displaystyle=[\theta_{\delta_{4}}^{\prime}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}^{\prime}(d)]=[\theta_{\delta_{4}}(c),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(d)]=\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mud\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\cdot_{C}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muc\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)=\theta_{\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}}^{\prime}(c\cdot_{C}d)

for all
c,d\in C
, so
C=C^{\prime}
as (nonassociative) rings.

Finally, we have
\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{2},\delta_{3}}=(1,1)
,
\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{2},\delta_{3}}=(-1,1)
,
\eta^{\text{c}}_{\delta_{3},\delta_{2}}=(1,1)=\eta^{\text{e}}_{\delta_{3},\delta_{2}}
and thus

\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}(t)=\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}^{\prime}(-t)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(c)=\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}^{\prime}(c)

for all
t\in k
,
c\in C
, which implies that

\displaystyle\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(-t\cdot_{C}c)\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(tn(c)\big)

\displaystyle=[\theta_{\delta_{2}}(t),\theta_{\delta_{3}}(c)]=[\theta_{\delta_{2}}^{\prime}(t),\theta_{\delta_{3}}^{\prime}(c)]

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}^{\prime}(-t\cdot_{C^{\prime}}c)\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}^{\prime}\big(-tn^{\prime}(c)\big)

\displaystyle=\theta_{\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}}(-t\cdot_{C^{\prime}}c)\theta_{\delta_{2}+2\delta_{3}}\big(tn^{\prime}(c)\big)

for all
t\in k
,
c\in C
. Hence
n=n^{\prime}
and the
k
-algebra structures on
C
and on
C^{\prime}
coincide. Altogether, we conclude the following: Starting from an arbitrary multiplicative conic alternative algebra
C
over a commutative ring
k
, we can construct an
F_{4}
-graded group with a parametrization
(\theta_{\alpha}^{\prime})_{\alpha\in F_{4}}
that behaves exactly like the one in
Theorem

1.15
.

Appendix A
Higher Leibniz rules

In this appendix, we develop the machinery needed to verify that certain “exponential endomorphisms” preserve the Lie bracket, over arbitrary rings. We will apply this machinery to show that the maps
e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}
for
b\in J
and
e_{\rho x}
for
\rho\in k
from
Definitions

7.12
and
7.11
are endomorphisms of the Lie algebra
L
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
L
be a Lie algebra over an arbitrary commutative ring
k
.
Assume that
\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{s}
are
k
-linear endomorphisms of
L
such that

(*)_{\ell}

\delta_{\ell}([v,w])=\sum_{i+j=\ell}[\delta_{i}(v),\delta_{j}(w)]

for all
v,w\in L
and all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\delta_{\ell}=0
for all
\ell>s
.

Then the map
\varphi\coloneq\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\delta_{\ell}
is a Lie algebra endomorphism, i.e.,
\varphi([v,w])=[\varphi(v),\varphi(w)]
for all
v,w\in L
.

Proof.

We have
\varphi([v,w])=\sum_{\ell}\delta_{\ell}([v,w])
, so by invoking

(
∗
)

ℓ

for each
\ell
, we get

\varphi([v,w])=\sum_{\ell}\sum_{i+j=\ell}[\delta_{i}(v),\delta_{j}(w)]=\Bigl[\sum_{i}\delta_{i}(v),\sum_{j}\delta_{j}(w)\Bigr]=[\varphi(v),\varphi(w)].\qed

In the case that the maps
\delta_{0},\ldots,\delta_{s}
are related to a grading of
L
(an assumption that will always be satisfied in our situation), we have the following converse of
Lemma

A.1
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(A,+)
be an abelian group and let
L=\bigoplus_{a\in A}L_{a}
be an
A
-graded Lie algebra over a commutative ring
k
. Assume that
\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{s}
are
k
-linear endomorphisms of
L
such that the map
\varphi\coloneq\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\delta_{\ell}
is a Lie algebra endomorphism and put
\delta_{\ell}\coloneq 0
for all
\ell>s
. Assume further that there exists
a\in A
such that
\delta_{\ell}(L_{b})\subseteq L_{b+\ell a}
for all
b\in A
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(
∗
)

ℓ

holds for all
v,w\in L
and all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove

(
∗
)

ℓ

for homogeneous elements
u,v
, so let
b,c\in A
and let
v\in L_{b}
,
w\in L_{c}
. Since
\varphi
is an endomorphism of
L
, a slight rearrangement of the computation in
Lemma

A.1
yields that

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\delta_{\ell}([v,w])=\varphi([v,w])=[\varphi(v),\varphi(w)]=\sum_{\ell}\sum_{i+j=\ell}[\delta_{i}(v),\delta_{j}(w)].

Note that
\delta_{\ell}([v,w])
and
\sum_{i+j=\ell}[\delta_{i}(v),\delta_{j}(w)]
both lie in
L_{b+c+\ell a}
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
L=\bigoplus_{d\in A}L_{d}
, it follows that
\delta_{\ell}([v,w])=\sum_{i+j=\ell}[\delta_{i}(v),\delta_{j}(w)]
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

The guiding example we have in mind, is the case where
\delta
is a derivation of
L
with
\delta^{s+1}=0
for some
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and where
\delta_{\ell}\coloneq\frac{1}{\ell!}\delta^{\ell}
for each
\ell
, provided each
\ell!
is invertible in
k
. Notice that

\varphi=\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\delta_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\frac{1}{\ell!}\delta^{\ell}

is then the usual exponential map corresponding to
\delta
.
In this case,
(*)_{0}
is trivially satisfied (since
\delta_{0}=\id
) and
(*)_{1}
is simply the Leibniz rule that defines derivations.
By applying the Leibniz rule
\ell
times, we get that

\ell!\cdot\delta_{\ell}([v,w])=\ell!\cdot\sum_{i+j=\ell}[\delta_{i}(v),\delta_{j}(w)]

for all
\ell
, so if each
\ell!
is invertible in
k
, then

(
∗
)

ℓ

follows for each
\ell
.

The key point now is that the maps we care about, are exponential maps of derivations defined over arbitrary rings.
We will still have that
\delta_{0}=\id
and
\delta_{1}=\delta
is a derivation, but we cannot deduce the “higher Leibniz rules”

(
∗
)

ℓ

from the fact that
\delta
is a derivation. Proving each of these

(
∗
)

ℓ

is a computational and rather elaborate process, that we will describe below, providing enough details so that the courageous reader can reconstruct the full proof without too much effort.

Remark A.3
.

We have been wondering whether there is some general principle that would allow us to pass from
\mathbb{Z}
to an arbitrary ring
k
(e.g. by tensoring), so that we would be able to omit the lengthy proofs that follow. However, the fact that we need a lot of additional identities and lemmas in the process indicates that this seems unlikely.

A related example from the literature is that in the appendix of
[
13
]
, Faulkner constructs an
A_{2}
-graded Lie algebra and an
A_{2}
-graded group from an alternative ring. He faces the same problems as we do in
[
13
, (A.7)]
(except that his grading is a 3-grading, so only the invertibility of 2 is a problem). He proceeds precisely by proving the identities

(
∗
)

ℓ

by hand, so apparently he did not see a way around this.

A.1.
The exponential maps
e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}

We now proceed to show that the maps
e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}
from
Definition

7.12
are indeed endomorphisms of the Lie algebra
L
.
So fix
b\in J
, let
\varphi\coloneq e_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}
and decompose
\varphi=\sum_{\ell=0}^{3}\delta_{\ell}
according to the degree.
In particular,
\delta_{0}\coloneq\id
and
\delta_{1}\coloneq\ad_{(0,b,0,0)_{+}}
. Explicitly, the maps
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
and
\delta_{3}
are as follows.

(33)

\displaystyle\delta_{1}

\displaystyle\colon\begin{cases}x&\mapsto(0,b,0,0)_{-}\\
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}&\mapsto-b\times c+T(b,c^{\prime})(\zeta-\xi)-\mathbf{d}_{b,c^{\prime}}+\rho b\\
c^{\prime}&\mapsto\bigl(T(b,c^{\prime}),0,0,0\bigr)_{+}\\
\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}&\mapsto\bigl(0,-D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)+T(e,e^{\prime})b,0,0\bigr)_{+}\\
\xi&\mapsto(0,-b,0,0)_{+}\\
\zeta&\mapsto 0\\
c&\mapsto(0,0,-b\times c,0)_{+}\\
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}&\mapsto T(b,c^{\prime})y\\
y&\mapsto 0\end{cases}

(34)

\displaystyle\delta_{2}

\displaystyle\colon\begin{cases}x&\mapsto-b^{\sharp}\\
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}&\mapsto\bigl(-T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ T(b,c^{\prime})b-b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},\ -\rho b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}\\
c^{\prime}&\mapsto 0\\
\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}&\mapsto 0\\
\xi&\mapsto 0\\
\zeta&\mapsto 0\\
c&\mapsto-T(c,b^{\sharp})y\\
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}&\mapsto 0\\
y&\mapsto 0\end{cases}

(35)

\displaystyle\delta_{3}

\displaystyle\colon\begin{cases}x&\mapsto\bigl(-N(b),0,0,0\bigr)_{+}\\
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}&\mapsto-\rho N(b)y\\
c^{\prime}&\mapsto 0\\
\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}&\mapsto 0\\
\xi&\mapsto 0\\
\zeta&\mapsto 0\\
c&\mapsto 0\\
(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}&\mapsto 0\\
y&\mapsto 0\end{cases}

Remark A.4
.

(1)

To make the expressions more readable, we will usually omit parentheses and write
\delta_{i}v
in place of
\delta_{i}(v)
.

(2)

Observe that
\delta_{\ell}(L_{i,j})\subseteq L_{i+\ell,j}
for all
i,j,\ell
.
In particular,

(
∗
)

ℓ

is trivially valid if
v\in L_{ij}
and
w\in L_{rs}
when
L_{i+r+\ell,j+s}=0
.
This will greatly reduce the number of cases we have to verify.

The following little trick will severely reduce the required computations.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
n\geq 2
and let
a,b,c\in L
.
Assume that

(
∗
)

ℓ

holds for all
\ell\leq n
for each of the following five pairs:

(1)

v=a
and
w=b
;

(2)

v=a
and
w=c
;

(3)

v=b
and
w=c
;

(4)

v=b
and
w=[a,c]
;

(5)

v=c
and
w=[a,b]
.

Then

(
∗
)

ℓ

also holds for
v=a
and
w=[b,c]
, for all
\ell\leq n
.

Proof.

By the Jacobi identity, both sides of

(
∗
)

ℓ

for
v=a
and
w=[b,c]
can be simplified to the same expression

\sum_{i+j+m=\ell}\bigl[[\delta_{i}a,\delta_{j}b],\delta_{m}c\bigr]-\sum_{i+j+m=\ell}\bigl[[\delta_{i}a,\delta_{m}c],\delta_{j}b\bigr].\qed

Here is one specific instance that we will use several times.

Corollary A.6
.

Let
r\in\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
, let
n\geq 2
, let
e\in J
and
e^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Assume that

(
∗
)

ℓ

holds for all
\ell\leq n
in each of the following situations:

(1)

v\in L_{r}
and
w=e
;

(2)

v\in L_{r}
and
w=e^{\prime}
.

Then

(
∗
)

ℓ

also holds for all
v\in L_{r}
and
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
, for all
\ell\leq n
.

Proof.

By definition, we have
\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}=[e^{\prime},e]
.
Observe that for each
v\in L_{r}
, the elements
[v,e^{\prime}]
and
[v,e]
also belong to
L_{r}
.
Moreover,

(
∗
)

ℓ

also holds for
v=e
and
w=e^{\prime}
: it holds trivially for
\ell=0
and
\ell=1
, and once
\ell\geq 2
, it holds by
Remark

A.4
because
L_{\ell,0}=0
.
The result now follows from
Lemma

A.5
.
∎

We are now ready to prove

(
∗
)

ℓ

for each
\ell
, starting with
\ell=2
.
(Recall that
(*)_{1}
is just the Jacobi identity since
\delta_{0}=\id
and
\delta_{1}
is an inner derivation.)
For each
\ell
, this will require a large number of case distinctions depending on
v
and
w
, where we will go over each of the different cases as in
Table

1
.

The case
\ell=2
.

We have to show that

(*)_{2}

\delta_{2}[v,w]=[\delta_{2}v,w]+[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]+[v,\delta_{2}w]

for all
v,w\in L
.
We first list the different cases we have to consider.

(1)

v=x
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
,

(2)

v=x
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
,

(3)

v=x
,
w=\xi
,

(4)

v=x
,
w=\zeta
,

(5)

v=x
,
w=c\in J
,

(6)

v=x
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
,

(7)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
,

(8)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
,

(9)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
,

(10)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\xi
,

(11)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\zeta
,

(12)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=c\in J
,

(13)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
,

(14)

v=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
,
w=c\in J
,

(15)

v=\xi
,
w=c\in J
,

(16)

v=\zeta
,
w=c\in J
.

Observe that several cases have been omitted because of
Remark

A.4
.
For example, when
v=x
and
w=c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, we have
v\in L_{-2,-1}
and
w\in L_{0,-1}
, but
L_{-2+0+2,-1-1}=L_{0,-2}=0
.
As another example, note that when
v\in L_{0,i}
and
w\in L_{0,j}
, then we end up in
L_{2,i+j}
, which is
0
unless
i+j=1
; this implies that only cases
Items

14
,
15
and
16
arise (up to symmetry) for
v,w\in L_{0}
.

We will now deal with each of these cases separately. In each case, we will simply give the result of the computation of each of the four terms involved in

(
∗
)

2

and mention if the computation or the verification of

(
∗
)

2

involves additional identities.

(1)

v=x
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},\ \rho b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{-}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-2T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ T(b,c^{\prime})b-2b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},\ -2\rho b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{-}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ -T(b,c^{\prime})b+b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},\ \rho b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{-}.

(2)

v=x
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
. By
Corollary

A.6
, we can skip this case, as it will follow from
Item

5
. (Notice that the case
v=x
,
w=e^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
holds by
Remark

A.4
.)

(3)

v=x
,
w=\xi
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=-2b^{\sharp}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=-2b^{\sharp}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(4)

v=x
,
w=\zeta
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=-b^{\sharp}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=-b^{\sharp}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(5)

v=x
,
w=c\in J
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{d}_{c,b^{\sharp}}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=2T(c,b^{\sharp})\zeta-\mathbf{d}_{b,b\times c}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=-T(c,b^{\sharp})\xi.

For the computation of
[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]
, we used the fact that
T(b,b\times c)=T(c,b\times b)=2T(c,b^{\sharp})
.
Moreover, to verify

(
∗
)

2

, we require
Lemma

4.17
Item

3
.

(6)

v=x
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime}-T(b,c^{\prime})b,\ \rho b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(c,b^{\sharp}),\ b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},\ \rho b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ -T(b,c^{\prime})b,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(7)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
.
A direct computation is possible but quite involved, and it is much more efficient to invoke
Lemma

A.5
.
So write
w=[z,x]
with
z=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
.
Then

(
∗
)

2

for the given pair
(v,w)
will follow once we know it for each of the five pairs
(v,z)
,
(v,x)
,
(z,x)
,
(x,[v,z])
and
(z,[v,x])
.
For
(v,z)
, this is case
Item

13
below. For
(v,x)
and
(z,x)
, this has been dealt with in the respective cases
Items

1
and
6
above.
Since
[v,z]\in L_{0}
, the case
(x,[v,z])
follows from
Items

2
,
3
,
4
and
5
above. The case
(z,[v,x])
, finally, is trivial because
[v,x]=0
.

(8)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=c^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-T(c^{\prime}\times a^{\prime},b^{\sharp}),\ \mu T(b,c^{\prime})b-\mu b^{\sharp}\times c^{\prime},\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(b,a^{\prime})T(b,c^{\prime})-T(b^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime},c^{\prime}),\ -\mu c^{\prime}\times b^{\sharp},\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-T(b,a^{\prime})T(b,c^{\prime}),\ \mu T(b,c^{\prime})b,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(9)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
.
By
Corollary

A.6
, we can skip this case, as it will follow from
Items

8
and
12
.

(10)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\xi
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-T(a,b^{\sharp}),\ T(b,a^{\prime})b-b^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime},\ -\mu b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(T(a,b^{\sharp}),\ -T(b,a^{\prime})b+b^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime},\ \mu b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-2T(a,b^{\sharp}),\ D_{b,a^{\prime}}(b),\ -2\mu b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

By definition,
D_{b,a^{\prime}}(b)=2T(b,a^{\prime})b-2b^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime}
.

(11)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\zeta
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-T(a,b^{\sharp}),\ 0,\ \mu b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-T(a,b^{\sharp}),\ 0,\ \mu b^{\sharp},\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(12)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=c\in J
. We can again invoke
Lemma

A.5
to avoid computations.
Indeed, again write
v=[z,x]
with
z=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{+}
.
Then

(
∗
)

2

for the given pair
(v,w)
will follow once we know it for each of the five pairs
(x,z)
,
(c,z)
,
(x,c)
,
(z,[x,c])
and
(x,[z,c])
.
For
(x,c)
, this is case
Item

5
above. For
(x,z)
and
(x,[z,c])
, this is case
Item

6
above.
For
(c,z)
, this is trivial because of
Remark

A.4
. The case
(z,[x,c])
, finally, is trivial because
[x,c]=0
.

(13)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=T(\rho a-a^{\prime}\times c^{\prime}+\mu c,\ b^{\sharp})y

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=\rho T(a,b^{\sharp})y+T(b,a^{\prime})T(b,c^{\prime})y-T(b^{\sharp}\times a^{\prime},c^{\prime})y+\mu T(c,b^{\sharp})y

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=-T(b,a^{\prime})T(b,c^{\prime})y

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(14)

v=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
,
w=c\in J
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=-T\bigl(D_{e,e^{\prime}}(c),b^{\sharp}\bigr)y

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=T\bigl(D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)-T(e,e^{\prime})b,b\times c\bigr)y

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0.

To prove equality of the two non-zero right hand sides, we expand

\displaystyle-T\bigl(D_{e,e^{\prime}}(c),b^{\sharp}\bigr)=-T(e,e^{\prime})T(c,b^{\sharp})-T(c,e^{\prime})T(e,b^{\sharp})+T\bigl((c\times e)\times e^{\prime},b^{\sharp}\bigr),

\displaystyle T\bigl(D_{e,e^{\prime}}(b)-T(e,e^{\prime})b,b\times c\bigr)=T(b,e^{\prime})T(e,b\times c)-T\bigl((b\times e)\times e^{\prime},b\times c\bigr).

Now invoke
Lemma

2.8
Item

9
to rewrite

\displaystyle T\bigl((b\times e)\times e^{\prime},b\times c\bigr)=T\bigl((b\times e)\times(b\times c),e^{\prime}\bigr)

\displaystyle=T(c,b\times e)T(b,e^{\prime})+T(c,b^{\sharp})T(e,e^{\prime})+T(e,b^{\sharp})T(c,e^{\prime})-T\bigl((c\times e)\times b^{\sharp},e^{\prime}\bigr)

and the result follows.

(Notice that we could not apply
Lemma

A.5
or
Corollary

A.6
since we would run into a circular argument.)

(15)

v=\xi
,
w=c\in J
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=2T(c,b^{\sharp})y

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=-2T(c,b^{\sharp})y.

(16)

v=\zeta
,
w=c\in J
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{2}[v,w]

\displaystyle=-T(c,b^{\sharp})y

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=-T(c,b^{\sharp})y.

The case
\ell=3
.

We have to show that

(*)_{3}

\delta_{3}[v,w]=[\delta_{3}v,w]+[\delta_{2}v,\delta_{1}w]+[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]+[v,\delta_{3}w]

for all
v,w\in L
.
Again, we first list the different cases we have to consider.

(1)

v=x
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
,

(2)

v=x
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
,

(3)

v=x
,
w=\xi
,

(4)

v=x
,
w=\zeta
,

(5)

v=x
,
w=c\in J
,

(6)

v=x
,
w=(0,0,0,\rho)_{+}
,

(7)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
,

(8)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
,

(9)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\xi
,

(10)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=\zeta
,

(11)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=c\in J
.

Of course, we have again applied
Remark

A.4
.
In particular, notice that for case
Item

6
, where
v=x\in L_{-2,-1}
and
w\in L_{1}
,

(
∗
)

3

is trivial when
w\in L_{1j}
with
j\neq 1
, which is why we can restrict to the case
w=(0,0,0,\rho)_{+}
.

(1)

v=x
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{3}[v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{3}v,w]

\displaystyle=-N(b)c^{\prime}-\rho N(b)(\xi-\zeta)

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=-T(b,c^{\prime})b^{\sharp}+D_{c^{\prime},b}(b^{\sharp})-\rho\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\sharp}}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=T(b,c^{\prime})b^{\sharp}-N(b)c^{\prime}+3\rho N(b)\zeta-\rho\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\sharp}}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{3}w]

\displaystyle=-\rho N(b)\xi,

where we have used
Definition

2.3
Items

3
and
6
in the computation of
[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]
.
Now these two identities also imply that
D_{c^{\prime},b}(b^{\sharp})=2N(b)c^{\prime}
.
Together with the fact that
\mathbf{d}_{b,b^{\sharp}}=N(b)(2\zeta-\xi)
by
Lemma

4.17
Item

1
,

(
∗
)

3

follows.

(2)

v=x
,
w=\mathbf{d}_{e,e^{\prime}}
. By
Corollary

A.6
, we can skip this case, as it will follow from
Item

5
. (Notice that the case
v=x
,
w=e^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
holds by
Remark

A.4
.)

(3)

v=x
,
w=\xi
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{3}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-2N(b),\ 0,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{3}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(N(b),\ 0,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-3N(b),\ 0,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{3}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(4)

v=x
,
w=\zeta
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{3}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-N(b),\ 0,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{3}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(-N(b),\ 0,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{3}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(5)

v=x
,
w=c\in J
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{3}[v,w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{3}v,w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ N(b)c,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ -b^{\sharp}\times(b\times c),\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=\bigl(0,\ T(c,b^{\sharp})b,\ 0,\ 0\bigr)_{+}

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{3}w]

\displaystyle=0.

The result now follows from
Lemma

2.8
Item

8
.

(6)

v=x
,
w=(0,0,0,\rho)_{+}
. Then

\displaystyle\delta_{3}[v,w]

\displaystyle=\rho N(b)y

\displaystyle[\delta_{3}v,w]

\displaystyle=\rho N(b)y

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}v,\delta_{1}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\delta_{1}v,\delta_{2}w]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[v,\delta_{3}w]

\displaystyle=0.

(7)

v=(\lambda,a,a^{\prime},\mu)_{-}
,
w=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{-}
. Again, we will invoke
Lemma

A.5
, writing
w=[z,x]
with
z=(\nu,c,c^{\prime},\rho)_{+}
.
Then

(
∗
)

3

for the given pair
(v,w)
will follow once we know it for each of the five pairs
(v,z)
,
(v,x)
,
(z,x)
,
(x,[v,z])
and
(z,[v,x])
.
For
(v,z)
, this is trivial by
Remark

A.4
. For
(v,x)
and
(z,x)
, this has been dealt with in the respective cases

and

above.
Since

, the case

follows from

,
3
,

and
